# M0 log

What merged, what waits on you, what changed in die-core's API, and where the budget stands ([m0-plan.md](m0-plan.md) §3). Newest first.

## 2026-11-08: the MVP run, 17 packages on `m0/integration`

**Waiting on you:**
1. **A day in it.** Exit test (a) needs you. Keep any terminal in the next tile for cargo and git, and use `die file:line` from it to reach a file. DIE's own terminal stays in M2, as planned. `EDITOR=die` doesn't work for `git commit`, as there's no `--wait`.
2. **Whether this reaches `main`.** Nothing in this run has.
3. **The hand checks** below.
4. **Any call below.** The ones I'd read first: 444 (project search and the finder are in core), 445 (die-vim is at 8,801, against 5,000 at the guard's start), 449 (`platform::macos`'s limit and the test that holds it), 467 (`<CR>` in a finder waits on the main thread for up to 2 s), 472 (`gf` isn't your Evil's `gf`), 478 (`*grep*` doesn't survive a restart) and 530 (a whole big file yanked doesn't survive one either).

**Checkpoint, after this entry was first written:** on b85389e, `ci --linux` passed in 169 s, and exit test (f) holds. `ci --full` ran every test with the ignored ones and the old property counts, and only one failed: `the_restart_loop_finds_equal_digests_and_logs_each_time`, whose first restart took 1,021.6 ms against 1 s while the whole suite ran. Its failure poisoned `two_launches_leave_one_instance` too. Alone, it passed. Exit test (b) therefore holds with no margin under load. `mutate` caught 57 of 58 on b85389e, missing M41, a property-test catch that took 6 to 15 s when caught before; a rerun caught all 58 in 155 s, so M41 is a rare catch rather than a regression (layout.rs is unchanged). Earlier rounds of `ci --full` found four problems, all in checks or the build rather than in what you'd use: the clipboard's exception catch kept 86 lines of assembly in a `.s` file that lint and the budget don't read, now in `catch.rs` (call 560); an app test read `session.json` between a write's two renames; `a_day.rs`'s read-only test failed as root on Linux; and die-vim was one line over after the merges.

### What you can do now, on `m0/integration`

[using-die.md](using-die.md) has every key. New in this run:

- **Open files from a terminal.** `die foo.rs:12:3` opens it in the running DIE, or starts one, and `C-o` comes back.
- **Find things.** `\\` fuzzy finds from home, `\p` from the project root, with results while you type at 500,000 paths. `\/` switches buffer.
- **Search the project.** `:grep` and `\r` stream into `*grep*`, `<CR>` opens a row, `:cn` and `:cp` step. `gf`, `gF`, `]f` and Visual `gf` open `path:line:col`.
- **More Vim.** Marks, the jump list and `g;`. `ge gE g_ | [[ ]] gd gJ & g& = ga`, `+ - _`, `g0 g^ g$`, `g* g#`, `[( ]) [{ ]}`, `io ao`, `ZZ ZQ`, `C-^`. `:2,5s` with addresses, `:bn :bp :bd :up :u :$ :wa :qa :wqa :xa :x`.
- **Insert state.** `C-o`, `C-p`, `C-n`, `C-x C-n`, `C-x C-p`, `C-t`, `C-d`, `C-a`, `C-@`, `C-e`, `C-y`, `C-r`, `C-v`, `C-q`, `C-u`, `<Del>`.
- **Prompts.** Cmd-V, `C-y`, `C-r` and `C-r C-w` paste into any of them.
- **Splits in the app.** Before this run the windowed app drew only the focused window of a split.
- **Restart keeps more.** `.`, `@@`, `;`, `,`, `n`, `N`, `gv`, `&`, marks and jumps come back.
- **Huge and odd files.** A 100 MB file opens in 42 ms and most keys take under a millisecond. `*` on a giant word no longer crashes it. Directories, FIFOs and `/dev/zero` are refused.
- **Idle.** About 0.55-0.6 OS wakes a second, down from 2.4.

**Oracle, from a fresh `DIE_ORACLE_BLESS=1` run on 30ed2f0:** 2,136 of 2,136 cases pass, with 107 deviations excluded and 0 pending. That's 2,135 from your Evil (2,045 batch, 90 tty), less the 107, plus 108 hand-written fixtures. It was 1,411 in the entry below. `DIE_ORACLE_BLESS` only rewrites `pending.txt`, so I also regenerated every fixture from batch Emacs into a scratch directory: all are byte for byte what's in the repo.

### What only you can check

These stand with the entry below's list, which no one has worked through.

- `die foo.rs` from another app, also with DIE minimized or hidden: does it come forward? `come_to_front` has never run, since every test stays in the background. If it doesn't, the U1 review's guess is that the client should also run `open` on the bundle.
- `cargo xtask app` for your real `~/Applications` and `~/.local/bin` link. V1 ran it only under `--home`.
- Launch from the Dock and type, real keys and input methods (V1 posted events to a background instance), restart while in front, and AeroSpace tiling and a live resize with splits.
- Press `p` after copying in another app: the pasteboard alert should appear only then. Copy a file in Finder and an image in a browser, then `p`: the register's text should paste. Yank and paste both ways on the real pasteboard (V6).
- The leader keys, `:grep`'s streaming and a real mouse in a drawn window. None of it has been tried in one.
- Colours on a P3 display: the Metal layer sets no colorspace, so reds and greens may look stronger than in Emacs (V4, from reading the code).
- `gd` in your GUI Emacs, where xref might ask for a TAGS file rather than fall through to the search the oracle saw (U4). `ga` on non-ASCII in graphical Emacs (U5).

### What wasn't verified

- **Nobody has typed into this.** V1 posted real key events to a release bundle before later packages landed. Everything since ran through the harness or through offscreen frames. U2, U3, U4, U5, U6, U7, U8, V2, V3 and W2 ran no window test.
- **No gate covers the tip.** A package that had a push refused re-merged and pushed on `cargo check`, as the rule allows, so no test has run on the merged whole. U1, U6, V2 and W3 say so for their own, and V6 re-ran only the budget and `tests/platform.rs`. W1's 6 window tests passed before two later merges. V5's `idle-60` and wake figures weren't re-run on its tip. W3 changed `crates/die/src/app.rs` and didn't run `xtask --test app`. V4 changed die-render and ran no window test.
- **Not run:** `ci --full`, `ci --linux`, `mutate`, `vim/compare.sh`, anything on Linux, and `restart-loop` with marks present.
- **The window rule, broken once and possibly twice.** U1's window tests ran up to three times. V6 ran `crates/die-render/tests/keys.rs` repeatedly and didn't check whether it put windows on the screen.
- **A flake, cause unknown.** `the_restart_loop_finds_equal_digests_and_logs_each_time` failed once in V1's full run, with `two_launches_leave_one_instance` failing on the poisoned mutex. It passed alone and on rerun.
- **Compared with Evil by reading, not running:** `n` after `gd`, because batch Emacs loses keys after `gd` and why isn't traced. Anything a click touches, and opening a file with `gf`, because the oracle can't click or set up files. `` `F `` for a killed file, and a jump into a killed buffer.
- **Measured once, under load:** the finder at 500,000 synthetic paths. A real `~` was never walked. No real-tree timing of `:grep` since the review's fixes. V3's before and after figures aren't from the same load.
- **The original SIGABRT in `tests/platform.rs` never reproduced** in 60 runs, so its cause isn't known. Any raise is now caught, which is all V6 can claim.

### The packages

**U1, `die file:line`.** Review: 4 minor, all fixed. `die a.txt:4` set no jump unless it crossed a buffer. A file could replace a lone terminal, a client could hang writing a long request to a stuck instance, and `restart` hung for good if `open.sock` had been removed. Probes of prompts, goal column and paths found nothing. API: die-core adds `FileTarget`, `Ctx::open_target`, `Ctx::set_jump_rule`, and `OpenRequest`, `OpenListener`, `send_open`, `Sent` and `OPEN_SOCKET` in `instance`. die-render adds `come_to_front`. die adds `app::is_client`, `app::client`, `Launch.background`. xtask adds `app::link_command`. Open: coming to the front, `cargo xtask app` for real, exit 1 from the real binary. `open -a DIE file` and Finder aren't built, by estimate over 30 lines. The restart test may not exercise the retry, which only die-core's socket test proves.

**U2, finders.** No review. A worktree's `.git` file was listed: fixed. At 500,000 synthetic paths and load about 18, a key and its frame took p50 125 us, p99 343 us on the main thread, and the new list arrived in p50 70 ms, p99 451 ms. API: die-core adds `walk`, `project_root`, `PathSource`, `Ctx::find_under`, `Ctx::prompt_settle`, and `Ctx::step` takes the finder's news. Open: at load 33 the ranker reached about 2 s, so `<CR>` can do nothing and need a second press. The frame was imitated, not a real GPU frame.

**U3, `:grep` and `gf`.** Review: 1 major, 5 minor, all fixed. 4,360 of 4,398 rows on DIE's own tree came from `.claude/worktrees` (major). Rows waited through long stretches with no match, `<CR>` ignored a window already on the file, a deleted file's row opened an empty buffer that `:w` could write back, an empty match made a row past the last line, and `]f [f ]F [F` and Visual `gf` were missing. API: die-core adds `grep`, `compile-goto-error`, `next-error`, `previous-error`, `Pattern::ignores_case`. Open: `.gitignore` isn't read, `:cc` and the rest aren't bound, and opening a file with `gf` has never been compared with Evil. `rows_show_while_the_search_goes_on_through_files_with_none` has about 700 ms of margin under load.

**U4, motions.** No review. `ge gE g_ | [[ ]] gd` with 150 oracle cases. `gd` hit a batch Emacs error, fixed in the oracle with `(require 'etags)`, and a full regeneration then matched. API: 7 commands, and `[<any>` and `]<any>` cancel an operator. Open: keys after `gd` are lost in batch Emacs. `[[ ]] gd` now set jumps through U7's list, which U4 left open.

**U5, edits.** No review. `gJ & g& = == ga`, with `:&`, `:&&`, the `&` flag and `~` in a replacement, in 31 oracle cases. `=` read a stale column and `u` after `&` went to the wrong place, both fixed before landing. `gJ` on the last line is a new deviation row. API: 5 commands and `Operator::Indent`, and `:s/x/~/` now means the last replacement. Open: `:sort :d :y :m :t` weren't built, at about 160 lines against a limit of about 60. V2 later built the numeric ranges. `&` and `=` on blocks, folds and Hide aren't checked.

**U6, insert state.** Review: 3 major, 8 minor, all fixed, and it built the builder's open keys. A macro with `C-o` ran its later text as normal commands (major). `C-p` after a click panicked and lost the session (major). `C-o` then a command that wasn't die-vim's stayed in normal state (major). Also `C-o` with `:b`, with `R`, with `gi`, and with `dd` on the last line, `C-a` with a count, and two multibyte failures in completion. API: die-core adds `Ctx::on_command_ended`. 16 commands in all, 246 oracle cases, 243 from Evil. `registers::read` now answers `:`, `/`, `%`. Open: `<Insert>`, a count on `C-x C-p`, no message when line completion finds nothing, non-ASCII symbols in a word for `C-p`. Four `C-o` probes that killed the batch run stay unprobed.

**U7, marks and the jump list.** Review: 1 major, 5 minor fixed, 1 made a deviation. A mark or jump into another buffer left the cursor off screen (major). `:s`, `&` and `g&` set no jump and `:s` no `[ ]`, a key naming no mark gave no error, a killed file's `A`-`Z` were lost, and `.` after `:%s` went to the first line changed. One product bug: `cargo fmt` under a clean buffer moved the cursor, and now it stays on its line when the line count holds. Missing small keys are bound, as listed above. **V3, hostile input.** No review. Fixed: out of memory on `*` on a 100 MB word. `ggVGrX` on 100 MB never finished, now 0.4 s. `gg<C-v>G$d` 300 s to 5 s, `:%s` 6.2 s to 0.66 s, `gg>G` 71 s to 1.5 s, a macro fan-out 74 s to 0.3 s, `w` in a 5 MB word 2-10 s to 0.2 s, lines of combining marks 15 s to 190 ms, and `:noh` on a highlighted 5 MB line 3.7 s to under 30 ms. Odd files, resizes and regex blow-ups held with no change. API: `Ctx::move_register`. A `Tx` of 4,096 changes or more runs in one pass and may leave `edits_since` returning None. `Pattern::new` refuses over 64 KiB. One defect fixed: a status line or a prompt's row stopped up to a cell short of the right edge. API: `Frames::build` extends a row's last run. Open: no syntax highlighting. ### Calls (veto any)

**Budget and scope**

441. die-vim's limit went from 5,790 to 7,000 ahead of the run (85b9410), for the Evil keys left out of M0, marks and the jump list.
442. session's limit went from 1,086 to 1,250 ahead of the run (85b9410), for opening files in the running instance and what a restart keeps.
443. A new module, project, got 600 lines ahead of the run (85b9410), for the walk, the finder and grep.
444. Project search and fuzzy file finding are in core, not an extension (scope §11).
445. die-vim's limit during the run: 7,800 (U6), 7,850 (W1), 8,050 (U8), 8,100 (V1), 8,150 (U3), 8,200 (V3), 8,500 (U6's review), 8,800 (V2).
446. session: 1,305 (U7), 1,385 (U8). project: 750 (U3). files: 760 (U1), 780 (V2).
447. text and undo: 1,285 (U7), 1,300 (U3), 1,420 (V3). The last is a real increase in core, for the one-pass path.
448. workspace, windows, pointer: 1,340 (V6), 1,365 (U7). ext, dispatch, rt: 1,510 (V1, for another package's overrun), 1,525 (V3). keys, keymaps, describe: 660 (V3), 675 (U6's review).
449. `platform::macos`: 1,160 (V6), 1,170 (U7), 1,180 (V5). Stub: 165 (U7). U7's two only cover another package's overrun. V1 moved the test that holds the macOS limit from 1,060 to 1,180 to match, and that test exists to hold the unsafe-review limit, so look at why the limit moved without it.
450. checks: 720 (V5).
451. Paused WIP history was squashed into one commit each (U2, U6, V5, W1), and commits carry no attribution lines, by your rule over the session's reminder.

**U1**

452. A run whose parent is launchd is an instance, any other from a bundle binary a client, unless it has `--handover`. A process orphaned to pid 1 before that check would become an instance, judged harmless.
453. `die` with no files from a terminal brings the instance forward or starts one.
454. If the focused window is a terminal, the file opens in the most recently used window that isn't, and that window takes focus. Tested only with a buffer set to Passthrough.
455. Several files open as `:e` would, so the last is on screen. `die a b` outside a bundle still shows the first and parses no positions.
456. Columns count characters. rg's `--column` counts bytes, so it can land off on non-ASCII lines.
457. `+LINE PATH` takes PATH literally, and takes `+LINE:COL` too. `foo:12:3` where `foo:12` exists means line 3 of that file.
458. A new instance gives a client's lock probe 20 ms, adding up to 20 ms to a second launch about to fail.
459. If `~/.local/bin` isn't on PATH, the printed line adds the bundle's `Contents/MacOS` to PATH. A regular file at `~/.local/bin/die` is left alone and reported.
460. Activation uses the deprecated `activateIgnoringOtherApps`, because the newer `activate` only takes what the active app yields.
461. A lone terminal is split with the new window on its left, not replaced. If it's too small to split, it's replaced.
462. `die file:line` sets a jump only with a line. A plain `die file` into another buffer gets it at the next key, as `:b` does.
463. The jump goes through a keymap rule on `Ctx`, so core knows nothing of Evil's jump list.
464. A file arriving during a prompt opens underneath, and the prompt stays up.
465. `open -a DIE file` and Finder aren't built, by both agents' estimate of over 30 lines.

**U2**

466. Two plain `std::thread`s rather than `Ctx::spawn`, as the ranker needs a long-lived loop and the walker a stop flag. Closing the prompt stops both.
467. `<CR>` and `<Tab>` wait for the ranker's answer for up to 2 s on the main thread, and do nothing if it doesn't come. Typing never waits.
468. Only the best 1,000 matches reach the main thread. The count shows the true total.
469. Non-UTF-8 paths, sockets and pipes aren't listed. A symlink to a file is. A symlink to a directory is neither followed nor listed.
470. `.cargo/registry` is skipped only under `.cargo`. The other skips match by name at any depth, so a project directory named `target` or `Library` is skipped.
471. If the app starts in `/` and no buffer has a directory, `find-file-project` uses home.

**U3**

472. `gf` does what your `gF` does, line numbers included, because the package asked. Your Evil's `gf` is plain `find-file-at-point`, which prompts on a name that isn't a file, and here it's an error. No fixture can show this, so no row. A directory counts as not a file.
473. `:grep` keeps focus in the source window. `*grep*` goes in a window showing it, else the most recently used other window that isn't a terminal, else a split below.
474. One row per line at its first match, so `:cn` steps by line.
475. `[DIR]` is the last word if it names a directory, so a pattern can't end in one, and `:grep mod tests` searches `tests/`.
476. A row shows at most 500 characters of its line.
477. Non-UTF-8 files with no early NUL are searched, with replacement characters.
478. `*grep*` isn't kept by the session, because its `<CR>` binding and run state can't be restored. V2 notes this may matter for restart-heavy use.
479. `*grep*` fills its window, without max width or centering.
480. Emacs's command names and end messages: `next-error`, `previous-error`, `compile-goto-error`, `Moved past last match`.
481. `.claude/worktrees` is skipped by name rather than by reading `.gitignore`.
482. Visual `gf` tries the buffer's directory and then the project root, where Evil tries only the first. No row, as the oracle can't set up files. After one that succeeds, the old buffer's selection ends, not compared.
483. `:line:col` keeps character columns, where Evil's `gF` uses `move-to-column`, so tabs land differently.

**U4**

484. New deviation rule, "sections start at a bracket, whatever is open" (evil-bug, 2 cases): `]]` and `[[` go by lines starting with `(`, `[` or `{` outside a `"` string. Evil's results differ only for nested or unbalanced column-0 brackets.

**After the run**

560. The clipboard's Objective-C `@try` is hand-written aarch64 assembly inside `catch.rs`, as clang writes it, since objc2's own needs a C compiler (D16). platform::macos's limit went from 1,180 to 1,270 to count it.
561. die-vim's limit went from 8,800 to 8,810 for the merges after V2, then to 8,876 for X1. files went from 780 to 833 for X1.
562. `C-g` shows file info as Vim's does. In your Evil it's `keyboard-quit`, which the oracle can't run.
563. `:w own.txt` naming the buffer's own file by a relative path is just `:w`. Evil refuses, since it compares the raw path.
564. An existing file is refused without `!` ("PATH exists; ! overwrites it"), where Evil asks y/n. That includes `:saveas` with no name on a buffer with a file, which DIE treats as `:w`.
565. A missing directory in `:w PATH` is an error and changes nothing, where Evil offers to make it.
566. `:saveas` or `:w PATH` onto a file another buffer visits refuses, and so does any `:w PATH` from a file that opened read-only.
567. `*grep*` and `*messages*` write a copy with `:w PATH` and keep their names.
568. `:saveas` with no name on a buffer with no file is an error, where Evil prompts for one.
569. `:ls` shows `*messages*` in the window, as Evil's buffer menu takes it. The format is DIE's: marks, the name padded to 20, then the path.
570. The restart loop's 1 s limit stays, though one restart took 1,021.6 ms under the full suite's load.
571. `M-x` opens the command palette in normal, insert, replace, visual and copy, bound by `die` beside Cmd-Q, as Emacs's global map has it under Evil. Not in a prompt, where Emacs refuses it, nor in operator-pending, where it ends the operator. Your first `M-x` in the app said `<A-x> is undefined`.
572. `:rebuild` runs `cargo xtask app --home H` from inside DIE, where `H/Applications/DIE.app` is the bundle running, and it refuses for a bundle anywhere else. It builds the checkout the running bundle came from: `cargo xtask app` writes its workspace in `Contents/Resources/checkout`, and `rebuild` refuses if that's missing or isn't a DIE checkout (`crates/die` and `xtask`) any more. At first it built the focused buffer's checkout, or else the last one built. Your first try, from `*scratch*` and a file in another project, was refused, and `rebuild` means DIE whatever you're editing. The output streams into `*rebuild*`, split below with the focus staying, and a window on its last line follows it. Exit 0 closes the window a rebuild opened, even on an earlier run, kills `*rebuild*` so the session doesn't carry it, and restarts. A failure leaves the cursor on the first `error` line. It finds rustup's cargo in `~/.cargo/bin`, since a DIE started from the Dock has launchd's PATH. It waits for cargo to exit, not for the pipe to close, so a process left behind doesn't hold it up. It has no way to stop a build. Killing `*rebuild*` mid-build loses the output, and the split it opened stays, showing another buffer. Your first review found the fallback to the compile-time path, `*rebuild*` coming back after a restart with your 80-column centering, and the split staying after a fail-then-fix loop. All three are fixed.
573. The cursor is hollow when DIE's window hasn't got the keyboard, as in Emacs, and in a window other than the focused one, or behind an open prompt. You saw it hollow "sometimes", with no pattern you'd noticed. The window only learnt the focus from winit's `Focused` events, so after each batch of events it now also asks AppKit whether the window is key. If the two disagree, it believes AppKit and says so in `*messages*`. It doesn't do this before the first `Focused` event, so a window that opened in the background keeps the solid cursor it always had. `show` checks the correction in a background window, and the checks budget went from 720 to 730 for it. Not fixed: `die file` brings DIE forward with `activateIgnoringOtherApps`, which recent macOS may refuse. Then the cursor is rightly hollow and your keys stay in the terminal.
574. `<S-Tab>` in a file prompt goes up a directory, as `vertico-directory-up` does: a name being typed goes, then the directory on the line, back to the `/` before it. `~/` goes to home's parent, and the root stays. You asked for it, since going back took held backspace. Your Emacs binds nothing for it, and `<S-Tab>` was plain `<Tab>` before. The prompt module's limit went from 1,300 to 1,310.
575. With a maximum width on the focused buffer, as `\z` gives it, the prompt, its list and the echo area use that window's text column, as the text above them does. In a split that's the focused window's column, and without a maximum width it's the whole frame, as before. You asked for it. Vertico in a full-width minibuffer doesn't do this in your Emacs, unless visual-fill-column reaches the minibuffer. A chosen row's highlight is that column too. The scene module's limit went from 950 to 965.
576. `:` lists commands as `M-x` does while the line is a command's name, after any range: the ex commands under their whole names and every other command, flex matched, the ones run last from `:` first, 20 rows. You asked for it. `<CR>` runs the line as typed until you move to a row, so `:w`, `:q`, `:wq` and `:x` are as they were, and a row runs with its range kept. `<Tab>` puts the chosen row, or the top one, in place of the name and adds no space. Once anything follows the name the list goes and nothing about arguments changes. `/` and `?` don't change.
577. Core's part is one builder, `PromptSpec::matches_part`: the prompt's owner names the byte range of the line its candidates match, or None for no list. With one, no row is chosen until you move, as on a directory with `descends`, and a row goes in place of the range, the rest of the line kept. With None the rows go, to come back as tall as they were, and the keys are a list-less prompt's, so `<Up>` and `C-p` are history again. The line keeps its column when the list goes, padded where the count was, so it doesn't jump as you type a space. The range is asked for on each change and guarded like `candidates_from`. The prompt module's limit went from 1,310 to 1,331.
578. A trade-off: Vim's `:` then `<Up>` recalls the last line. Here the list is showing, so `<Up>` stays on the line and the most recent command is the top row. `M-p` recalls the line. The same goes for `:vim-f<Up>`, which recalled a matching line and now moves in the list. `<Up>` matching what's typed works once there's an argument, as `:s/f<Up>`. core_review's line-keys test now asks that.
579. The list is Evil's set, the ex commands and every command, as `evil-ex-completion-table` has it with `evil-ex-complete-emacs-commands` set to `t`, sorted together as the palette sorts. Your init leaves it at `in-turn`, which offers Emacs commands only when no ex command matches. Evil also lists `write!` and the other bang forms, and DIE doesn't: `!` ends the name, so `:w!` has no list.
580. The names come from the tables the dispatcher reads: `ex.rs`'s new `EX`, `window.rs`'s, and `:s`'s name in substitute.rs. `ex.rs`'s `match` became that table, with Vim's rule for short forms, so `:wri`, `:bdel`, `:nohls`, `:quita` and `:sub/a/b/` now work where only the spellings listed did. `:up!` now forces the write as `:w!` does, since Evil's `evil-update` calls `evil-write`, which reads the same `!` (from its source, not run), and `:'<,'>write` is refused as `:'<,'>w` is. A command read before the tables, as `:s` is, has to add its name in `ex_names`. die-vim's limit went from 8,876 to 8,912.
581. A range is read loosely for the list, so `:'a,` keeps it while you type the second mark. The history's lines count as their commands' whole names for "run last first", so `:w` puts `write` on top.
582. a_day's "add a match arm" now adds a row to that table, as the arm it copied is gone. `:wqa` and `:xa` call `quit_app` in their rows, so its rename task still finds every `quit_app` before another `quit`.

**Macros: `recording @a`, `:norm`, `:g` and `:reg`**

583. While `q` records, the bottom row's right end says `recording @a`, with the name as typed (`recording @A` after `qA`), until `q` stops it, as Vim shows it. Your Evil only flashes "Defining kbd macro..." and your mode line shows only the directory, so it's an addition. It's on the bottom row, as Vim's is, not the status line: a message goes beside it, stopping a column short, and an open prompt covers it, as Vim's command line does. die-core adds `Ctx::set_note`, a string the bottom row keeps until it's set again, not logged.
584. `:norm[al] keys` is `evil-ex-normal`: the range's lines are markers first, each line's keys start at its column 0, and after each line it's back to normal state as `evil-force-normal-state` leaves it. A session ends without being what `.` repeats, and a selection, an operator, a `q` waiting for its register and a count go. A prompt the keys left open gets a `C-g`, as your Evil's minibuffer gives up when the keys run out. *Wrong: call 632 answers it, as your Evil does.* The cursor stays where the last line left it, and one `u` undoes it all. Each character is a key, so Esc and Enter go in with `C-v`, and spaces at the end count, as in your Evil.
585. A line whose keys fail ends there and the next starts, with no error shown, as your Evil's `ignore-errors`. die-core adds `Ctx::hush_key_errors`: while it's on, a failing key, an unbound one or a prompt's answer ends what was fed and says nothing. A typed `C-g` stops the whole `:norm`: die-core adds `Ctx::take_quit`, so the `on_fed_dropped` call tells a quit from a failure.
586. `:norm` feeds each line's keys and waits for them, so it runs between keys, not inside one command as Evil's `execute-kbd-macro` does: the window draws and `C-g` gets in. A line whose last key waits for another, as in `:norm f`, ends when the keys stop: an Escape follows it.
587. After `:norm`, `.` is the last change its keys made, as your Evil records them with its command hooks, and a macro they ran counts as `@x`: `:%norm @a` then `.` replays `@a`, and `:norm @ax` then `.` is `x`. An insert left open isn't recorded, so `:%norm Ay` leaves `.` as it was, as in your Evil.
588. `:norm!` is `:norm`, as Vim's `!` only leaves out mappings. Your Evil takes no bang there and types the `!` as a key, its filter operator, so `:%norm! x` sent your first line through a shell command `x`. No oracle case, since that runs a shell.
589. `:g[lobal]`, `:g!` and `:v[global]` are `evil-ex-global`: the lines are markers first, the command runs from where each line's match starts, and the first error ends it. Any delimiter, `\` keeps it in the pattern, an empty pattern is the last search, and the pattern becomes `n`'s, keeping its direction. `:v!` is `:g`, and the default range is the file. A match has to lie within its line, so `:g/a\nb/` matches nothing, as in your Evil. *Call 637: within its line, bounded at its end.* Patterns are Rust's (D4), as for `/` and `:s`. With no line to run on, the cursor goes to the range's end, as your Evil leaves it, but not onto the empty line after a final newline (row "the last line is the last line").
590. Your Evil's `:g` has no default command: `:g/a` says "No command given", and `:g/a/` and `:g/a/3` fail with a Lisp error. Here all three say "No command given". Vim's default is `:p`, which DIE doesn't have, and neither has `:d`, so `:g/pat/d` isn't there.
591. `:g/pat/norm .` repeats on every matching line, as in Vim and as `:%norm .` does in your Evil, whose `:g` leaves `.` with nothing after its first line. New evil-bug row "the . in :g's :norm repeats on every line", with `macros/g-norm-dot` hand-written.
592. A range given backward, as `:3,1norm x`, does nothing, as `:s`'s does here. Your Evil asks to swap it, which hangs the oracle. Ranges are `:s`'s otherwise: `%`, `'<,'>`, `N,M`, `.`, `$`, `'x`, `+n`, `-n`.
593. `:norm` and `:g` stop at 100 inside each other, as `:norm @a` with `a` holding `:norm @a` would go on for good, where your Evil's runs out of Lisp nesting. The count of macros that can run without a typed key starts again on each line, so a line's macro can call itself as often as a typed one.
594. By reading, not tested: a failure in a `:norm` that a macro or another `:norm`'s keys ran drops everything fed, so the outer macro or line stops too, where your Evil's `ignore-errors` lets it go on. Fed keys are one queue in die-core. *Call 633 fixed it.*
595. `:reg[isters]` and `:di[splay]` open `*registers*` below, or in the window already showing it, with the focus there, as `evil-show-registers` opens its list: `"`, `*`, `+`, `%`, `#`, `/`, `:`, `.`, `-`, `1`-`9` and `=` always, then any other register that holds something, all in the order of their names. A row is the name padded to 10, a space, then the text with a newline as `^J`, Evil's columns. The header is the buffer's first line, where Evil's is a header line. A recording is in DIE's notation, `A!<Esc>j`, where your Evil writes `A ! <escape> j`. `:reg ab` lists those two. `+` reads the clipboard, as Evil's does, so macOS's paste alert may come up then.
596. `q` in `*registers*` records a macro, as anywhere in DIE, where your Evil's list closes on `q`. `:q` closes it. Your Evil's `switch-to-buffer-other-window` takes another window if there is one, and this splits instead.
597. Budget: die-vim from 8,912 to 9,340, for about 430 lines. die-core's keys, keymaps, describe from 675 to 680, and ext, dispatch, rt from 1,525 to 1,535, for `take_quit` and `hush_key_errors`. `set_note` fits faces and scene's limit.
598. 130 oracle cases in `cases/macros.json`, now 164 there. 10 harness tests in `tests/macros.rs` for what the oracle can't hold: the bottom row, `*registers*`, `:norm!`, a typed `C-g`, a search inside `:norm` (batch Emacs dies on one), keys left waiting, 5,000 lines, a `:norm` that starts itself, and using-die.md's examples. Not matched with your Evil: jumps after `:norm` and `:g` (after `G:1,2norm x` your Evil's ``` `` ``` stays and DIE's goes back where `G` began, not looked into), `q` inside `:norm` (DIE records only typed keys, so `:norm qbxq` leaves `b` empty), and `u` inside `:norm` (undo is Vim's, row "undo is Vim's u and C-r").
599. `:`'s list has `normal`, `global`, `vglobal`, `registers` and `display`: `:reg` and `:di` are rows of `EX`, and `each.rs` gives its three names to `ex_names`, as a command read before the table does.

**The Buffer List**

600. `\}` and `\?` open the Buffer List, `buffer-menu`, as your init.el binds them, with evil-collection's keys for it in normal state. You asked for it. Its behaviour was read from Emacs 30.2's buff-menu.el and tabulated-list.el and checked against batch Emacs with your Evil and evil-collection loaded read-only: the listing, and each key on the same buffers. `crates/die-core/tests/menu.rs` holds what it showed, `:ls` in die-vim's `ex.rs` tests and `\}` in die-dev's `leader.rs`.
601. It's in die-core, a module of its own (`menu.rs`), beside `switch-buffer` and `kill-buffer` and as `*grep*` is. Scope §11 listed buffer-menu among what stays in Emacs until you build it, and guard 1 keeps applications out of core. This one is Emacs's own, not a package, and manages core's buffers. By guard 5, an extension would have needed buffer-local keys and burying, which only core has, as new primitives for one user. die-vim was the other place: the keys are evil-collection's, but the menu is Emacs's, and die-vim is 9,317 lines against scope's 5,000.
602. The keys are buffer-local in normal state, so they beat die-vim's as evil-collection's beat Evil's, and every other key is die-vim's. `1` and `2` can't start a count there, as in your Emacs. Two more keys your Emacs has there come with them: evil-collection's tabulated-list module gives `q` and `S`, though the buff-menu module doesn't list them.
603. DIE has no header lines, so the titles are the text's first line, as with Emacs's `Buffer-menu-use-header-line` off, in a new `header-line` face that mono-dark styles as your header line, without its box and only as wide as the titles. The cursor starts on the first row, as yours does, but `gg` goes to the titles, where the menu's keys say "No buffer on this line". With the titles in the text, `<Del>` on the second row unmarks the first, as Emacs's does then. Under your header line it can't.
604. The Mode column says a buffer's first mode, the file-type mode of contract §8, or a surface's kind, or else Fundamental. DIE has no major modes yet, so it's Fundamental everywhere, and for `*messages*`, `*grep*`, `*help*` and `*rebuild*` too, where Emacs says Messages, Grep, Help and Compilation. Size counts characters, as `buffer-size` does. File has `~` for your home, as `abbreviate-file-name` does. Names are bold, in a new `buffer-menu-buffer` face.
605. Every buffer is listed but the menu and names starting with a space without a file, as Emacs lists its own. That includes `*messages*`, `*grep*`, `*help*` and `*rebuild*`. `*messages*` never shows `*`, since DIE never counts it as modified, where your Emacs's does. The order is DIE's buffer list, which counts a buffer made but never shown as used when it was made. Emacs puts such a buffer last.
606. Widths are Emacs's: the name column is as wide as the longest name, at least 19 and at most the window's text width over 4.2, which is 19 at your 80 columns. A longer name runs into Size's room, as far as the widest size in its row and the rows on either side leaves, and is cut with `…` past that. Mode is cut at 16. Widths count characters, not cells, so a name with wide characters pushes its row's later columns right.
607. `x` asks for a modified buffer with a file, as `:kill-buffer` does here, `(yes or no)`, which it now shares. Emacs 30 asks `(yes/no/save and then kill)`, and DIE's `:kill-buffer` didn't before either. A no keeps that row's `D` and goes on, and a cancel stops there, as Emacs's quit does. An `S` on a buffer with no file and unsaved text says `Error saving NAME` and keeps its `S`, where Emacs asks for a file name.
608. `gO` and `2` pick the other window as `display-buffer` does: one already showing the buffer, else a split of this one when it's alone or 160 columns wide (`split-width-threshold`, and to the right if so), else the window used longest ago. `*grep*` (call 473) takes the window used last, and splits below only with no other. The menu follows Emacs instead.
609. `q` and `ZZ` show what the window showed before the menu, which the menu remembers, as DIE keeps no history per window, or else `other-buffer`'s. `X`, `1`, `2`, `gv` and `q` put a buffer last as `bury-buffer` does: it counts as never used, last with `*messages*`. Among buried buffers the order is the order they were made, where Emacs puts the latest last.
610. `*Buffer List*` isn't kept by the session, as `*grep*` isn't (call 478): its keys and its rows' buffers wouldn't come back with it.
611. `:ls` and `:buffers` open the menu, and `:files` opens it with only buffers with files, as your Evil's are `buffer-menu` and `(buffer-menu 1)`. This replaces call 569's listing in `*messages*`, and die-vim has 22 fewer lines.
612. Left out: `gV` (DIE has no View mode), `t` (tags tables, so `t` is Evil's motion there), `M-s a C-s`, `M-s a C-M-s` and `M-s a C-o` (isearch and occur across the marked buffers), `M-DEL` (unmark one kind, a key of buff-menu's that Evil doesn't shadow), a middle click, and tabulated-list's `gl gh { }` for columns, about 30 lines. `I`, Emacs's internal buffers, stays Evil's `I`, as in your Emacs.
613. The message on opening lists the menu's keys, `Commands: d, s, x, u; go, gO, 1, 2, m, gv; ~, %; q to quit`. Emacs's lists its own (`f`, `o`, `v`, `C-h m`), which aren't the keys under Evil. Command names are Emacs's in DIE's lower case: `buffer-menu-delete` for `Buffer-menu-delete`, and `buffer-menu-refresh`, `buffer-menu-sort` and `buffer-menu-quit` for `revert-buffer`, `tabulated-list-sort` and `quit-window`.
614. The new module's limit is its size, 494 lines. Core commands went from 1,331 to 1,339 for the shared kill question. faces gained two lines for the two faces, inside its limit. die-core is 12,403 of 14,000.

**`:`'s list, after its review**

615. `:` keeps your Evil's history keys beside its list. From the line, `<Up>` (matching what's typed), `C-p` and `<PageUp>` go back in the history, as `evil-command-line-map` has them, so `:<Up>`, `:s<Up>` and `:<C-p>` bring back lines again. `<Down>`, `C-n` and `<PageDown>` go into the list, and `<Up>` from its first row goes back to the line. Once you're in the history, all six walk it until a key changes the line, so `C-p` `C-n` go back and forth past a line that's only a name. Before, such a line brought its list back and the next `C-n` chose a row: from `:e x`, `<C-p><C-n><CR>` past a `:w` ran `:wq`. This replaces call 578's trade-off. `M-x` has a row chosen from the start, and `:` starts on the line, so `<Up>` there takes nothing from the list. Four oracle cases in `cases/ex.json`. It's die-core's prompt, for a part prompt only.
616. The top row is the command the name runs, by Vim's short forms: `quit` for `:q`, `write` for `:w`, `substitute` for `:s`, `display` for `:di`, `bNext` for `:bN`. The names it starts come next, then the other matches, each group in flex's order. Flex alone put the shortest name first, so `:q<Tab>` gave `wq` and `:q<Down><CR>` ran `:wq`. die-core adds `PromptSpec::tiers`, a tier for each candidate given the text, run guarded. Once something's typed the history doesn't order the rows, as with `M-x`, so call 581's ordering holds only on the empty line: after `:noh` three times, `:no` still lists `normal` before `nohlsearch`. `:no` runs neither (Vim's is `:noremap`).
617. `:s` and `:substitute` alone, after any range, repeat the last substitution as `:&` does, which is how your Evil's `evil-ex-get-substitute-info` reads an empty argument. That's the top row after any `:s`, and choosing it said "no command named substitute". Two oracle cases. Flags with no pattern, as `:s g`, aren't built.
618. `:up!` is `:up` again. Call 580 was wrong: `evil-update` takes no bang, so your Evil reads `:up!` as `:up` with the argument `!`, and `evil-write` runs that as an empty shell command and writes nothing (batch Emacs with your Evil). DIE writes as `:up` does, refusing over a file changed on disk, as it did before 580. `:'<,'>up` is now refused as `:'<,'>w` is, since your Evil's writes only those lines: `:2,3up` asked to overwrite the file with them.
619. A range before a command that takes none is ignored, as `%` and `'<,'>` were. The list makes one when you choose a row after `:2,5no`, and `:2,5nohlsearch` said "no command named 2,5nohlsearch". A line number alone still goes to that line, `:3sp` keeps its count, and a range before `:w`, `:wq` or `:up` is refused, as `:2,5w isn't built`.
620. When the list goes, its rows go too, and the line moves to the bottom row. Call 577 said the line didn't move: only its column stays. Your vertico's minibuffer grows and doesn't shrink (`resize-mini-windows` is `grow-only`), but there the windows above shrink and keep their cursors in view. DIE's list covers the windows instead, so 20 empty rows kept would cover the text a `:s/` pattern is written for. While the list shows, it covers the cursor's line when that's in the bottom 21 rows, as `M-x`'s does.
621. Left as they were: `<S-Tab>` on `:` puts the top row on the line as `<Tab>` does, as on every list but the file prompt's, and your Evil's `:` binds no `<S-Tab>`. Not matched with your Evil, before this or since: its history keys say "No later matching history item" at either end, where DIE's say nothing, and its `<Down>` from the newest matching line stays there, where DIE's brings back what you typed. No oracle case holds those.
622. Budget: die-core's prompt module from 1,339 to 1,370, for the history keys and `tiers`. die-vim stays inside 9,340.

**The Buffer List, after its review**

623. `gv` sizes its windows as `Buffer-menu-select` does: each window but the last has the frame's rows over how many windows there are, status line included, and the last has the rest, so 24 rows go 4 4 4 4 8 for four others, as batch Emacs's did. Before, each split halved the last window, and a fourth other buffer was too small to split, after the marks had gone. Emacs goes down to windows of a mode line alone, but a window here has a row of text at least, so when the frame can't hold that, `gv` says "That window is too small to split" and changes nothing, marks included. Emacs takes the marks off first.
624. After a count's first digit, `1` and `2` are digits of it, as your Emacs's `universal-argument-map` has the digits over the Buffer List's keys once one is typed: `31d` marks 31 rows and `C-u 1 d` one, as in batch Emacs. With a count, the menu's command runs what the key does without the buffer-local keys, which is die-vim's digit. Call 602's "`1` and `2` can't start a count" holds for the first digit only.
625. Evil's insert and edit keys do nothing in the menu: `i I a A c C D r O p P J = < >` and `<Insert>`, bound to a new core command, `ignore`. Your evil-collection remaps those Evil commands to `ignore` in `special-mode-map`, which the Buffer List inherits (batch Emacs's bindings there). Keys it doesn't remap, as `R`, `gi`, `gJ` and `gu`, stay Evil's, as there. This corrects call 612: `I` isn't Evil's there, your Emacs ignores it. `*grep*`, `*help*` and `*messages*` are the same in your Emacs and aren't yet here.
626. `.` doesn't repeat the menu's keys. Your Evil repeats any command that isn't Evil's by its keys, while it stays in the buffer, so `d .` marks two rows there. Here only die-vim's own commands say they're changes, and doing it for core's would be a die-vim change for every core key, in the module the macro work is changing now. It's under "What isn't there yet".
627. A line break in a buffer name or a path shows as Emacs describes the character, `^J` for a newline, so each row stays one line. The other characters DIE breaks lines at show as `^K`, `^L` and `^M`, or by code, as `\u2028`. A name with a newline printed over two lines, and `d` on the second marked the next buffer, so `x` killed that one, without asking. Emacs prints the newline, and its rows find their buffers by text property, which DIE's rows don't have.
628. Listing again (`gr`, `T`, `o`, or opening the menu) puts other windows on the menu at the first row, column 0, where Emacs's window points go: the start of the text, under the header line. They went to the titles. The window it ran in keeps its row, as before.
629. `o` on a row whose name is cut finds Size where the cut name ends, so its size's digits sort by Size. It measured the whole name, so they sorted by Buffer.
630. Tests: `gv` with four and five others against batch Emacs's heights, and in a frame too small; `31d` and `C-u 1 d` in die-vim's `:ls` test; the silenced keys; a name with a newline; a second window on a listing again; `o` on a cut name. A comment that said 38 wide now says 26, the longest name. die-vim's test of `:no`'s list checked that `undo` came first after the names `no` starts, and `ignore` now matches before it, so it checks what call 616 says: those names first, then the other matches.
631. Budget: the buffer menu from 494 to 536, for the above. die-core is 12,476 of 14,000.

**Macros, after their review**

632. A prompt a `:norm` line's keys leave open is answered with its line as it stands, as your Evil's minibuffer exits when the keyboard macro runs out: `:%norm :s/./X/` runs the `:s` on every line, and one `u` undoes it. Call 584 said it was cancelled, and the case it rested on, `:norm :<CR>x`, couldn't tell the two apart. Before, a prompt left open with text in it waited for you, a line at a time, and `C-g` and `u` reached only one line. If the answer leaves a prompt open, a `C-g` follows, and if that leaves one open the run stops with an error. Vim gives up on such a line (`vim -es` leaves the text alone). This follows your Evil.
633. die-core adds `Ctx::feed_keys_apart`, `on_fed_run_out` and `keys_pending` (contract §6). Keys fed apart are kept apart from the fed keys behind them: a failure among them, or among keys a macro in them feeds, drops only those, and once they've all run, `on_fed_run_out` calls run before the next key, fed or typed. `:norm` feeds each line's keys so. A line that fails inside a macro, or inside another `:norm`'s line, now leaves the rest of that macro or line to run, as your Evil's `ignore-errors` does: `@q` with `q` holding `:%norm fbx<CR>ggA!<Esc>` ends with its `ggA!`, where it stopped silently at the first failing line. A macro that a line runs and that fails still ends the line, as in your Evil.
634. A line now ends when die-core says its keys have run out, not when the loop next goes idle, so what they left waiting is ended before anything else reaches it. A key typed while `:norm` ran, or the rest of a macro that ran the `:norm`, used to go into a prompt the line left open, or be the character `:norm f` waited for. die-vim's idle check and its check after every command are gone.
635. A `:g` or `:norm` that a `:norm` line's keys start runs as far as its first line's keys at once, inside the key that started it, so an error in it is that key's: it ends the line quietly, as your Evil's `ignore-errors` does. A `:g` there used to run after the key, so its error was shown and the line went on. A `:g` whose command isn't known leaves the cursor where it was, as your Evil reads the command before it moves. DIE went to the first match.
636. `@:` with a count runs the ex line again once the last run is over, as your Evil runs them in turn, so `:g/a/norm jdd` then `u2@:` works on the text the first run left. All the runs used to start at once, on the same lines, newest first. A `:norm` or `:g` started outside a macro or `.` is a step of its own for `u`, as `evil-with-single-undo` begins one, so `2@:` then `u` undoes the second. Repeated `:s` stays one step, and inside a macro its one step holds them all, both as in your Evil.
637. `:g` looks in each line for a match that ends by the line's end, as your Evil's `re-search-forward` bounded at `line-end-position` does, through die-core's new `TextBuf::first_match_in`. A match that starts on the line above and runs into this one no longer hides this line's own, and `a` then any number of newlines matches a line's `a` where it took the newline too and so didn't count. The oracle cases type the newline as `C-v C-j`.
638. A count before `:` starts the line with as many lines from the cursor's, `.,.+2` for 3 and `.` for 1, as your Evil's `evil-ex` does. It was dropped without a word, so `3:norm @q` ran on one line. That was so before the macro work too.
639. `:s` copies only its lines and the character after them to search, not the whole text, which any change before it had made stale. `:g/./s/;/!/` on 20,000 lines of 80 columns went from 1.3 s to 0.6 s. What's left grows with the square of the lines matched: `:g` and `:norm` keep a marker per line and every change moves all of them, as Emacs's markers do. On 40,000 lines that's about 1.8 s for `:g/./s/;/!/` and 3.9 s for `:g/./norm @a`. Not fixed: a `:g` whose command isn't `:norm` runs inside one key, so the window doesn't draw and `C-g` can't stop it. That needs die-core to tell an extension a typed `C-g` is waiting. Your Evil's `:g` runs in one command too, though Emacs's `C-g` can quit it.
640. Budget: die-vim from 9,340 to 9,385, for about 45 lines. die-core's keys, keymaps, describe from 680 to 710, and ext, dispatch, rt from 1,535 to 1,545, for keys fed apart. text and undo from 1,420 to 1,425, for `first_match_in` and `:s`'s copy.
641. Tests: 32 oracle cases in `cases/macros.json`, now 196 there, of which 23 fail on the code before this. The 164 already there came back byte for byte. 3 tests in die-core's `fed_keys.rs` for keys fed apart. In `tests/macros.rs`: a typed `C-g` after a line has run, and keys typed while `:norm` runs. `using_dies_examples_do_what_it_says` now runs all six of using-die.md's examples, and the `:norm` that starts itself checks that a failure is shown again after it. `:%norm :<CR>` on three lines hangs batch Emacs, so `:norm :<CR>x` is still the only case with an empty prompt left open.
642. Stopping a recording says so, as you asked: `Recorded @a`, `Added to @a` after `qA`, `Recorded nothing, so @a is empty` for Vim's `qaq`, and `Added nothing to @a`. Your Emacs's `end-kbd-macro` says "Keyboard macro defined", which doesn't name the register, so DIE names it. One harness test, `recording_shows_on_the_bottom_row_until_q_stops_it`, covers all four. die-vim's limit goes from 9,385 to 9,390 for it.
643. With a list showing, the status line of each window under its top row is drawn on the row above the prompt, from edge to edge, as your Emacs has the mode line above the minibuffer when it shrinks the windows. You asked for it from a screenshot of your M-x. The list is still drawn over the windows, so that row's text is covered too. With a maximum width, the prompt, its list and the echo area now start at the margin, where the line numbers start, and end where the text does, as your Emacs's minibuffer has them. This replaces call 575's text column. Without line numbers nothing moves. die-core's faces and scene limit goes from 965 to 975 for it. Test: `a_list_has_the_status_line_above_it_and_its_content_inside_the_margins`. Also, `an_empty_register_fails_unless_recording` now expects `Recorded @b`: call 642 broke it, and `ci --full` missed it because cargo stops at the first failing test binary, which was the window timing test.
644. Linux gets terminals from the same engine: DIE's CI builds the Linux libghostty-vt archive with a pinned Zig from Ghostty's signed source (platforms.md §4.3 path B). You chose it: "if it can be solved with zig in CI only then thats fine. We dont need to setup the CI now we can do that later." Embedding another terminal's window was ruled out again: macOS and Wayland can't, and Vim motions over the output need the cells. alacritty_terminal everywhere would about double die-term. Community prebuilt archives aren't signed by Ghostty either. The job waits until before M2. The MVP you use locally comes first. Scope D10, D16, D17 and S7 say so now.

**`die --wait`, and files from Finder**

645. `die --wait file...` (`-w`) waits as `emacsclient` does without `-n`, so `EDITOR="die --wait"` serves `git commit` and `git rebase -i`. Done is what your Evil's `evil-quit` calls `server-edit` for, `:wq`, `:x`, `ZZ`, `:q`, `ZQ` and `C-w q`, and killing the buffer, as `server-kill-buffer` does. One difference: your Evil runs `server-edit` only once `evil-window-delete` fails, in the last window, so in a split `:wq` closes the window and the client goes on waiting. DIE finishes the file in any window, as asked, so a split can't leave git waiting. `C-w c` and `:close` still only close.
646. What done does is `server-buffer-done`'s and `server-switch-buffer`'s. A buffer made for the client is killed, unsaved text and all, and its windows go back to what they showed. DIE's kill rule picks that, the buffer used last that no window shows, where Emacs uses each window's own previous buffer. A buffer that was open first stays on screen and goes last in the buffer list, with Emacs's "No server buffers remain to edit". Then the next file a client waits on comes up: `die --wait a b` shows `b`, as `emacsclient` does, and done with it shows `a`.
647. `:q` on a waited file with unsaved changes refuses with `:q`'s own message, where your Emacs's `server-done` asks "Save file ...?". It's how DIE's `:q` and `:bd` already answer where Emacs asks. `:q!` drops them and finishes, as `server-buffer-done` does.
648. The exit status is emacsclient's, which I measured: an `emacs -Q --daemon` under a scratch HOME, and `emacsclient` waiting on a file, returned 0 after `kill-emacs`, after SIGKILL, after `server-edit` and after `kill-buffer`, and 1 only after `server-edit-abort`. So `die --wait` returns 0 when you're done and 0 when DIE quits, restarts or crashes first, and in that case says on stderr that DIE went. A restart doesn't hand waiting clients over to the new instance: they return, and the files stay open in it.
649. With no DIE running, `die --wait` starts one with no files, through `open -n`, and sends them once it listens, waiting up to 10 s, so they count as made for the client. With no file named it exits 2. A file that won't open is logged, and with nothing open the client returns 0 at once, where `emacsclient` returns 1.
650. Quitting DIE with clients waiting (`:qa`, Cmd-Q) doesn't ask, where Emacs's `server-kill-emacs-query-function` asks whether to exit anyway. They return 0. A client that has gone, as one stopped with Ctrl-C, isn't noticed: Emacs's sentinel deletes it and kills its unchanged new buffers, and in DIE its file waits on until you finish it. No `server-edit-abort`. All three are on using-die.md's list.
651. The socket's request says ` wait` after `front` or `background`, in the same field, so an instance without this refuses it rather than open a file named `wait`. The listener's handler gets the request with its connection (`Asked`) and answers once it's carried out. For one that waits the answer goes before the client is registered, so `done` can't come first. A buffer already open on the file with no unsaved text is read again first, as `server-visit-files` reverts one, since the poll would wait out 200 ms of settling after git's write.
652. Files from Finder, the Dock and `open -a` come as an Apple event, and winit 0.30.13 has no event for it and its app delegate no `application:openURLs:`. So `platform::macos` adds that method to winit's delegate class with `class_addMethod`, before the loop runs, as the Character Viewer's hook replaces methods on winit's view. Nothing is forked. The method hands the paths to a thread that sends them over `open.sock` to the lock's holder, as `die --background file` would, which is this instance once it listens. A launch to open a file therefore waits for its own first frame, up to 10 s, and a restart's two instances can't split the files between them. A request from the background now takes the keyboard if and when DIE is the active app, as Finder and the Dock make it, and never activates DIE, so `open -g` takes no focus.
653. Info.plist declares one document type, `public.data`, as an Editor at the `Alternate` rank. Finder lists DIE in Open With for any file, and the Dock takes any file dropped on it, but DIE opens nothing by default until you choose it. Folders aren't `public.data`, and DIE refuses them. The app tests now take each test bundle out of LaunchServices when they end, so Open With doesn't go on offering deleted copies.
654. The test of `open -a` builds its bundle with an id of its own, `dev.die.DIE.test.open`, since LaunchServices hands a file to any running app with the id, and other packages' tests run `dev.die.DIE.test`. It doesn't use `cargo xtask app --home`: that bundle has your id, `dev.die.DIE`, so `open -a` could hand the file to your running DIE.
655. Budget: die-core gets a module, clients, at 83 of 90. session from 1,385 to 1,425, for the request's `wait` and the connection kept. die-vim from 9,390 to 9,400, for `:q` on a waited file. `platform::macos` from 1,270 to 1,300, and the test that holds it with it: the hook is two `unsafe` blocks, a transmute and `class_addMethod`, and isn't a thin wrapper, so it's the thin-wrapper question again. The platform stub from 165 to 170, for `on_open_files`.
656. API: die-core adds `Ctx::open_for_client`, `is_client_buffer` and `client_done`, and in `instance` `Asked`, `Client`, `Waiting` and `OpenRequest::wait`. `OpenListener::start` takes `FnMut(Asked)`, and `Sent::Delivered` holds the connection a waiting request goes on with, so `Sent` is no longer `Copy` or `PartialEq`. die-render adds `window::Options::on_open`, `window::OnOpen` and `driver::on_open_files`. die adds `app::carry_out` and `Launch::wait`, and `app::client` takes the `Launch`.
657. Tests: 9 in die-vim's `tests/wait.rs` for each way to finish, the refusal, a split, a file open before, kills, two files, two clients, a file that won't open and the reload. 3 in die-core's `tests/instance.rs` for waiting, a client whose instance goes, and clients side by side. 2 in die's `tests/wait.rs` from the socket through `carry_out` to `:wq`, and an editor that goes. App tests, with a bundle of their own and `open -g`: `die --wait` starting an instance and returning when it's killed, a restart returning two waiting clients, and `open -g -a` starting an instance with a file and then sending more to it with no focus taken, which fails with the hook turned off. Not tried: a real Finder double-click or Dock drop, which send the event `open -a` sends, a real `git commit`, and coming to the front for `die --wait` without `--background`. `the_restart_loop_finds_equal_digests_and_logs_each_time` failed once more under load, poisoning `two_launches_leave_one_instance`, and passed alone and on a rerun.

**Ex commands on lines, and insert state's Emacs keys**

658. `:d :y :pu :m :t :co :j :> :< :sort` follow your Evil's commands of those names, held by 288 new oracle cases in `cases/ex-lines.json`, run in batch Emacs with your Evil loaded read-only, under `:g` and `:v` too. They're rows of `EX`, so `:`'s list has them, and a row now says whether it takes a range: `P(...)` takes none and `L(...)` gets the lines. A name is a run of letters, or of `~&*@<>=:#`, as your Evil's parser reads one, so `:m0`, `:t.`, `:d2` and `:j3` need no space. A name that goes on with `-` is the command of that whole name, if one exists.
659. `:>>` and `:<<` are errors, and a count after `:>` or `:<` does nothing, as in your Evil: its parser reads `>>` as one unknown name, and `evil-shift-right` takes a count only in visual state. Vim shifts twice, or count lines. Veto for Vim's.
660. `:g/pat/d` and `:v/pat/d` with nothing after the `d` delete every line in one edit, into no register and not the clipboard, and leave the last search as it was, as your Evil's `evil--ex-performant-global-delete` does. The cursor goes to the last match, or where its line was. `:g/pat/d x` or `:g/pat/d 2` go line by line through the registers, as there. Both look for a match within each line, where Evil's quick path lets one run across lines.
661. Two new deviations, both Evil bugs. `:pu` takes the yank handler off the register's text in place, so the register, `0` and the kill ring hold characters after it, and `p` puts them inside a line (18 cases). And `:pu` on the last line puts an empty line first and drops the final newline (3 cases). Here the register keeps its lines, and lines put after the last line keep the final newline, as `p` does.
662. Ten cases join "the last line is the last line": when `:d` or `:g/pat/d` takes the last lines, the cursor goes to the end of the new last line, where your Evil's goes on the text without its final newline, and `:$j` keeps the final newline, as `J` does. `C-j` then `<BS>` in replace state joins "R's backspace takes back a newline".
663. A range given backward, as `:3,1d`, is refused with "Backward range given". Your Evil asks `y-or-n-p` whether to swap it. `:s`, `:norm` and `:g` still take it as no lines, as before.
664. `:sort` is `evil-ex-sort`'s: stable, `!` keeps ties in their order, `u` compares whole lines, folded with `i`, and a line with no number sorts first. Numbers stop at the size of an `i128`, where Emacs has bignums. A pattern is Rust's (D4) and always ignores case, as Emacs's `re-search-forward` does with `case-fold-search` at its default: smartcase doesn't apply. `:sort //` is a plain sort, as your Evil's is, since its last pattern is only set by `evil-search`, not by your isearch `/`.
665. `:0pu` puts after line 1, as your Evil's does (Vim's puts above it), and `:m0` and `:t0` go to the top. `:0d` deletes line 1, where your Evil's deletes nothing and keeps a newline in `"`, so that case isn't in the oracle. `:pu q` of a recording puts its keys as text, as `p` does here, where your Evil's `:pu` fails with "Wrong type argument".
666. In insert and replace state, `<Home>` and `<End>` go to the whole text's start and end. Your Emacs is the macOS build, whose `ns-win.el` binds them to `beginning-of-buffer` and `end-of-buffer` over `move-beginning-of-line`, and batch Emacs from the same install has that map too. `C-Left` and `C-Right` are `left-word` and `right-word`, with Emacs's word syntax: `$` and `%` are word characters and `_` isn't. `C-j` is `electric-newline-and-maybe-indent`, a newline with no indentation, which ignores `C-u`'s count as there. `<PageDown>` and `<PageUp>` are `scroll-up-command` and `scroll-down-command`: a window less two rows, or `C-u`'s count of rows, a cursor left behind going to the start of the row it left by, held by 13 tty cases.
667. `C-k` is your Evil's `evil-insert-digraph`, with its whole table: RFC 1345's 1,665 digraphs, either way round, generated into `digraphs.rs` by `tools/evil-oracle/oracle/digraphs.el` from evil-digraphs.el, as `scripts.rs` is from `word-scripts.el`. This reverses call 499's first half, which kept `C-k` out because scope §10 lists digraphs as out; this package was asked for it. scope.md isn't edited, so veto here to take it out again. Evil's file is GPL; what's taken is the mapping, which is RFC 1345's, for your licence review.
668. `<Insert>` goes from insert state to replace state and back, as your Evil's `evil-enter-replace-state` and `evil-append` do: the session's count goes, each state is a `u` of its own, `.` repeats both, register `.` holds the newer state's text, and leaving replace state steps back off a line's end. `<Insert>` in normal state, your Evil's `i`, isn't bound, since normal keys weren't in this package.
669. `C-g` in insert and replace state is `keyboard-quit`: a `C-u` count goes, it says `Quit`, and the state stays. `C-c` then any key, and `<S-Tab>`, your Emacs's `<backtab>`, say `C-c x is undefined` and `<backtab> is undefined` and do nothing, as your Emacs's global map has nothing there (init.el binds no `C-c` key). Your Emacs's bell then ends a running macro; here it's a message, so a macro goes on. The oracle only sees typed keys, where both say it and go on.
670. `C-h` is your Emacs's help prefix there, since your Evil's `evil-want-C-h-delete` is off: `C-h k` and `C-h c` run `describe-key`, `C-h x` and `C-h f` run `describe-command`, `C-h C-g` quits, and its other keys say they aren't built. This reverses call 499's second half for `C-h` and `C-j`.
671. The oracle knows `<C-Left>`, `<C-Right>`, `<PageUp>`, `<PageDown>` and `<S-Tab>` now, in evil-oracle.el's key names. New cases: 288 in `ex-lines.json`, 87 in `insert-emacs.json`, 13 in `insert-emacs-tty.json`, with 32 hand-written fixtures. Only those three fixture files were written; no other fixture was regenerated. The oracle test passes 2,696 of 2,696, with 140 deviations excluded.
672. Budget: die-vim from 9,400 to 10,160, its size: `lines.rs` 292, `sort.rs` 150, `digraphs.rs` 63 of data, insert state's keys about 170 in `insert.rs`, `insert_keys.rs` and `lib.rs`, `ex.rs` 37 and `scroll.rs` 26.
673. Tests: 9 harness tests in `tests/lines.rs` for what the oracle can't hold: a range given backward, a bad `:sort` pattern, a name that goes on with `-`, `:g/^/m0` on 5,000 lines undone by one `u`, `C-g`, `C-c` and `<S-Tab>`'s messages, `C-h k` and `C-h x`, and call 665's `:0d` and `:pu q`. Unit tests for `:sort`'s numbers and the digraph table. `a_day.rs`'s test that adds a row to `EX` by hand follows the rows' new `P(...)`. Not seen in a real window, and `:g/^/m0` on 20,000 lines takes about 5 s in a debug build, for call 639's markers.

**Running commands: `:make`, `:!` and `next-error`**

674. `compile`, `recompile`, `kill-compilation`, `shell-command` and `next-error` over `*grep*`, `*compilation*` and `*rebuild*` are in die-core, in a new `process` module, as grep is in core (call 444). die-vim's `:make`, `:!`, `:{range}!`, `:r` and the `!` operator call them. `next-error` and `compile-goto-error` moved there from grep.rs, so grep's rows and compile's errors share one way of going to a place. rebuild's runner (its pipe, reader thread, drain wait and 100 ms timer) is now core's `Ctx::stream`, which `compile` uses too, so `*rebuild*` gets `<CR>` and `:cn` on its errors for nothing.
675. How it relates to M2's process API: `die_core::process` is pipes only, with stdin a filter's text or nothing, no PTY and no `process(cmd)` builder with callbacks. Its public API, for your later review: `process::{shell, path, shown, last_line_start, Finished, Ran}` and `Ctx::{stream, stream_running, stop_stream, append_output, output_buffer, shell_output, shell_command}`. Contract §12's `process` and `pty` replace it, with `posix_spawn` and the signals from `sys`, and keep its PATH rule. die-core forbids `unsafe`, so a signal goes to a command's process group through `/bin/kill -s INT -- -PGID` until then.
676. The PATH comes from your login shell, once per instance: `$SHELL -l -c` prints it, on the first command's thread, and `~/.local/bin` and `~/.cargo/bin` go first if it hasn't got them. Your Emacs is emacs-plus, which took your shell's PATH when it was built (`EMACS_PLUS_PATH` in its Info.plist), and your `init.el` adds `~/.local/bin`. Asking at run time keeps up with changes to your profile without a rebuild. The cost: zsh -l from launchd's environment took 50 to 60 ms here, once per DIE start, never on the main thread. It reads `.zshenv` and `.zprofile`, not `.zshrc` (no `-i`), and yours gives Homebrew, cargo and `~/.local/bin` there. A shell that fails or takes over 3 s leaves DIE's own PATH with those two. Scope §5.8's PATH additions from config go on top later. `rebuild` uses this PATH now instead of adding `~/.cargo/bin` itself.
677. A command runs as `$SHELL -c` (or `/bin/sh`), as Emacs's `shell-file-name` with `shell-command-switch`, in its own process group so that `C-c C-k` reaches cargo and the rustc under it, with `PAGER` empty as `compile` has it. It runs in the buffer's directory, Emacs's `default-directory`, or the home directory for a DIE started in `/`, as `:grep` does.
678. A `:!`, a filter and `:r !` wait up to 500 ms on the main thread, as Emacs's `shell-command` always waits, so `:%!sort` in a macro, or before the next key, works on the result. Past that the command goes on in the background, with a message, and its output comes when it's done. A filter or `:r !` whose text changed meanwhile (its revision moved) leaves the text alone and says so. Nothing yet stops a `:!` that went on in the background, where Emacs's `C-g` stops its synchronous one: `:make` is the way to run something long.
679. Output stops at 8 MiB everywhere. `*compilation*` keeps the first 8 MiB with a note, and the command runs on until it ends or `C-c C-k`. A `:!` is stopped there (KILL to its group), since nothing could show the rest, so `:!yes` ends. A filter or `:r !` that reaches it leaves the text as it was.
680. `*compilation*` reads as Emacs's: the mode line with `default-directory` abbreviated, `Compilation started at` with `current-time-string`'s first 19 characters, a blank line and the command, then `Compilation finished at ..., duration 0.63 s`, `exited abnormally with code 101`, `interrupt`, `killed`. The command row says `Compilation finished`. die-core has no time zone code, so local time is UTC plus the offset `date +%z` gave once per instance: a daylight-saving change is an hour off until the next restart. The cursor stays at the top, as `compilation-scroll-output` nil leaves it, and the window comes as `*grep*`'s does, with the focus staying.
681. Its keys are your evil-collection's for `compilation-mode` in normal state: `<CR>`, `gj` `gk` `C-j` `C-k` `<Tab>` `<S-Tab>` (`compilation-next-error` and `-previous-error`), `gr` (`recompile`), and `C-c C-k`, which Evil leaves to the mode. Not `g` alone, as Emacs has it: your evil-collection unbinds the mode's `g`, so `gg` works, and recompiles on `gr`. Not built: `go` and `M-RET` (show without going), `[[` `]]`, `q` `ZZ` (`quit-window`).
682. What counts as an error: rustc's and cargo's ` --> path:line:col` and ` ::: `, `thread 'name' (id) panicked at path:line:col:`, and GNU's `path:line:col: message` and `path:line: message`. Your Emacs 30's `compilation-error-regexp-alist` has no Rust entry, so its `*compilation*` doesn't find `-->` lines; DIE does, as you asked. A `-->` under a `note:` or `help:` line, and a GNU message starting with `note` or `info`, is a note: `:cn` passes it, as `compilation-skip-threshold` 1 does, and `<CR>` opens it. A path with a space doesn't count, which is stricter than Emacs's pattern, so prose and clock times never match. A path is tried from the directory the command ran in, then from that directory's project, since cargo prints paths from the workspace root wherever it runs: from `crates/die-core/src`, `cargo test` says `--> crates/die-core/src/...`. A real `cargo build` and `cargo test` on a scratch crate, run from its `src`, checked this in a throwaway harness test.
683. `next-error` goes through the results made or gone to last, as Emacs's `next-error-last-buffer`: a new grep or compile, or `<CR>` in one, makes it the one. It says "error" for a compilation and "match" for grep, as Emacs's `compilation-error` does: "Moved past last error", "No more errors yet". With neither, "No grep or compilation results", where it said "No grep results".
684. One at a time, as `compile` asks: another while one runs asks "A compilation process is running; kill it? (yes or no)". `yes` kills the old one's group and drops its output and its last line. Anything else says "Cannot have two processes in `*compilation*' at once". `kill-compilation` sends INT, as Emacs's `interrupt-process`, or says "The compilation process is not running".
685. Before `compile` and `recompile`, each file with unsaved changes asks "Save file PATH? (y, n, !, q)", as `save-some-buffers` with `compilation-ask-about-save` does. The answer takes `<CR>`, as DIE's prompts do; Emacs takes the key alone. `Esc` runs nothing.
686. The command offered is the last one run, or the newest in the `compile` history, which the session keeps, or Emacs's `make -k `. `:make cmd` adds to that history too, where Emacs's `compile` called from code doesn't, so the command outlives a restart. `recompile` runs the last command where it ran, or after a restart the history's newest in this buffer's directory. `*compilation*` isn't kept by the session, as `*grep*` isn't: nothing could bring back its keys or its run.
687. `:!` without a range is Emacs's `shell-command`: one line of output goes on the command row, more into `*Shell Command Output*` below, the focus staying, and nothing printed says `(Shell command succeeded with no output)` or `(Shell command failed with code 3 and no output)`. DIE's command row holds one line, where Emacs's echo area grows to a quarter of the frame, so two lines go to the buffer. The cursor goes to its line's start first, as your Evil's `evil-shell-command` is an operator (oracle). `%` and `#` are the file's and the other buffer's names, `\%` a `%`, `:!!` the last command again, and `:!` alone "No shell command", as `evil-ex-replace-special-filenames` and `evil-previous-shell-command` have them.
688. Filters and `:r` follow your Evil, from 26 oracle cases: stderr goes in with the output, a failing command still replaces the lines, output without a final newline runs into the next line, the cursor goes to the range's start, and `u` puts it back where the text went. `:r !` goes below the line, `:0r` above the first, with a newline added after, the cursor on the last line read. `:r file` reads a file, the cursor on its first line, as `evil-read`'s `insert-file-contents` leaves it; no oracle case can name a file, so that one is from the source.
689. The `!` operator opens `:` with `.,.+N!`, as your Evil's does from normal state. From a selection your Evil's shows `'<,'>!`, and DIE's the same lines as `.,.+N!`. `.` doesn't repeat it, as it doesn't repeat `:`.
690. `M-!` is bound beside `M-x`, in the same states: Emacs binds it globally and your Evil leaves it there. Its prompt, `Shell command: `, has its own history.
691. Not built: `M-&` and a command ending in `&` (async shell commands), `C-u M-!`, completion in the command prompts, `compilation-auto-jump-to-first-error`, `:cc`, `:cfirst`, `:w !cmd`, and putting a refused filter's output somewhere. They're under "What isn't there yet".
692. Budget: die-core gets a module, "processes: compile, shell commands, next-error", at 1,012 of 1,020. die-core is 13,545 of 14,000, and grep's module went from 747 to 620 of 750, left as it was. die-vim from 10,160 to 10,330, at 10,326, for about 166 lines of `:!`, `:{range}!`, `:r`, the `!` operator and `:make`. The die crate lost 122 lines of rebuild's runner.
693. Tests: `crates/die/tests/compile.rs` (8: streaming and Emacs's lines, next-error over every form and past a note, `<CR>` and `gf` and `gj` `gk` in the output, `C-c C-k`, one at a time, `gr` and a bare `:make`, the prompt's default and history, saving first, the limit), `crates/die/tests/shell.rs` (9: the command row or the window, nothing printed, `M-!`, the directory and `%`, the PATH, filters and `:r`, a slow command and a slow filter over changed text, a runaway command), `:cn` through `*rebuild*`, and unit tests for the login shell's PATH, the clock, GNU lines and the output limit, all with `sh` as the command. 26 oracle cases in `cases/shell.json`, all passing. die-core's palette test typed `shl` for `say-hello`, which `shell-command` now outranks, so it types `sayh`.

**`die --wait` and files from Finder, after their review**

694. `die --wait` returns 1, not 0, when DIE quits or crashes before you're done, after `:cq`, and when none of its files would open. This reverses call 648's exit status. The review ran real git through the real client: DIE going after a `:w` of half a message committed `WIP: half a mess`, and `:q!` on `git rebase -i`'s list ran the rebase. Now git says "There was a problem with the editor" and stops, with nothing committed and a rebase not touched. `emacsclient` returns 0 when Emacs goes, and 1 for a file that won't open, so only the first is a difference. The client says on stderr which it was: "DIE quit before you were done with these", or "DIE gave up on these: :cq, or they wouldn't open". Rerun with the review's probe: a commit, a reword, two at once, the half message and `:cq` on a rebase all behave so.
695. A restart hands each waiting client to the new instance, so `:rebuild` with a commit open is invisible to git. Once the new instance's first frame is up, the old one says `moved` on each client's connection, with every file it still waits on and whether that file was made for it, and then goes. The client asks the next instance to wait on them `again`, giving it 15 s and trying again over the moment the lock is between the two. The new one waits on them in the buffers the session brought back (`Ctx::wait_again`): nothing is read again and nothing moves on screen, and a file the session didn't bring back is opened, as made for the client. If the restart fails nothing is said, and the clients go on waiting on the old instance. The review suggested passing the client's socket itself over the handover (SCM_RIGHTS). That needs `unsafe`, which die-core forbids, and this needs none. An older build that doesn't know `again` refuses it, and the client returns 1.
696. `:cq` (`:cquit`) on a file `die --wait` waits on gives it up, as Emacs's `server-edit-abort` does: every client waiting on that file fails, the file goes as `:q!` has it, unsaved text and all, and its clients' other files made for them go unless they have unsaved changes, as `server-delete-client` has it. `server-edit-abort` fails every client the server has, not only that file's. Your Evil's `:cq` refuses on a client's buffer ("Cannot exit client process with error code") and quits Emacs with an exit code anywhere else. DIE's refuses anywhere else, with `server-edit-abort`'s "This buffer has no clients", since DIE.app's exit code reaches no one and quitting would drop unsaved text. Veto to keep your Evil's refusal, which would leave `:cq` doing nothing here.
697. `die --wait` gives an instance that holds the lock and isn't listening up to 15 s, more than a restart's 10 s limit, where `die file` still gives 2 s. A DIE starting with a big session, or one in a slow handover, made git stop with "There was a problem with the editor" after 2 s.
698. `:q!` on a waited file runs only `server-buffer-done`, as your Evil's `evil-quit` with a bang does: no other file comes up, and nothing says "No server buffers remain to edit". `:q`, `ZQ`, `C-w q`, `:wq` and `:x` still run `server-edit`'s next step. The review counted `ZQ` with `:q!`, but your Evil binds `ZQ` to `evil-quit` without a bang, so it's `:q` there, as it already was here. What shows differs only for a file that was open before, which stays on screen. A killed file's window shows the buffer used last, which is usually the next file anyway.
699. Files from Finder, the Dock and `open -a` have their paths composed (NFC) with NSString's `precomposedStringWithCanonicalMapping`, so `café` from Finder is the buffer `café` typed in a terminal names, and `:b café` finds it. The review suggested NSURL's `path` instead of its file system representation, but a URL made with `fileURLWithPath:` stores the path decomposed, so its `path` is decomposed too, as the new test shows.
