A name stored decomposed on disk now shows composed. It's the same file, as APFS finds names either way. `platform::macos` goes from 1,300 to 1,305 for it, with the test that holds that limit: safe calls, with no `unsafe`.
700. Corrections the review asked for. Call 646 and using-die.md said a done file's window goes back to what it showed, as Emacs's `server-edit` has it. It shows what DIE's kill rule picks, and using-die.md now says so. Call 652's "takes the keyboard if and when DIE is the active app, as Finder and the Dock make it" isn't tried. The app test uses `open -g`, which doesn't make DIE active, so its assertion only catches DIE bringing itself to the front. Call 653's Open With claims aren't tried either. The review registered a test bundle and found that LaunchServices marks one in a temporary directory `launch-disabled`, and NSWorkspace offered it for none of 9 kinds of file. So no test bundle can show the claims, only your own `~/Applications/DIE.app`, and nothing here touched yours. using-die.md now calls them untried.
701. Not fixed: a file from Finder, the Dock or `open -a` that reaches a DIE about to go is still lost. (a) A launch that finds the lock held, with no files on its command line, goes before AppKit hands it the event: `open -n -a DIE file`, a second copy of DIE.app with your state, or a double-click in the moment after Cmd-Q, before the old instance has gone. (b) The old instance takes one in the second of a restart's handover, and the thread sending it on dies with it. Both last about a second, and only the open is lost: you open it again. (a) would need the event loop run with no window just to take the event, in the module at its `unsafe` review's limit, and neither can be tested without timing a race. It's on using-die.md's list.
702. Budget: clients from 90 to 145, at 143, for `:cq`, the handover and `wait_again`. session from 1,425 to 1,480, at 1,480, for `failed`, `moved` and `again` on the socket and `send_again`. die-vim from 10,330 to 10,340, at 10,334, for `:cq` and `:q!`. `platform::macos` from 1,300 to 1,305, at 1,301 (call 699).
703. API: die-core adds `Outcome`, `Waited`, `Ctx::wait_again`, `client_failed` and `hand_over_clients`, and in `instance` `send_again` and `Asked::again`. `Ctx::open_for_client` takes an `FnOnce(Outcome)`, and `client_done` takes `force`. `instance::Client::done` becomes `tell(&Outcome)`, and `Waiting::wait` gives an `Option<Outcome>`. die-render adds `appkit::file_path`. die adds `app::client_in`, the client given its state directory.
704. Tests: die's `tests/wait.rs` runs the real client (`client_in`) against an editor in the test. `:wq` returns 0. DIE going after a half-saved rebase list, `:cq`, and a directory return 1. A restart's handover, with a moment when nobody holds the lock, ends in `:wq` in the next instance, which returns 0 and takes the made buffer away. A lock held 3 s with nobody listening is waited out. die-vim's `tests/wait.rs`: `:q!` brings nothing up and says nothing. `:cq` fails the client and drops its files, except one with unsaved changes, and refuses anywhere else. A restart hands each client over with its made flags, and the next instance waits again without moving anything, and opens a file the session lost. A file that won't open fails its client. die-core's `tests/instance.rs`: `moved` and `again` carry an odd path there and back. die-render's `tests/platform.rs`: `file_path` composes what a file URL decomposes. The ignored app tests, run by hand and passing: `die --wait` returns 1 when its instance is killed. A restart hands two waiting clients to the new instance, which keeps them waiting until it's killed too. `open -g -a` with `café 日本.txt` finds the buffer by its composed name. No oracle case: a server buffer needs an `emacsclient`, which the oracle's batch Emacs doesn't have. Not tried: a restart of your DIE.app with a real `git commit` waiting, and a Finder double-click.

**Fixes from the review of the ex commands on lines and insert state's Emacs keys**

705. `C-g` in insert and replace state ends the session's recording, as your Evil's advice on `keyboard-quit`, `evil-repeat-abort`, does: `.` goes on repeating the change before the session, or has nothing to repeat if there was none, and the session's count (`3i`, `3o`, `3R`) and a selection's other lines put in only what was typed before the `C-g`. The review said the count goes. Your Evil, run in batch Emacs one key at a time with the quit caught, gives `abaax` for `3ia<C-g>b<Esc>` on `x`, because its count replays `evil-insert-repeat-info`, which still holds the keys before the abort. The review's run sent `3` and `i` as two macros, and that loses the count. Register `.` keeps all of it. `C-c C-g` and `C-h C-g` do the same; I didn't check whether your Emacs runs `keyboard-quit` for those. The oracle can't type `C-g`, since it quits the batch run, so `tests/lines.rs`'s `c_g_ends_what_dot_and_a_count_repeat` holds 8 sequences from that batch run. This amends call 669.
706. A count after `:d`, `:y` or `:j` takes lines from the range's last line, even when that's the text's last line with no final newline. There your Evil starts a line early, since `(line-beginning-position 0)` from the range's end is still on that line, so `:d 1` deletes the line above. New evil-bug deviation, "a count counts from the range's last line", with 5 hand-written fixtures. It's Vim's result.
707. A range's line 0 is line 1 for `:d`, `:y`, `:j`, `:m` and `:t`, as Vim has it for commands that can't take line 0. Your Evil makes `:0` an empty range at the text's start: `:0d` and `:0y` take an empty line, `:0j` does nothing, and `:0m$` and `:0t$` add an empty line at the end. New evil-bug deviation, "line 0 is line 1", with 5 hand-written fixtures; `:0d` is an oracle case now too. `:0>`, `:0sort` and `:0,2d` already matched. This extends call 665.
708. A `:m` to where the lines already are moves nothing. Given a range, it now leaves the cursor at the start of the range's first line, because your Evil's ex code goes there before `evil-move` returns. Without a range, the cursor stays. 5 new cases, with the cursor in the middle of a line.
709. After a range, `:y` puts the cursor back at the selection's start only if `:` was typed in visual state. Your Evil's `:restore-point` checks whether visual state is on when the command runs. A `'<,'>` typed in normal state leaves the cursor where it was, and so does `@:`. ex.rs passes whether the line came from visual state to the commands on lines, as `Vim::ex_visual`. 5 new cases.
710. `C-Left` and `C-Right` end words by your Emacs's own `word-separating-categories` and `word-combining-categories`, `((?H . ?K))` and kanji followed by kana, not by your Evil's CJK lists, which only its motions bind. So Latin then Greek or Cyrillic ends a word, `日本は` is one word, and `でテ` is two. text.rs has both sets of lists over the same script table. This amends call 666. 10 new cases.
711. `C-k` reads `<Tab>`, `<CR>`, `<BS>` and `C-a` to `C-z` (but not `C-g`) as the characters 9, 13, 127 and 1 to 26, as a first or a second key, because that's what your Evil's `read-key` gets from the oracle. An arrow or `<Esc>` still fails. In your GUI Emacs, `<tab>`, `<return>` and `<backspace>` are symbols, and Evil's `[t]` default binding may make `read-key` return them unchanged, so there they might fail. The oracle sends characters, and Vim inserts them too. 6 new cases.
712. Two new evil-bug deviations for text without a final newline, and DIE keeps its own behaviour in both. First, `:sort u` leaves the text without a final newline. Your Evil's unique pass deletes a repeated last line from its start, which keeps the newline before it (5 fixtures). Second, a `:m` of every line, such as `:m$` on a single line or `:%m$`: your Evil deletes all the lines and then fails with "Beginning of buffer", which leaves the text empty. DIE puts the lines back where they were, ending in a newline, as any `:m` to after the last line of such a text does in your Evil (3 fixtures).
713. In `:sort n`, `x`, `o` and `b`, a line with no number sorts as your Emacs's `most-negative-fixnum` does, so a number below -2^61 sorts before it, as in your Evil. This amends call 664's "a line with no number sorts first". 2 new cases, one with a tie.
714. `C-u n <Home>` and `C-u n <End>` are your Emacs's `beginning-of-buffer` and `end-of-buffer` with a number: n tenths of the text's characters from the start (plus one) or from the end, then the start of the next line. A bare `C-u` (or `C-u C-u`) isn't a number to them, so it goes to the start or the end. `.` and the session's count replay `<Home>` without the count, as your Evil's do. This amends call 666. 14 new cases.
715. `a_name_that_goes_on_is_another_command` now registers a real `move-it` command and checks that `:move-it far` runs it. `:move-nope` fails with "Invalid address", and the oracle's new `name-dash-unknown` case holds that your Evil fails there too. The review asked for the unknown-command error instead, but your Evil's parser falls back to `:move` with `-nope` when the longer name isn't a command, so I kept that.
716. Budget: die-vim goes from 10,340 to 10,383, its measured size. Most of the 45 lines are for `C-u n <Home>`, `C-g` ending the recording, and Emacs's word lists. sort.rs now uses operators.rs's `after_line` and drops its own copy.
717. Tests: 68 new oracle cases, 38 in `ex-lines.json` and 30 in `insert-emacs.json`. I regenerated both fixture files, and every case already in them came back byte for byte. There are 19 hand-written fixtures: 18 under the four new rules and one, `:$d 1`, under "the last line is the last line". In `tests/lines.rs`: the `C-g` test and the rewritten dashed-name test. Unit test: `emacs_words_keep_emacs_lists`. The oracle test passes 2,790 of 2,790, with 159 deviations excluded.

**Running commands: the review's fixes**

718. `:cn`, `<CR>` and `gj` read a line in time linear in its length. GNU's `path:line:` form looked at every colon and, for each, the whole line before it, so a 256 KB line of minified JSON in `*compilation*` took about 10 s per `:cn` on the main thread. Now only the colons before the line's first whitespace count, since a path has none, and a `-->`'s look back for its head reads 16 characters of each line. Long lines aren't skipped: Emacs's `compilation-max-output-line-length` only hides their end.
719. Commands still running when DIE quits or restarts are stopped: a hangup to each one's process group, as a closing terminal sends, then a kill after 300 ms to any still there. That's `:make`'s, `*rebuild*`'s and a `:!` that went on in the background, which core now keeps track of. Emacs asks "Active processes exist; kill them and exit anyway?" first (`confirm-kill-processes`, which your config leaves on). DIE doesn't ask, as quitting with `die --wait` clients doesn't (650), and a restart has no one to ask. Quit takes up to 300 ms longer only when a command ignores the hangup. A process a command left holding the pipe after it ended isn't stopped, nor anything after a crash.
720. rustc's paths, in ` --> `, ` ::: ` and `panicked at` lines, are tried from the cargo workspace's root first: the nearest directory up with a `Cargo.toml` that has `[workspace]` or a `[workspace.` table, or else the nearest with a `Cargo.toml`. Then the directory the command ran in, then its `.git` root. GNU's `path:line:` lines are tried from the directory it ran in, then the `.git` root, as before. The reviewer's three layouts (`.git` above the workspace, a root that's a package, cargo 1.96) failed before. A `rustc` run by hand from a member's directory, naming a path the root also has, now opens the root's: cargo is much the commoner. A path found nowhere says "Can't find PATH from DIR", where it said "PATH no longer exists".
721. Only the first `-->` under an `error` or `warning` line is a stop for `:cn`. A later `-->` in the same message, as rustc 1.96 prints into `/rustc/<hash>/library/std/...` for E0599's "trait not in scope", and every `:::`, as it prints for "method not found for this struct", is a note, as rust-mode has `:::` as info. `<CR>` on one still opens it.
722. `*compilation*` and `*rebuild*` show a line before its newline comes, as `cargo test -q`'s dots, and their carriage returns move as `comint-carriage-motion` has them: one with more of its line after it wipes the line so far, and one before a newline goes. Backspaces aren't moved over. While a command runs, a line it hasn't finished can read as an error, as `src/a.rs:3` before its `:5: error`, as it can in Emacs.
723. A filter or `:r !` whose output isn't UTF-8 leaves the text alone and says "`cmd` printed bytes that aren't UTF-8, so the text is as it was", as DIE opens files that aren't UTF-8 read-only and refuses a filter past 8 MiB. Your Evil would put the bytes in. `:!` with no range still shows them as replacements in `*Shell Command Output*`.
724. The session keeps no output buffer: whatever `output_buffer` made, which is `*grep*`, `*compilation*`, `*Shell Command Output*` and `*rebuild*`. It kept `*Shell Command Output*`, up to 8 MiB, and wrote it again about 1 s after each burst of changes. Your Emacs keeps none of them, as your config has no `desktop-save-mode`.
725. `:make` alone, `recompile` and `gr` after a restart run the last command where it ran, as they do before one. The command and its directory are kept as a two-entry history, `compile-ran`, which the session keeps with the prompts' histories, so session.json has no new part. It ran the newest `compile` history entry in the current buffer's directory. A fresh Emacs's `recompile` runs `make -k ` in the current directory, but DIE's restart means to change nothing you'd notice.
726. The PATH test runs in a process of its own with launchd's PATH, a scratch `HOME` and a login shell of its own that adds `/only/from/login`, and checks the whole PATH a `:!` gets. With the login shell's answer thrown away, it fails; the old test passed then.
727. Budget: the processes module from 1,020 to 1,110, at 1,106, about 95 lines: carriage returns and unfinished lines 35, stopping at quit 20, the workspace root 15, rustc's secondary spans and linear GNU lines 10, `compile-ran` and refusing bytes that aren't UTF-8 15. die-vim from 10,383 to 10,387, its size, for the 4 lines that refuse a filter's bytes that aren't UTF-8. ext, dispatch, rt at its 1,545, with shutdown's one line. session went down one, to 1,479 of 1,480.
728. Tests: 4 in `crates/die/tests/compile.rs` (a workspace whose root is a package, inside a bigger repository, with rustc 1.96's E0599 output verbatim and a GNU line; a bare `:make` after a restore; an unfinished line and carriage returns; a `:make` that ignores the hangup and a backgrounded `:!`, both gone after `shutdown`), 3 in `crates/die/tests/shell.rs` (the PATH in its own process, bytes that aren't UTF-8 through a filter, the session without a 3 MB `*Shell Command Output*`), and unit tests for a 256 KB colon-dense line, carriage returns and characters split between reads. Each new harness test but the PATH one failed on the code before, run by hand. No oracle cases: none of this is your Evil's.

**Tests off your screen**

729. A test's DIE can't be seen. You saw the tests' DIE windows open over your work. With `DIE_UNSEEN` set, DIE's window is see-through (alpha 0), clicks go through it, it casts no shadow, and DIE has no Dock icon. The window server still has the window and calls it covered, so DIE draws its first frame and then waits, as a covered window does. The app tests, the restart loop and `die file`'s window test set it. `open --env` carries it to a restart's new instance and to an instance a `die` client starts. In two full runs of the app tests, a read-only watch of the window list saw 40 test-DIE windows on screen, all at alpha 0, and every app test passed. The restart loop failed once under load and passed on its own and on the rerun (the flake in call 570).
730. S1's checks (`typing`, `redraw`, `firstframe`, `idle`, `resize`, `scale`, `occlusion`, `show`) can't be unseen, because they measure what reaches the display. I tried it. At alpha 0, no present reached the screen (`shown=0` of 500 keys). At alpha 0.01, 27 of 500 did, and macOS called the window covered, so `occlusion` had nothing to cover. So die-dev's window tests run them only with `DIE_ON_SCREEN=1`, and otherwise pass having done nothing, as they do where there's no backend. `ci --full` says when it left them out. They need a display no one is using: CI's macOS runner, once CI is set up, or a run you start when you step away.
731. Budget: `platform::macos` from 1,305 to 1,315, at 1,312: three safe setters, with `Surface::attach`'s one `unsafe` lookup shared by `view_of`, so the `unsafe` count is unchanged. Also checks from 730 to 735, and the platform stub from 170 to 175.

**A terminal in DIE**

732. You asked on 2026-11-09 for a terminal you can use in DIE as soon as possible, ahead of M1, and for it to respect Vim's modes: "Escape should change from insert to normal mode, but in normal mode perhaps escape can pass through to the terminal". D18 is amended to your evil-ghostel and vterm model. A terminal starts in insert, where every key but the DIE layer goes to the program. Esc goes to normal, except on the alternate screen, where it goes to the program, with a per-buffer toggle. Normal state is copy state over the screen and scrollback, with die-vim's grammar (D19). Esc there goes to the program, and `i a I A` return to insert. Survival across `:rebuild` (D20's holder and helper) comes in the next round, so a first terminal hangs up its shell on a restart.

**The terminal's engine, headless**

733. die-term's engine side is in, ahead of its surface: a `Term` that owns one engine terminal and needs no `Ctx`, so the next package can put it on a PTY in DIE now, and D20's helper can run it later. Bytes in, die-core cells out; keys, the pointer, pastes and focus in, bytes for the PTY out. It runs on the thread that calls it, as the engine's objects can't move between threads. For your Esc (732), it says whether the program is on the alternate screen, and it gives copy state the text with rows and columns. It uses core's public `Cell`, `Glyph`, `Rgb`, `Underline`, `CursorShape`, `KeyEvent` and `Mods`, and nothing else of core; core is unchanged.
734. die-term-sys is S2's, with the archive under `vendor/aarch64-apple-darwin/`, a directory per target as D10 has it. On any other target its build script links nothing, so the workspace builds and cross-checks for both Linux targets, and only a binary that calls the engine fails to link there. libghostty-rs is pinned at d2036bae with `default-features = false` and `kitty-graphics`, and the workspace's `[patch]` swaps its sys crate for die-term-sys. Builds stay offline: the rev is in `~/.cargo/git`, and clean-build passes from an empty environment.
735. `cargo xtask ghostty verify` checks the archive against `ghostty-vt.lock`: its SHA-256 and size, its 205 globals all `_ghostty_*`, its 39 needed symbols exactly the lock's list, and a link of the whole archive against libSystem alone. It holds the generated bindings to the lock's hash, and checks the signature's trusted comment and the wrapper's rev and patch. `ci` runs it as a step, and `clean-build` runs it first, since the archive is the one prebuilt binary D16 lets in. A changed archive or lock now makes `ci` run guards and clean-build. `ghostty bump` isn't built: S2's `tools/verify`, `tools/gen-bindings` and `derive-archive.sh` still do its steps by hand.
736. The lint: die-term-sys denies `unsafe` and allows it on its `ffi` module only, with both unsafe-block lints denied, as die-render and die-test-cap do. bindgen's 49 `Default` impls have no SAFETY comments and do two unsafe things each, so `mod bindings` alone may allow those two lints, and verify's hash keeps hand-written code out of that file. The bindings stay byte for byte as bindgen wrote them, with rustfmt skipping them. die-term has no `unsafe` and is neutral: no platform branches. A git dependency counts as pinned at a full commit hash and nothing else, and `sources` takes a crate at such a commit as a dependency, as it takes crates.io's.
737. Keys go through the engine's encoder, with Alt as Meta, the kitty flags (release and repeat included) and the legacy modes. In legacy encoding, a bare Ctrl-[, Ctrl-i and Ctrl-m send Esc, Tab and Return, as GUI Emacs, Vim and die-core's keymaps fold them; Ghostty alone sends CSI u for them. That's narrower than the record's shim. Shift-Return keeps Ghostty's `CSI 27;2;13~`, which your ghostel sends today and Claude Code reads as a new line, where kitty would send a plain CR. With the kitty flags on, all of them stay apart, as kitty's do.
738. The pointer is a small `Pointer` of die-term's own, in physical pixels from the grid's top-left; the integrator maps core's new pointer events to it. `Term::wheel` has the record's policy: wheel buttons to a program that tracks the mouse, arrow keys on the alternate screen with mode 1007 (on by default, as in Ghostty, so `less` scrolls), and otherwise the scrollback.
739. What the engine asks of DIE comes out as events: titles, the working directory, the bell, OSC 52 clipboard writes, and OSC 9 and 777 notifications. A callback would need `Ctx`, which a `Term` doesn't have. OSC 52 reads are denied inside the engine's own callback, so DIE is never asked or waited on. Replies to queries collect for the PTY: DA as Ghostty answers it (`62;22`), DSR, XTVERSION as `DIE 0.0.0`, XTWINOPS and mode 2048 in physical pixels, OSC 10, 11 and 4 in the theme's colors, and XTGETTCAP's terminal name as `xterm-256color`.
740. `feed` parses at most 64 KiB a call and returns how much it took. A 16 MB flood of colored build log took 257 calls in release, at 0.22 ms median, 0.27 ms p99 and 0.40 ms at most, under load 4, with nothing lost where the pieces cut a sequence. Synchronized output (2026) holds the frame for up to 1 s. The frame held is the last one drawn, not one captured as the hold starts, as the record's G13 has it, which needs a render-state update inside the engine's callback.
741. Copy state's text is one line per row, with its absolute row, whether it soft-wraps, and each character's column, so a byte maps to a cell and back, with a wide character two columns wide. Rows ever pushed out of the scrollback still count, so a row keeps its number as the oldest go. The cursor's row keeps its blanks through the cursor's cell, so the cursor has a character to be on. `mark` and `added_since` count the lines added since a point; after a resize, a switch of screens or a flood past the whole scrollback, `added_since` says so, and copy state reads it all again. `text_from` reads from a row on. The rows come from a second render state over the whole scrollback: 9,637 rows of 80 columns take 12.8 ms in release. S2's formatter took 2.1 ms, but it loses the columns and the wraps. If entering copy state feels slow on a full scrollback, reading only the screen first is the fix.
742. Deferred: DIE's PNG hook and kitty image placements, which need core's placements. Until then the engine refuses a kitty PNG, and stores raw images it can't show. Hyperlinks, OSC 133 prompts, the engine's selection and search, and the helper's messages are later packages'. die-term-sys's notices name the archive's six components and their licences, but only Ghostty's text is there; `ghostty bump` is to fetch the rest.
743. `ci --linux` would have refused the git source, and die-term's tests can't link on Linux. Its containers now keep cargo's git checkouts in a third volume, the networked fetch may fetch libghostty-rs at the lock's rev too, and die-term's tests are left out on Linux, as D10 says. Not run in colima this round, so the next checkpoint tries it first.
744. Budget: die-term at 1,140 of guard 7's 5,000 (4k of glue plus 1k for the helper), with ratchets at their size and a little room: terminal 307 of 320, cells 213 of 225, input 379 of 395, copy text 241 of 255. die-term-sys at 71 of 100. A crate's `generated` files are now counted apart, in no total, so the 5,575 lines of bindings aren't product code. Product code goes from 29,616 to 30,827.
745. Tests: 36 in die-term, all headless. Cells: true color, the palette and default colors from the theme, every attribute, the underline styles and colors, wide characters and clusters, scroll regions, the alternate screen, the cursor, dirty rows, a scrolled-back viewport, resize and synchronized output. Keys: legacy, the shim, DECCKM, the kitty flags with release and repeat, an AZERTY key, modifier sides and Caps Lock. Mouse: four tracking modes, five encodings, modifiers and the wheel's three ways. Also replies, events, pastes, focus, copy text and the flood. Three tests pin the known gaps (OSC 99, OSC 66, the title stack), so the bump that closes one says so. Unit tests cover verify, the lint's two new rules, `sources` with a pinned git crate, and the budget's generated files. Where the engine differs from the record is in its new §10 ([records/die-term-engine.md](records/die-term-engine.md)).

**The terminal's core half: surfaces and PTYs**

746. A surface renders into a `Frame` of `FrameCell`s over its window's whole area, with no gutter, margins or centered width, as contract §10 now has it. A cell's colors are `Color::Rgb` or `Color::Default`, the `default` face's, so a surface needs no theme. Core resolves the attributes the scene's cell doesn't hold: reverse swaps (the defaults too), faint blends 65 % foreground toward the background before the swap, as ghostel's `dimColor` does, invisible draws a space with no underline or strikethrough, and blink draws steadily. `FrameCell::from(Cell)` takes die-term's resolved cells as they are. Core keeps each surface's frame between frames, so a terminal can rewrite only its dirty rows, and `is_new()` says when every row needs writing: first render, new size, or another window of another size drew it last.
747. `Surface::cursor` gives a `SurfaceCursor`: position, shape (block, bar, underline or hollow), visible and blinking, drawn steady. Core's hollow rule holds: hollow in a window that doesn't take input, with a prompt open, or with DIE's window unfocused. `CursorShape::for_state` gives text's shape for a state, with passthrough's as insert's bar, so die-term can let the modal state choose the shape on the main screen, as evil-ghostel does, and the program choose on the alternate one. That's die-term's to do; core draws what the surface says.
748. A surface window's status line is the buffer's status text, then the surface's `status()` (a terminal's title), then Evil's tag for its state at the right: `<I>` in passthrough and insert, `<N>` in normal, `<V>`, `<R>` and `<O>`. Text windows keep your mode line's directory alone, as your `mode-line-format` has no state. A terminal's cursor is the program's on the alternate screen, so the tag is the one thing that always says which state Esc and the keys are in. Veto it and the surface's segment stays.
749. Passthrough's Esc, as D18 is amended: a plain Esc, with no modifier, leaves for normal state unless the surface's new `takes_esc()` says Esc is the program's now (die-term: the alternate screen, or its toggle). Shift-, Ctrl- and Alt-Esc go to the program. Cmd-Esc always leaves. Either Esc that leaves swallows its repeat and release, as Cmd-Esc did. `describe-key` follows the same rule, calls passthrough "Insert", and says that a surface in insert state gets every key but the DIE layer's, the leader's too; `describe-command` lists a passthrough binding as Insert.
750. Cmd-V in passthrough runs `surface-paste`, a core command the `die` facade binds there beside Cmd-Q: one `Event::Paste` with the system clipboard, or the `"` register if the clipboard has no text. Given a register (`"a` through `Args`), it pastes that register, so die-term can bind `p` and `P` in normal state to it. A Cmd chord DIE doesn't bind goes to the program, as before. Every other key reaches the surface: a test in `crates/die/tests/terminal.rs` types the first key of every binding die-vim's insert state has in text, 37 keys, 22 of them Ctrl keys such as C-c, C-g, C-h, C-k and C-x, then `\`, under your real die-vim and facade, and the surface gets each one. In normal state die-vim's text keys reach nothing but the surface's `event()`, as no keymap of the `term` kind binds them yet.
751. `Event` is `#[non_exhaustive]` now, so the next events break no surface. `Resize` gains the window and `cell: CellPixels`, a cell's width and height in physical pixels and the display's scale, which die-render says through `Ctx::set_cell_pixels` at each new drawable; a change sends every surface window a `Resize`. `open_window` still sends none, since its caller gave the size. `Pointer(SurfacePointer)` has the kind (press, release, drag, move, precise scroll), the cell and the physical pixel in the area, the clicks and the modifiers. A press focuses the window and ends a waiting command, as a press on text does, and the drags and the release go to that window, held inside its area, as a terminal's mouse report wants. `PointerEvent` gains `px`, the pixel die-render had, kept at the last move within a cell. Motion is still an event per cell, so the SGR-pixels mouse mode (1016) gets cell-sized steps.
752. PTYs start without `unsafe`, through DIE's own binary: `die --pty-exec name program args`, on the PTY, calls `setsid` and TIOCSCTTY through rustix and `exec`s the program, which keeps the pid DIE's `Child` has. Your config's `main` calls `die::other_mode()` first; a test binary that opens PTYs has no harness and calls `die_core::pty::trampoline()`. On Linux it runs `/proc/self/exe`, which a rebuild can't take away. A warm start costs under 10 ms; the first run of a new build took 0.64 s once, macOS's first look at a new binary. S3's `posix_spawn` with `CLOEXEC_DEFAULT` and `SETSID` needs `unsafe`, so it isn't used: every fd DIE opens is close-on-exec instead. The master opens `/dev/ptmx` with `O_CLOEXEC`, which is what `posix_openpt` does on macOS and Linux, since rustix's `openpt` can't set it on macOS, so there's no window for a fork to catch it. A test checks one terminal's program holds no fd of another's, and fails with `O_CLOEXEC` taken off. Fds the holder sends arrive without close-on-exec on macOS (S3), so D20's round gives the trampoline a sweep, in `sys`.
753. Each PTY has a thread that polls the master and a nudge pipe, reads and writes non-blocking, and a waiter that waits with `waitid(WNOWAIT)` before reaping, so the kill after a hangup can't reach a pid that's been reused. Output goes to `on_output` from the top of the loop, at most 256 KiB a turn per PTY, and then a key or the frame goes, as timers' batches do. The reader keeps 1 MiB waiting at most, and then the program waits in the kernel. A host doesn't sleep while output waits: `Ctx::pty_output_waiting`, which die-render's loop now reads and `next_deadline` counts. The exit comes after the last output, or 200 ms after the program ends if something it left holds the terminal, and a reader that was full first reads what the kernel still holds: macOS keeps a program in its exit until then, but Linux's larger buffer needn't. `write` never waits. `foreground_group()` and `echoes()` read the terminal, for confirming a kill and spotting a password prompt.
754. Closing a `Pty`, or dropping it, hangs the terminal up and kills the program's process group 300 ms later if it's still there, and no callback runs after. Quit and restart close every PTY first, then stop the other commands, so quitting with a program that ignores the hangup takes up to 300 ms more. Until D20, a `:restart` therefore hangs up every terminal's shell, as call 732 says.
755. A program on a PTY gets DIE's environment with your login shell's PATH (`process::path`, what `:make` gets), `TERM=xterm-256color`, `COLORTERM=truecolor`, `TERM_PROGRAM=DIE` and `TERM_PROGRAM_VERSION=0.0.0`, die-core's version, which matches die-term's XTVERSION. What a terminal DIE started from would leave is taken out: `COLUMNS`, `LINES`, `TERMINFO`, `TERM_SESSION_ID`, `LC_TERMINAL`, `LC_TERMINAL_VERSION`, `INSIDE_EMACS`, `WINDOWID` and the `GHOSTTY_`, `KITTY_`, `ITERM_`, `WEZTERM_`, `ALACRITTY_` and `VTE_` variables. DIE.app from the Dock has none of them; `cargo run` from Ghostty has several, and Ghostty's `TERMINFO` would hide `xterm-256color` from macOS's ncurses. The command's own `env` and `env_remove` come last.
756. rustix `=1.1.5`, already in the lock through winit's Linux crates, is die-core's with `std`, `event`, `fs`, `pipe`, `process`, `pty` and `termios`: pure Rust, libc's calls on macOS and syscalls on Linux, no C and no build-script compile. die-core stays `forbid(unsafe_code)`, and `sys` gains only `own_exe`, so `sys` needs no `unsafe` this round either. Both Linux targets cross-check.
757. die-render needed no fix for keys: its matrix already showed every press, repeat and release reaching core, and a new check on the same real event loop puts a surface in passthrough and posts `j` held with 6 repeats, C-a and Shift alone to this process: the surface hears every event, and Esc's release is swallowed as it leaves. Its changes are the pointer's pixel, the cells' pixels to core, and not sleeping while PTY output waits.
758. Budget: die-core from 14,000 to 14,790, at 14,789, about 790 over guard 7's "about 14k", for the terminal's core half. Its new module, surfaces and PTYs, is 971 of 975: the frame and its cells, cursor, status and pointer about 420, PTYs and the trampoline about 550. Ext, dispatch and rt from 1,545 to 1,575 (the trait, the events, a PTY's turn); the command line's module from 1,370 to 1,390 (describe-key's wording); session from 1,480 to 1,490 (`sys::own_exe`); die-render's neutral from 2,300 to 2,305.
759. Tests: 11 real-PTY tests in `crates/die-core/tests/pty.rs`, without a harness so its `main` is the trampoline: `/bin/sh` echoes a line and `$((40+2))`; `stty size` says 24 80, then 33 101 after a resize; `exit 7`, `kill -9 $$` and a missing program (127, named on the terminal); the directory, `arg0`, the environment and the login PATH; `sh -i` is a session leader (`Ss`) whose job gets the foreground and whose `stty -echo` `echoes()` sees; 1 MiB written to a program that doesn't read takes no time; `yes` into a 4 ms-a-batch consumer doesn't hold up a key past 2 turns or a turn past 250 ms, which hangs with the per-turn gate taken out; all 3,600,003 bytes of a flood into that slow consumer before its exit, and none after; no fd of one terminal in another's program; a close kills a program ignoring the hangup after 300 ms; and nothing (a deaf `sh`, `sh -i` and its background `sleep`) is left after `shutdown`. 9 harness tests in `crates/die-core/tests/surface.rs` (every attribute's resolution over the whole area, the frame kept until a resize, the cursor's shapes and hollow cases, the status line, `Resize` in pixels, the pointer in the area's cells and pixels, paste, the DIE layer and Esc in passthrough, normal state's keymap), 3 in `crates/die/tests/terminal.rs`, and the key check in die-render's `keys.rs`. Existing tests now say Esc leaves passthrough.
760. Open: die-term isn't connected, so no `term` command exists; the integrator maps `Term::frame`'s cells (`FrameCell::from`), its cursor, title and `modes` to `render`, `cursor`, `status` and `takes_esc`, and core's events to its keys, `Pointer` and `paste`. D20's holder takes the PTY next round, with kqueue for the exit after a restart and the trampoline's fd sweep. Placements (kitty images) wait for core's. The Linux PTY path is cross-checked, not run. Copy state's own package builds normal state's motions.

**Copy state: normal state over a terminal's text**

761. Normal state on a surface that gives its text is copy state (D19). Core takes a read-only `TextBuf` snapshot as the buffer leaves passthrough, by either Esc or `set_state_of`, and drops it on the way back. While it's there, `Ctx::text` and `text_mut` give it, so every die-vim motion, count, object, `/ ? n N * #`, mark, `v V C-v` and yank runs on it unchanged. There's no copy-state keymap: core looks a key up in the text kind's keymap right after the surface kind's, so die-term's `<Esc>`, `i a I A` and `p P` win. The contract had die-vim register its grammar again as a copy keymap; this is the same keys with no second table to keep in step.
762. `Surface::text(update) -> Option<SurfaceText>` and `Surface::scroll_to(Option<usize>)`, both defaulted. A `SurfaceText` is lines ending in newlines, the surface's cursor as a line and a byte, and the line its live view starts with. An update replaces the last text from line `from` after `dropped` lines left its start. It's synchronous, not the contract's `text(done)`: the engine's whole read of 9,637 rows takes about 10 ms. `Ctx::surface_text_changed(buf)` marks the snapshot stale. A surface with no text has no copy state. Scope §5.5's fallback to its rendered cells isn't built: it would take `j` and `k` from surfaces that handle their own keys in normal state.
763. What shows: the window keeps the surface's own cells and colors. Before each render, core tells the surface (`scroll_to`) which snapshot line is at the window's top, and die-term shows its viewport there. Over those cells core draws die-vim's cursor, in the state's shape, and recolors the cells under the selection and the search's matches with the `selection`, `search` and `search-current` faces. A face that sets no foreground leaves the text's color. The surface's own cursor isn't drawn in copy state. The frame needed no change.
764. Following works the way evil-ghostel's point follows the cursor's row. A window follows when the buffer is in normal state, no prompt is open, and its cursor is on the surface's cursor line or below it. A follower on the surface's cursor stays on it. One below keeps its distance and display column. Each keeps its distance above the live view. Other windows stay on their lines, less any dropped, so the snapshot is frozen for them. The scope says `G` resumes; any way back to the bottom does, `j` too. Visual and operator-pending state wait until they end. An update is read at the next key or frame, so a flood costs one read a frame.
765. An update is a splice. It replaces the lines from the old screen top and drops the oldest. Markers and views move as for an edit, but nothing goes in the change log or undo (`TextBuf::splice`), so marks in the history stay on their text. die-term's `CopyText` reads `text_from` the old screen top, or the whole text after a resize, a screen switch, or a flood past what the marks count. A test checks that twelve updates through a scrollback-wide flood come to what one whole read gives.
766. A window's top never scrolls below the live view's top line (`TextBuf`'s top limit), since the engine can't show rows below its screen. So `zt` on the last line, and `C-e` and `C-f` at the bottom, stop there, and so does `G`'s recentring.
767. The snapshot ends at the terminal cursor's line, or at the last row drawn on if that's lower, so `G` and `j` stop at the prompt, as evil-ghostel's `j` and `G` stay on it. A soft-wrapped line is a line per row, so `yy` on a wrapped command yanks one row.
768. Edits on a snapshot fail with "Buffer is read-only", Emacs's words. die-vim's `insert::begin` refuses on a snapshot, so `o O R gi c s S C` and visual `I A` never put a terminal in insert or replace state. `u` says there's nothing to undo, and `.` that there's no previous change.
769. A resize reads the whole text again after the surface hears it, since a terminal reflows. Followers go back to the cursor, and other windows keep their line numbers, so a split doesn't lose your place. After a width change, the lines there are the reflowed ones.
770. The pointer in copy state works as on text: a click moves the cursor, a drag selects into visual state, the wheel scrolls the snapshot, and the program hears none of it. Each drawn cell maps to its snapshot position for that.
771. `C-u` stays the prefix argument in a terminal's normal state, as in your Evil and in text. The package listed it among the scroll keys. You can veto this: it would be one binding in the term kind.
772. Budget: die-core from 14,790 to 15,160. Copy state is about 370, and die-core is now about 1,160 over guard 7's "about 14k". Surfaces and PTYs goes from 975 to 1,285 (the new `surface/copy.rs`), text and undo from 1,425 to 1,450, layout from 950 to 960, ext, dispatch and rt from 1,575 to 1,600, and die-vim from 10,387 to 10,390. die-term has a new module, copy state, at 63 of 80.
773. Tests: 12 in `crates/die-vim/tests/copy.rs` over a shell-like test surface:
     - 20,000 lines through Esc, `gg`, `/19999<CR>`, `yy`, `G` and `vey`
     - following at the bottom, not after `k`, with `G` catching up, and updates only, not whole reads
     - visual state holding it
     - the oldest lines dropping under a frozen cursor
     - the surface's red under the selection, and the cursor's place
     - 24 edit keys refused
     - the kind's Esc winning, `i` back to the live view, and describe-key naming `kind text`
     - the scroll keys stopping at the live view, and the wheel
     - `p` pasting into the program, and a split keeping its place
     - a click and a drag
     - die-vim's marks and jumps from an old snapshot doing no harm in a new one
     6 in `crates/die-term/tests/copy.rs` over a real `Term` with no PTY:
     - `seq 1 20000`'s output, then search, yank and the bottom, with each cursor row showing its line's cells
     - twelve updates that equal a whole read, frozen after `5k`
     - the engine's red kept, and the selection over it
     - the alternate screen's rows alone
     - `i` back to the live screen
     - the timing in 774
774. Timing, in the test profile, which is optimized: a whole read of the engine's 9,637 rows of 80 columns takes 10.4 ms at best of five. Entering copy state over them through core takes 10.2 ms with the rope and the window's view, and Esc on 20,000 rows of `seq` output takes about 23 ms with its frame. Over the test surface, 10,000 lines of 69 columns take 0.3 ms, so the engine's read is the whole cost.
775. Open:
     - Wiring into die-term's surface once it lands: `text` and `scroll_to` from `CopyText`, `surface_text_changed` after output, and a full frame after the viewport moves.
     - Joining soft-wrapped rows for `yy`.
     - Editing the shell's line from normal state, which is D18's later item.
     - A frozen window over rows that a flood has pruned shows whatever the engine has there.
     - A snapshot's screen rows can change under a frozen window, so a yank there gives what was read, not what's drawn now.
     - Wide characters whose width core and the engine disagree on, some emoji sequences among them, would put the cursor a cell off.

**The engine's review, fixed**

776. A review of the engine side (733–745) found 3 major and 8 minor findings. I checked each before fixing it, with a probe or, for the licences and the xtask rules, by reading, and all 11 held. Ten are fixed, and one is partly fixed: the licence texts (784). Each code fix has a test that fails with the fix taken out; I took them out one at a time to check.
777. A program's own colors now redraw the screen. OSC 10 and 11 set the default colors, and DECSCNM reverses the screen (vim's `set vb`, `tput flash`). The engine marks neither as a full redraw, so `frame(false)` gave nothing, and later output repainted only its own rows in the new colors, which left the screen striped. The frame now keeps the default colors it last drew in, and a change makes the next frame full. Palette changes (OSC 4 and 104) were already full.
778. Rows keep their numbers through a flood that comes in one read. Core reads 64 KiB at a time (753), which is 21,845 lines of `yes`, about twice what the scrollback keeps. So the tracked row was pruned inside one `feed`, and numbering restarted from the old base. The old numbers were reused, so a stored row landed on the wrong line. `feed` now parses 1 KiB at a time and counts after each slice. That costs about 1 %: on the 16 MB build log, 0.218 ms median a call, against 0.215 ms for one write. Crafted output can still push out the whole scrollback within 1 KiB (REP, `a\e[65535b`, makes 819 lines from 9 bytes). Then the rows are numbered on from past every number given out. An old number names no line rather than the wrong one, and `added_since` says to read everything again, as 741 has it. A reverse index at the screen's top moves the tracked row without losing it, and keeps the numbers, as before.
779. `feed` is bounded in time as well as bytes. It returns at 64 KiB, or after the slice that passes 2 ms (`FEED_BUDGET`). Crafted output used to take 0.13–0.37 s a call at 200×60 (REP, DECALN, ED 2, RIS, IL). In release, under load 5, it now takes at most 8.4 ms (REP); RIS takes 4.2 ms and the rest 2.2–2.6 ms. Ordinary output still goes 64 KiB a call: a read of `yes` takes 1.9 ms, and the build log 0.22 ms. Replies waiting for the PTY are capped at 1 MiB (`REPLIES_MAX`). Past the cap, new replies are dropped; a paste's bytes don't count. `FEED_MAX`'s doc promised the main thread was never held for long. It now says the bound is on bytes, and so is 740's.
780. Kitty graphics are off until DIE draws them. The engine's image limit is now 0, not 64 MiB, so a program's support query gets no answer. Programs send DA1 after the query, and DA1's answer arriving alone says no, so they show their text instead of images nothing would draw. Before, they got `OK`, and up to 64 MiB of images a screen were kept unseen. `IMAGE_BYTES` stays as the limit for when placements are drawn (742).
781. Text the OS commits goes to the program as its UTF-8, whatever the kitty flags, as kitty sends it. That covers an input method, the emoji picker and a dead key's composition, the cases where `KeyEvent::text` is set. With flag 8 alone, the encoder sent 日本 as `CSI 26085 u`, a key for its first character, and lost the second.
782. Two small fixes. `Term::new` now makes a zero cell size one pixel, as `resize` did, so a surface that makes its `Term` before it knows the font still gets mouse reports. `Event::Pwd` is the program's own form: a `file://` URL from OSC 7, or a bare path from ConEmu's OSC 9;9. The docs now say so.
783. `ci --linux` would have failed at clean-build. Its `cargo build --workspace --all-targets` links die-term's tests, which need the engine. I reproduced it on macOS with the archive moved aside: the build fails on die-term's `flood` test, and the two builds below pass. Where die-term-sys has no archive for the host, clean-build now builds the workspace without die-term, then die-term's library alone. linux-host's "other tests" leave die-term out on the same check, `ghostty::links`, which reads `vendor/<target>/`. Both exclusions go away once DIE's CI vendors a Linux archive. Still not run in colima.
784. Tighter rules on the two exceptions, and the licence field. The budget's `generated` may name only die-term-sys's bindings, the file verify holds to the lock's hash, so no other file can drop out of the totals. The lint and `sources` now take a git dependency only from the wrapper's repository (`ghostty::WRAPPER`) at a full commit; before, any repository at a full commit passed. die-term-sys's licence field said `MIT OR Apache-2.0`, which covers only its own code. It's now an SPDX expression for the archive too, as THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES names its parts. The other five components' licence texts still aren't vendored. They have to come from the sources at the locked commits, which is a download outside cargo, so `ghostty bump` still fetches them (742).
785. Tests: 10 new in die-term, 52 in all. Cells: the program's colors and DECSCNM in `frame(false)`, every row after a screen switch, and a hold that outlasts `HOLD_TIMEOUT`. Text: a 64 KiB read of `yes` and one of `\n`, numbered exactly; REP past the scrollback, numbered on; a reverse index keeping the history's numbers. Also flood (REP stops at the budget), keys (commits under flags 0, 1, 8 and 31), replies (kitty's query unanswered, the reply cap, OSC 9;9) and mouse (a zero cell). xtask has 3 new unit tests (clean-build's builds, Linux's other tests, where the engine links), and the budget, pin and sources tests now cover the narrower rules. Budget: die-term from 1,205 to 1,244. The terminal module goes from 320 to 335 (now 328) for the slices and the cap; cells is at 218 of 225, input 387 of 395, copy text 248 of 255. Product code is 32,355.
786. Open:
     - Core hands a surface up to 256 KiB a turn (753). A surface that loops `feed` over all of it takes as long as those bytes do, up to about 1 s of crafted output a turn. The surface should feed only while its turn's budget lasts, and keep the rest for the next turn.
     - Core's `Pty::write` queue has no cap. A program that floods queries and never reads its input grows it, by about three times its output.
     - Synchronized output still holds the last frame drawn (740), and the five licence texts wait for `ghostty bump`.

**The terminal's core half: what its review found**

787. Metal opens its shader cache (four files under `com.apple.metal`, one of 400 MB) read-write without close-on-exec, and std's spawn passes them on, so every program on a PTY in DIE held them, and so would a tmux server it started, after DIE quit. 752 and 760 were wrong that every fd reaching a program is DIE's. `die::other_mode()` now marks every fd above 2 close-on-exec before the trampoline `exec`s: a 20-line `platform::macos::fds` in die-render lists `/dev/fd` and sets the flag with rustix, with one `unsafe` borrow of a listed fd. It sits in die-render's reviewed `platform` rather than in `sys`, so die-core stays `forbid(unsafe_code)`; that block is yours to review, and you can veto where it lives. `die_core::pty::trampoline()` alone doesn't sweep, which is fine where no framework runs (die-core's and die-term's tests); the Linux stub does nothing until `platform::linux`. `crates/die/tests/pty_fds.rs`, a harness-less binary whose `main` is `die::other_mode()`, runs `ls -l /dev/fd/` on a PTY from a DIE with its window and Metal up, unseen: it lists only 0, 1, 2 and `ls`'s directories, and the four cache files without the sweep. D20's holder fds get the same sweep.
788. A program on a PTY gets `LANG` when none of `LC_ALL`, `LC_CTYPE` and `LANG` is set, as in DIE.app from the Dock (launchd has none): your region from `AppleLocale` in UTF-8 (`en_AU` gives `en_AU.UTF-8`, `zh-Hans_CN` gives `zh_CN.UTF-8`) if `/usr/share/locale` has it, else `en_US.UTF-8`, as Ghostty falls back to. Without it zsh shows `中é` as `�<00ad>é` and `locale` says C. It's `defaults read -g AppleLocale` once, 12 ms, on the PTY's thread, in `sys::lang`; on Linux it's `C.UTF-8`. A locale DIE has is passed on as it is, and the command's own env still comes last. This adds to 755. A test runs the PTY tests' binary again without the three variables, and its shell says `LANG=…UTF-8` and `charmap UTF-8`.
789. A key that leaves the focused buffer in passthrough, when it didn't go through passthrough itself, swallows its own repeat and release: die-term's `i a I A`, a command that opens a terminal, `<CR>` on a prompt over one. Before, `i`'s release reached the program, which with kitty's flags 3 is `\x1b[105;1:3u`, a release with no press. The other way round, Cmd pressed alone before Cmd-Esc reaches the program and its release comes in normal state; that's in §7 rather than a made-up release, since core doesn't know which Cmd it was and only a program with flag 8 sees it.
790. A drag or release whose press went to a text window never reaches a surface it crosses: it stays with the text, whose drag selection then ends as it should. Before, the surface got a `Drag` and a `Release` with no `Press`, which a program with mouse tracking takes as button 1 held.
791. After `render` panics, the next good render finds its frame blank and new, so a terminal that writes only dirty rows writes them all. Before, the frame kept the placeholder and wasn't new, and rows the panicking render had taken from the engine's dirty set never came back.
792. `surface-paste` given a register reads it by the rule `<C-r>` at a prompt uses (`set_prompt_register`, die-vim's `for_prompt`), not core's raw map: `"+p` is the clipboard, `"Ap` is `a`, `"_p` pastes nothing, and `:`, `/` and `%` are die-vim's. Tested with the real die-vim in `crates/die/tests/terminal.rs`. No key gives it a register in a terminal yet, since `"` isn't bound there.
793. PTY output a frame: with no key waiting, batches go on for 4 ms (`pty::OUTPUT_TURN`) from the turn's first, then the frame; a waiting key still goes after one batch, and the flood test's key still runs within 2 turns. Before, a frame took one 256 KiB batch per PTY, about 15 MiB/s at 60 Hz and 30 at 120, as the review worked out. `yes` with a 120 Hz frame between turns now gives about 990 KiB a frame, 80 MiB/s, and the reader's 1 MiB is the cap a frame. This replaces 753's "at most 256 KiB a turn" and 786's "up to 256 KiB a turn": a turn is batches now.
794. Wording: describe-key's line for a key in a terminal's insert state no longer says the leader works in normal state: die-vim binds the leader's maps, `C-w` and `:` on kind `text`, which reach a terminal's normal state only through copy state's snapshot (761), once die-term gives its text (775). It says keys go through the keymaps in normal state. describe-command lists a passthrough binding as "Insert on a surface", so Cmd-V doesn't read as bound in text's insert state. Faint "keeps 65 % of the foreground" (it said "65 % toward the background", in 746 too: the formula's right). using-die.md's Cmd-V row drops `:surface-paste` with `"a`, which no key can do. The landed commit's body keeps its "65 % toward".
795. Budget: die-core from 15,160 to 15,235, about 1,235 over guard 7's "about 14k", 75 of it this review's. Ext, dispatch and rt from 1,600 to 1,615 (789, 793), the command line's from 1,390 to 1,395 (792), surfaces and PTYs from 1,285 to 1,300, session from 1,490 to 1,520 (`sys::lang`), die-render's `platform::macos` from 1,315 to 1,335 (787's sweep).
796. Open: the leader, `C-w`, `:` and `"` (for 792) in a terminal's normal state wait for die-term's text to reach copy state (775), and a test of them with die::start then. 793 measures time between batches, so one batch a surface feeds slowly still takes what it takes (786). The sweep has no Linux half until `platform::linux`. On screen, 793's rate and the first terminal's 3 s wait for the login PATH (760) are unmeasured.

**The terminal, connected**

797. A terminal opens with `term` (also `:term`, `:terminal`, `M-x term`): your `$SHELL` as a login shell (`-zsh`), in the buffer's directory, in this window, in insert state. The first is `*terminal*`, the next `*terminal*<2>`. `term-switch` goes to `*terminal*` or opens it, as `ghostel` does. die-dev binds them where init.el has them, `\e` (your `termin-open`) and `\g` (`ghostel`); neither clashes with a DIE key. Your Emacs's vterm, ghostel and termin all set `/bin/bash`, and termin names `term1`, `term2`. DIE runs `$SHELL` and names `*terminal*` as asked; `Terminals::with_shell` in die-dev changes the shell. Veto if you'd rather have bash.
798. Insert state is core's passthrough. Every key but the DIE layer goes through the engine's encoder to the PTY, `\`, C-c, C-z, C-d, C-r, C-k, C-h, C-g and Tab included. A Cmd chord nothing binds goes nowhere, as D18 says every key but the DIE layer's goes to the program; core's call 750 hands them to the surface, and die-term drops them. A key brings back the live screen.
799. `takes_esc()` is evil-ghostel's three routings: auto (the program on the alternate screen), always the program, always normal state. `term-toggle-send-escape`, on `C-c C-r` in normal state and in `M-x`, toggles as `evil-ghostel-toggle-send-escape` does: from auto to whichever way auto doesn't send Esc now, and from either back to auto. A count of 1, 2 or 3 picks one, as `3 C-c C-r` does (830 corrects this call, which said no key passed one). The first Esc that goes to a program on its alternate screen says so once a run, as evil-ghostel's hint does.
800. Normal state is copy state (761). The kind `term`'s keys come before die-vim's text keys: Esc runs `term-send-escape`, Esc through the encoder, so a program with the kitty flags gets its CSI u; `i a I A` run `term-insert`; `p`, `P` and Cmd-V run core's `surface-paste`, which pastes the clipboard, or `"` when it holds no text, as die-vim's `p` sees the clipboard first; and `C-c C-r` toggles. Everything else is die-vim's, `:` and `C-w` included.
801. die-term's surface gives copy state its text through `CopyText` (`text` and `scroll_to`), and says `surface_text_changed` after each batch of output and each resize, as call 775 asked. Core calls `scroll_to(None)` before every render outside copy state, and `CopyText` takes None as the live screen, so insert state's wheel was undone at each frame. die-term moves to the live screen once, when copy state ends, and otherwise leaves insert's viewport where the wheel put it. A viewport copy state moved draws as a full frame, which the engine reports by itself.
802. The cursor is the program's, where it put it, and shown or hidden as it says, in insert. Its shape is evil-ghostel's: a bar on the main screen, so insert and normal look apart now that Esc moves between them there, and the program's shape on the alternate screen. In normal state core draws copy state's cursor. The brief said "the program's" cursor in insert; veto to keep the program's shape on the main screen too.
803. A surface's `status()` now stands in for the buffer's status text, where call 748 put it after. Your status text is made from the buffer's name and file, so a terminal's line showed the directory DIE started in. die-term's is the terminal's directory, as your mode line shows `default-directory` (`~` and a final `/`), then the program's title, or the buffer's name while there's none. Evil's tag stays at the right. Contract §10 says so.
804. One terminal has one size, whichever windows show it (C10): the smallest columns and rows among the split tree's windows showing it, as Emacs's default `window-adjust-process-window-size-smallest` gives a process. A larger window shows the grid at its top left. It's worked out at each `Resize` and focus change; a window that stops showing it with neither leaves the size until the next. Cells and pixels both go to `Term::resize` and the PTY's TIOCSWINSZ.
805. Output is parsed for at most 4 ms a call, as call 786 asks. `on_output` adds core's batch (at most 256 KiB, call 753) to what's waiting, and `Term::feed` takes it a slice at a time until 4 ms have gone; the rest waits for a timer due at once, which core runs after the next key or frame, as it runs timers (contract §12). Core's own 4 ms of batches a frame (793) counts after each call, so a frame's output takes about 8 ms at most. An ordinary flood's 256 KiB takes about 1 ms, so it all goes in its turn. More than 8 MiB waiting is parsed past the 4 ms, so crafted output, which can come faster than it parses, can't grow it without end. The engine's replies go to the PTY after each turn's parse. `yes | head -c 50000000` took 16.7 s: the Mac's PTY is the limit, since `script -q /dev/null sh -c 'yes | head -c 50000000'` alone takes 16.8 s.
806. The pointer goes to the mouse encoder while the program tracks the mouse: presses, releases, drags and motion, in the area's physical pixels. The wheel goes through `Term::wheel` (call 738), with a trackpad's part rows added up. A click on an untracked screen in insert does nothing, and neither does scope §6's Shift-click; in normal state the pointer is copy state's (770).
807. A paste goes through `Term::paste`, bracketed if the program asked. One the engine refuses, a line break or control code outside a bracketed paste, asks "Paste it? (y or n)" first, as Ghostty's `clipboard-paste-protection` does.
808. OSC 52 writes are a kill, as Emacs's `kill-new` makes them: die-vim's ring push, now public as `kill_new`, moves the ring down and puts the text in `"` and on the clipboard, so `p` in a text buffer pastes it. The facade passes it to die-term; die-term alone writes only the clipboard. A write to the primary selection goes there too, since the Mac has one clipboard. OSC 7 sets the buffer's directory when it names a directory here, from any host. OSC 9 and 777 notifications go to the messages. The bell does nothing.
809. The buffer closes when the program exits (scope §6). An exit that isn't 0 says "*terminal*: zsh exited with 3" or "was killed by signal 9" in the messages.
810. Terminals don't survive a restart yet. Quit, `:restart` and `:rebuild` close every PTY at shutdown (call 754): a hangup, which the shell passes to its jobs, then a kill 300 ms later of the shell's own process group, so a job that ignores the hangup (`nohup`) goes on, as after closing a Terminal window (830 corrects this call, which said a shell's jobs all go). The session keeps no surface, and a window that showed one isn't restored. Cmd-Q, `restart` and `rebuild` ask "A program is running in *terminal*. Quit anyway? (y or n)" while a terminal's foreground job isn't its shell, as D18 asks; a shell at its prompt goes without asking. `rebuild` asks before it builds. `:qa` and `:q` in the last window don't ask, since die-vim's quit doesn't know die-term.
811. Only macOS has the engine archive, so the facade depends on die-term on macOS alone (D10, D12); a Linux binary that registered it wouldn't link. Elsewhere `term`, `terminal` and `term-switch` say there are no terminals yet, so your config's `\e` and `\g` still start. `die::built_in()` gives die-vim and die-term, as `start` registers them, for tests and configs. die-term's own code stays neutral. Both Linux targets cross-check.
812. `Terminals::init` warms the login shell's PATH on a thread of its own, so the first terminal doesn't wait up to 3 s for it (call 760's open item).
813. Budget: die-term's new modules, surface at 380 of 400 and commands at 295 of 320; die-term is 1,923 of 5,000. die-vim from 10,390 to 10,391, its size, for `kill_new`. die-core is unchanged: the status line's change is no longer than what it replaced.
814. Tests: 14 real-PTY tests in `crates/die-term/tests/terminal.rs`, without a harness, with die-vim registered as DIE has it: `echo hi-$((40+2))` reaches the cells, with the status line and `pwd`; Esc on the main screen goes to normal, Esc there reaches `cat -v`, and `i a I A` return; Esc reaches `vim -u NONE`, which `:q` then quits, and `less`; Cmd-Esc leaves vim; the toggle both ways and `C-c C-r`; `stty size` after a resize and a split, `exit` closing the buffer, and `exit 3` said; a bracketed paste and an unbracketed one confirmed; the flood; 300,000 DECALNs, whose slowest frame was 101 ms without the turn's 4 ms and is under 10 ms with it; the wheel and a key back to the bottom; `gg`, `/`, `yy` and `G` over the scrollback, `x` refused, and `i` back with no snapshot; SGR mouse reports; OSC 52, 7 and 2; `term`, `terminal` and `term-switch`; and `shutdown` leaving neither the shell nor its job running, with no terminal in the restored session. 3 in `crates/die/tests/terminals.rs`, also without a harness: Cmd-Q asks while `sleep` runs and not at a prompt, OSC 52 is a kill `p` pastes, and `C-w v`, `C-w l` and `:q` in normal state. No app test: nothing drives keys into an unseen DIE.app yet.
815. Measured during the 50 MB flood, a frame being the host's turns then its scene, at load 5 to 6: in insert, 66 µs a frame and at most 0.23 ms. Esc ran in the first frame after it was typed, 27 ms later, most of it copy state's first read of 10,000 rows. In normal state, following the flood took 9.2 ms a frame, at most 10.5 ms, since each frame reads what's new (copy state's cost, for its package). `i` ran in the first frame, 10 ms. C-c stopped `yes` with the prompt back in 0.11 ms. Under 300,000 DECALNs, 2.2 ms a frame, at most 8.8 ms, and Esc ran in the first frame, 9.4 ms.
816. Open: D20's holder and helper, so terminals survive restarts. Following a flood in normal state costs about 9 ms a frame (815). Cmd-C, selecting in insert state, kitty images, OSC 8, shell integration (bash 3.2's, scope §6) and a terminal on Linux wait. Not tried in your DIE.app: Claude Code, htop with the mouse, and a Retina window's pixel sizes, which the tests give as 8 × 16.

**Copy state on the real terminal**

817. The terminal package wired copy state into die-term's surface (801), which was 775's first open item. Real-PTY tests now cover it. Three are in `crates/die-term/tests/terminal.rs`:
     - `seq 1 20000` through `/bin/sh`, then Esc, `gg` (the scrollback's first line on the window's top row), `/19999<CR>` (the cursor's row shows 19999), `yy`, `G` (the live screen) and `k0vey`
     - output from `sleep 0.3; echo` followed in normal state, frozen after `k` with the cells pinned where they were, and caught up by `G`
     - `printf`'s red unchanged by Esc and kept under the selection, with six edit keys refused
     One in `crates/die/tests/terminals.rs`, with `die::start`, checks your leader (bound globally, as your config binds it), then `"ayiw` and `"ap` into the program. That's the test 796 left open.
818. Timing on the real terminal: Esc over 9,955 lines of `seq` output takes 14.0 to 15.5 ms with its frame, in six runs at load 5 to 15. Following a flood still costs about 9 ms a frame (815), since each frame reads the new lines, about 1 µs a line. I left that as it is: reading less often would leave the window behind the live screen while it follows.
819. Open, besides 775's other items: when two windows share a terminal at the smaller one's size (804), copy state in the taller one can put the cursor on rows below the grid, which show nothing.

**The terminal's review, fixed**

820. A review of the terminal (797–819) found 1 major and 8 minor findings. I checked each with the reviewer's probes on a real PTY before fixing it, and all held, though `3"ap` turned out to be your Evil's own behaviour (827). Eight are fixed, each with a test that fails with its fix taken out; I took them out one at a time to check. The ninth, docs and a test that claimed more than they showed, is corrected (830).
821. A window that went up in normal state has its snapshot read as output comes, as a following one does; only the window holds still. Before, its text stayed as it was while the engine drew rows by their numbers, so once the scrollback lost lines, to a flood past 10,000 or to macOS's `clear`, which empties it, the window drew rows other than the ones `yy`, `/` and the cursor used: after `50k` and `clear`, row 0 drew `fresh-25` and yanked `240`. Each update now moves a frozen window's lines up by those that left the start, so the rows it draws are its lines. This replaces 764's frozen text, not its frozen window, and settles 775's items on pruned rows and screen rows changing under a frozen window, in normal state. Visual, operator-pending state and a prompt still hold the text until they end. A frozen window during a flood now costs what a following one does, about 9 ms a frame (815).
822. A prompt puts back the state of the buffer it was opened over, if that's still live, and touches no other. Before, it put its saved state on whichever buffer had the focus at the answer, so a terminal whose program ended under "Paste it?" left `*scratch*` in passthrough, where keys went nowhere and nothing said why. I didn't take the review's other idea, refusing passthrough on a text buffer: core's and die-vim's tests use one as a stand-in terminal (`open_target` leaves it alone), and the contract allows it.
823. A prompt opened from passthrough, as die-term's paste question and the quit question, leaves the terminal out of copy state: the program's output goes on showing, and the scrollback isn't read. Before, the prompt's CommandLine state took a snapshot, a whole read, and pinned the window to it until the answer. `:` and `/` from normal state are over the snapshot, as before. Core now opens the prompt before it sets CommandLine, so copy state can tell.
824. A repeat or release reaches a surface in passthrough only after its press did, matched by the physical key (`KeyEvent::code`), or by the key for keys from notation, which have none. A key's latest press says where its release goes, and the last 32 presses are kept. With kitty's flags 11, Esc then Shift-A, Shift let go first, sent the program `^[[97;2:3u`, the release of an `a` it never saw pressed (winit makes a release's key from the modifiers then), and `^[[57441;1:3u`, Shift's; now neither goes. This replaces 789's swallowing of keys that entered passthrough. The swallow list stays for DIE-layer chords and Esc, whose repeats mustn't reach normal state's keymaps.
825. Esc after the wheel scrolled insert's view back starts copy state there: the window shows the same rows, with the cursor on its last row in the terminal cursor's column, as Vim keeps the cursor on a window it scrolls. `SurfaceText` has `shown_top` for it, which die-term fills from its viewport. Before, Esc jumped to the prompt, and the place had to be found again.
826. `G` in a terminal's normal state goes to the terminal's cursor, as evil-ghostel's `evil-ghostel-goto-cursor` does, so the cursor then follows it exactly. It runs die-vim's `G` first, for the jump list, then moves to `Ctx::surface_cursor`, which is new. With a count it's die-vim's line; evil-ghostel ignores the count on the main screen, so veto if you'd rather match that. On the alternate screen it's die-vim's `G`, as there. Visual and operator-pending `G` stay die-vim's, since evil-ghostel binds its own in normal state alone.
827. `p` and `P` take a count: `surface-paste` sends the text that many times, as one piece, as evil-ghostel's `p` pastes it n times, and refuses past 64 MiB. `"a3p` pastes three times. `3"ap` pastes once, as in your Evil, whose `"` (`evil-use-register`) uses up the count before `p` runs; die-vim does the same. The review's case was that one.
828. `:term cmd` runs `cmd` through your shell (`$SHELL -c cmd`, not a login shell), as Vim's `:terminal cmd` does, in a buffer named as `term`'s are, which closes when it ends. Before, the argument was dropped and a plain shell opened. While it runs it's busy, so Cmd-Q asks.
829. A login zsh reports its directory (OSC 7) at each prompt, so the status line, `gf` on a relative path such as cargo's `src/x.rs:12:5`, and `\f` follow `cd`. macOS's `/etc/zshrc` reports only to Apple's Terminal, and your `~/.zshrc` doesn't, so a terminal stayed at its start directory. die-term starts it with `ZDOTDIR` at a `.zshenv` of its own, as ghostel and Ghostty do: it puts your `ZDOTDIR` back, or unsets it, sources your `.zshenv`, and in an interactive shell adds a `precmd` hook that prints OSC 7, with `%` escaped. zsh then reads your `.zprofile`, `.zshrc` and `.zlogin` as ever, and its programs see your `ZDOTDIR`. The file is `$TMPDIR/die-term-zsh/.zshenv`, written at each terminal's start and used only if the directory is yours and no one else can write it; without `$TMPDIR` zsh starts as before. A `.zshrc` that assigns `precmd_functions` outright drops the hook. bash and sh still don't report: scope §6's bash 3.2 integration waits. Veto if you'd rather DIE left zsh's startup alone; the cost is a `cd` that the status line and `gf` don't see.
830. Docs and a test that said more than was done. Call 799 said nothing passes a count to `term-toggle-send-escape`, but `3 C-c C-r` picks normal state and `2 C-c C-r` the program; 799 and using-die.md say so now. Call 810 and using-die.md said a restart leaves nothing running, but the kill 300 ms later reaches only the shell's own process group, and a job that ignores the hangup the shell passes on (`nohup`, `trap '' HUP`) goes on, as after closing a Terminal window; they say that now. The less half of `esc_reaches_vim_and_less_on_the_alternate_screen` sent a second `q` if the first didn't quit, so it passed whether or not less had the Esc. Now the first `q` must not quit, since less reads Esc q as one command, and a program on the alternate screen must read the Esc as the byte `1b` (`dd | od`).
831. Budget: die-core from 15,235 to 15,280 (15,277 now), about 1,280 over guard 7's "about 14k": ext, dispatch and rt from 1,615 to 1,635 (824), the command line's from 1,395 to 1,405 (822, 823), and surfaces and PTYs from 1,300 to 1,320 (821, 825–827). die-term's commands from 320 to 330 (826, 828), and a new module, shell integration, at 49 of 50 (829). die-term is 2,001 of 5,000.
832. Tests. die-core's `surface.rs`: a release matched by physical key, Shift-A let go as `a` and Shift's own, with `I`'s repeat; a prompt putting back its own buffer's state after the focus moved and after its terminal went; `3p` as three copies, and the 64 MiB refusal. die-vim's `copy.rs`: a prompt from insert leaving the live view unread; a frozen cursor's row drawing the line `yy` takes after the oldest lines go; the frozen test now expects the text read and the window held, as die-term's `copy.rs` does. die-term's `terminal.rs`, on real `/bin/sh` and `/bin/zsh`: `50k` then `clear`, and `gg` then a flood past the scrollback, with every drawn row its snapshot line and `yy` taking what's drawn; the wheel then Esc keeping the rows; `G` on the terminal's cursor after `gg0`, then following it, and `3G`; `"a3p` reaching `cat -v` as `ABABAB`; `:term printf …; sleep 5` through `/bin/sh`, busy; zsh's startup files read in order with your `ZDOTDIR` back, `cd /usr/bin` on the status line, and no `ZDOTDIR` left when you had none; and 830's checks.
833. Open: in visual or operator-pending state, or under a prompt from normal state, a `clear` or a flood past the scrollback still draws rows other than the snapshot's until the state ends (821). After the engine numbers its rows afresh (a reset, or crafted output that pushes out the whole scrollback in one slice), a frozen window keeps its line numbers in the new text: an arbitrary place, but drawn as it's yanked. bash's directory (829).

**Esc in a terminal's normal state, and your shell**

834. What you hit: "Display all 1773 possibilities? (y or n)", twice, after pressing Esc a few times in a terminal. DIE had started your login zsh (`$SHELL`, call 797), you'd typed `bash`, and each Esc in normal state sent bash an Esc (800). bash 3.2 reads Esc Esc as `complete`, which lists every command on the PATH the second time, and with no key timeout a lone Esc makes the next key Meta, so `Esc Esc i l s` reached it as `M-l` (downcase-word) and `s`. zsh's line editor takes Esc as a prefix too. Claude Code reads one Esc as interrupt and a quick second as rewind, so pressing Esc a few times did more there than you meant.
835. Esc in normal state sends nothing while the shell `term` started is at its prompt, which is when it's the PTY's foreground group (`TermSurface::busy`, as Cmd-Q asks), as Evil's Esc does nothing in normal state. A program in the foreground, Claude Code, `cat` or a nested program, still gets it. A shell typed inside the terminal, as `bash` at zsh's prompt, is such a program, so it still gets Esc; telling it apart needs the foreground process's name, which I left. A count always sends that many, at the prompt too.
836. **For veto:** Esc in normal state sends one Esc, then nothing until another key: more Escs with no key between them send nothing, so hammering Esc to be sure of normal state sends exactly one. Any other key in normal state (a motion, a count, `v`, `/`, `:`) re-arms it, and so does going back through insert. The Esc that leaves insert, and one that only ends something (visual, an operator, a prompt, `g`), aren't the sent one, and a held Esc's repeats send nothing more. So from insert, Esc Esc still sends one, which interrupts Claude Code, Esc ×4 sends one too, and `2<Esc>` sends two, its rewind. This departs from your `vterm-send-escape`, which sends every Esc; veto to send every Esc again, and 835 alone still keeps them from the shell.
837. How die-term tells another key came: core's `Ctx::observe_keys`, which sees every key outside passthrough before dispatch, as `q` recording does. die-term counts the keys other than a plain Esc, and each terminal keeps the count at its last send. No core change. Insert state's keys don't reach observers, but going back to insert from normal takes one that does (`i a I A`), and a new terminal has sent nothing.
838. **For veto (reverses 797 for die-dev):** your config's `term` starts `/bin/bash`, as init.el's `ghostel-shell` and `vterm-shell` set, interactive and not a login shell, as vterm starts it (termin, your `\e`, runs vterm), so it reads `~/.bashrc`: your prompt, PATH additions and vterm's hook, which checks `INSIDE_EMACS`. A login bash reads `~/.profile` and never `~/.bashrc`. ghostel on macOS wraps its shell as a login shell by default (`ghostel-macos-login-shell`); I followed vterm. The environment keeps the login PATH DIE works out (760), and your `.bashrc` adds to it. Every other config still gets `$SHELL` as a login shell.
839. A config chooses the shell with `Terminals::set_shell(path, login)`, from its startup hook through `Ctx::with_ext`, as init.el's `setq` does. Core and the facade have no extension options yet, so there's no new config field; contract §17's sketch has `die_term options: { shell: /bin/bash }`, and this is it until they come. die-dev's hook is macOS-only, as terminals are (D10). `Spec` has `shell` (the terminal's shell: busy only while a job runs, and 835's rule) apart from `login` (`argv[0]` is `-bash`), so a bash that isn't a login shell still counts as the shell for the quit and restart questions.
840. `:term cmd` runs through the same shell, `/bin/bash -c cmd` in die-dev and `$SHELL -c cmd` by default, and isn't the terminal's shell, so it's busy and Esc reaches it, under 836. Veto for `$SHELL -c` whatever the config's shell.
841. bash reports `cd`, scope §6's bash 3.2 integration (833's open item). The terminal's bash, not a login shell and with no arguments, starts with `--rcfile` at `$TMPDIR/die-term-bash/bashrc`, written at each start and used only if the directory is yours and nobody else can write it, as zsh's `.zshenv` is (829); otherwise bash starts plainly. The rc sources `~/.bashrc` if it's readable, as bash would have, then defines `__die_osc7`, which prints OSC 7 `file://$HOSTNAME$PWD` with `%` escaped and returns the `$?` it was given, and adds it on a line after your `PROMPT_COMMAND`: a string in bash 3.2, or the array's first element in 5.1 and later, whose other elements stay. bash 3.2 itself restores `$?` after `PROMPT_COMMAND`; the hook keeps it for anything added after it. Scope §6 had planned an rc that reads bash's login files and then `~/.bashrc`; your vterm's bash reads only `~/.bashrc`, so DIE's does, and §6 says so now. A login bash ignores `--rcfile`, so it doesn't report yet; that takes Ghostty's `--posix` and `ENV` route.
842. Budget: die-term's commands from 330 to 360 (355 now: 835 to 840) and shell integration from 50 to 75 (74: 841). die-term is 2,062 of 5,000. die-core, die-vim and die are unchanged.
843. Tests on real PTYs, each shell with a HOME and a ZDOTDIR of its own. In die-term's `terminal.rs`: at a `/bin/bash` and a `/bin/zsh` prompt, Esc ×4, half a second, `i`, then `lx=ok; echo got-$lx` prints `got-ok`, with no "Display all" and no "not found"; `4<Esc>` at bash's prompt does bring up "Display all".
