A raw reader in the foreground (`stty -icanon`, then `dd | od` up to an `x`) gets exactly one `1b` from Esc ×4 after insert, one from Esc Esc, one from Esc Esc and three held repeats, two from `<Esc>2<Esc><Esc>`, and two from `<Esc><Esc>k<Esc><Esc>`. Both fail with the old command, which sent every Esc, and the reader's fails with the key count taken out. die-dev's new `terminal.rs`, in a process of its own: `\e` starts `/bin/bash` (`$0` is `/bin/bash`, and `login_shell` is off), the temp HOME's `.bashrc` prompt shows, Esc ×4 then `i` and `lx=ok; echo got-$lx` gives `got-ok`, `cd /usr/bin` puts ` /usr/bin/  *terminal*` on the status line, the `.bashrc`'s own `PROMPT_COMMAND` prints `mine-3` after `(exit 3)`, `$?` is 3 at the next line, and a `PROMPT_COMMAND` added after DIE's hook sees `4` after `(exit 4)`.
844. Open: a shell nested in the terminal gets normal state's Esc (835). A login bash doesn't report `cd` (841). Only keys re-arm 836's Esc, so a click between two Escs doesn't.

**Esc in a terminal's normal state, after its review**

845. Two reviews of 834–844 found 13 things. I reproduced or read each, and fixed 10 of them (846–853) and the two doc slips (854). Two are left (855). Three were there before 834 and came to matter with it: Ctrl-[ in insert, the foreground group's 0, and the missing IUTF8.
846. **For veto:** normal state's Esc sends nothing while the terminal is in line mode (ICANON), as a shell leaves it for a job that reads no keys: `sleep`, a `cargo build`, `cat`. There an Esc only echoed `^[` and waited in the line, and the prompt read it as Meta, so after `sleep 2`, Esc Esc, `iecho hi` ran `cho hi`. That was your habit's case: Esc to scroll back through a build, then type. Core's new `Pty::line_mode` reads ICANON as `echoes` reads ECHO. Programs that read keys turn line mode off (Claude Code, vim, less, ssh, readline), so they still get the Esc. Your `vterm-send-escape` sends it either way.
847. **For veto (reverses 835's count):** a count goes only where a lone Esc would, so never to the shell at its prompt or in line mode, and it sends at most two. `<Esc>5<Esc>`, Vim's reflex for dropping a stray count, sent bash five, which listed every command and made the next key Meta. `99999999999<Esc>` would have built about 100 GB to write. `2<Esc>` is still Claude Code's rewind. Your `vterm-send-escape` ignores the count and sends one; veto for that, or for Vim's cancel. After a register (`"a<Esc>`) Esc only ends the prefix, as in Vim.
848. **For veto:** Ctrl-[ is Esc in a terminal too, as `C-[` is ESC in your Emacs. The key count goes by `KeyEvent::chord`, as a keymap reads keys, so Ctrl-[ three times in normal state sends one Esc, not three. In insert, core's passthrough leaves for normal state on a bare Ctrl-[ as on a plain Esc, where Ctrl-[ ×4 at bash's prompt brought up "Display all". On the alternate screen, or with the toggle, it goes to the program, as Esc does, and the hint counts it. Contract §7 now says the raw event keeps the difference only there.
849. `Pty::foreground_group` reads the terminal's session first (`tcgetsid`) and is None without one, and `TermSurface::busy` asks for the pid first. macOS reads a PTY's foreground group as 0 while it has no session, from the spawn until the trampoline's `TIOCSCTTY` and again after the shell ends, and rustix 1.1.5 made a `Pid` of that 0: a panic in a test or dev build, undefined behaviour in a release one. An Esc pressed just after `\e` hit it. A probe read 0 in 28,991 of 29,113 reads after a bash exited, and none in 368,000 over 300 jobs. One race is left: an exit between the two reads still gives the 0. Closing it takes a raw `tcgetpgrp`, which is `unsafe` and so forbidden in core (§12), or rustix mapping the 0 to an error, as it already does on Linux.
850. bash's hook goes into `PROMPT_COMMAND` as `__die_osc7 2>/dev/null`. A bash started inside the terminal inherits an exported `PROMPT_COMMAND` but not the function, and it printed "__die_osc7: command not found" at every prompt; now it says nothing. The redirect doesn't change `$?`. On Linux, a bash 4 or later with a `command_not_found_handle` would run that handler at each nested prompt, with its output hidden.
851. Both hooks escape `%` and control characters as `%XX` in OSC 7. bash 3.2 walks the name only when it holds one (`case` on `[%[:cntrl:]]`, then `printf -v`); zsh does it in one substitution (`(#m)` and `[##16]`). Before, a BEL in a name ended the OSC early and printed the rest on the screen, and a line break or a tab left the directory where it was.
852. DIE's rc directories under `$TMPDIR` are created 0700, and one that's yours but writable by others is made 0700. Under a umask of 002 the directory came out group-writable, was refused, and stayed refused for good. Each rc is written to a new file (`create_new`, so a link planted there isn't followed) and renamed into place, and written again once the directory is private. Before, the write went first and followed a planted `bashrc.<pid>` link, overwriting its target.
853. A PTY starts with IUTF8, as vterm runs `stty ... iutf8` before it execs your bash. Backspace over `é` in a line read (`read`, `head`, `cat`) took one of its two bytes and left a broken one. Readline's own editing was never affected. This predates 834.
854. Docs. 842's counts were off (shell integration was at 74, not 72, and die-term at 2,062, not 2,060); 842 is corrected. using-die.md said the bash hook goes after your `PROMPT_COMMAND`, but for bash 5.1's array it goes after the first element, and it now says so. It also now says that a nested shell reads the next key after Esc as Meta, that a program in the shell's process group doesn't get normal state's Esc, and that Ctrl-[ is Esc. Scope D18 and §5.5 and contract §7 and §12 match 846–849 and 853.
855. Not fixed, since each needs the foreground process's name or prompt marks (OSC 133) from DIE's hooks, which is a design for you. A program in the shell's own process group, such as `fzf` in `cd $(fzf --height 40%)` or a program run with `exec`, looks like the shell at its prompt: normal state's Esc doesn't reach it (`C-c C-r` sends insert's Esc), and Cmd-Q doesn't ask about it. A shell or a REPL started inside the terminal still gets one Esc, and its line editor reads the next key as Meta (835). die-dev starts `/bin/bash` itself now, so that only happens if you type `bash` from habit.
856. Tests, each failing without its fix. In die-term's `terminal.rs`, on real PTYs with HOMEs of their own: in bash and zsh, `<Esc><Esc>2<Esc>` during `sleep 1`, then `lx=ok; echo got-$lx` at the prompt prints `got-ok`; at each prompt `<Esc>5<Esc>3<Esc><Esc>"a<Esc>` lists nothing and eats no key; the raw reader gets one `1b` from Esc then Ctrl-[ ×3, two from `<Esc>9<Esc>` and none from `<Esc>"a<Esc>`; `cat -v` gets no `^[` from normal state, and Ctrl-[ leaves insert; a nested bash under an exported `PROMPT_COMMAND` says nothing of `__die_osc7`; `busy` asked in a loop from the moment a terminal opens doesn't panic (with pid-first alone and no `tcgetsid` it panicked 3 times in 3); bash and zsh report a directory named `nl\nt\tbel\apc%esc\ex` with none of it on the screen; and `head -n 1 | od` reads `61 0a` from `é<BS>a`. In `shell.rs`'s unit tests, a group-writable directory of yours is made 0700 and used, and a link planted at the temp name is removed, not written through. Three older tests are fixed too: the alternate-screen reader is a script, since its `$(dd …)` ran in the shell's group and "never busy" flaked; `a_flood_doesnt_hold_up_a_key` types `yes` without waiting for idle, which a flood never reaches, where it could run 17 minutes; and `slow_output_holds_a_turn_for_a_few_ms` takes turns as the host does, since idle came only once all was parsed, with `cat` long gone, so it failed "never busy" every time, and it now measures frames while the parse goes on.
857. Budget: die-core from 15,280 to 15,295 (15,289 now), its surfaces and PTYs from 1,320 to 1,335 (1,328: 846, 849, 853), and die-term's shell integration from 75 to 115 (110: 850–852). die-term is 2,107 of 5,000, and its commands are 358 of 360.
858. Open: the race left in 849. 855's two cases. Only keys re-arm the Esc (844). Claude Code's interrupt and rewind under 846 and 847, and your real `~/.bashrc`, are untried in your DIE.app.

**Esc in insert goes to normal state on the alternate screen too**

859. What you hit: "when im running somehting liek claude code in the terminal, in DIE, if I hit escape, it doesnt put me into normal mode. The escape gets captured by the claude code session". Your Claude Code (2.1.319) has a fullscreen mode on the alternate screen, and you run btop. The exception was ours, evil-ghostel's `auto` (732, 799), and not in what you asked for in 732. Insert's Esc now always goes to normal state, on the alternate screen too: `Escape::Normal` is the default, so `takes_esc()` says no. Cmd-Esc and Ctrl-[ are as before, and Shift-, Ctrl- and Alt-Esc still go to the program. So Esc Esc from insert interrupts a fullscreen Claude Code and leaves vim's insert mode: the first Esc leaves insert, and normal state's sends one, under 836, 846 and 847. Not for veto: you asked for it. This reverses 799's default; D18, contract §7 and using-die.md say so.
860. The toggle: plain `C-c C-r` goes from normal state, the default, to the program, for vim run in the terminal, and from the program back. A count picks one as before: `1` is evil-ghostel's auto, kept as an option, `2` the program, `3` normal state. **For veto:** from auto, plain `C-c C-r` goes back to normal state rather than on to the program, as evil-ghostel's goes from either other routing back to its default. The messages are as before ("Esc in insert: to normal state", "to the program", "auto (to the program now)"). The first-time hint on the alternate screen went with the rule it explained.
861. Normal state on the alternate screen is copy state over that screen, which has no scrollback, as it already was after Cmd-Esc (773's "the alternate screen's rows alone"); nothing changed there. A program that tracks the mouse doesn't hear normal state's clicks, which are copy state's.
862. **For veto:** normal state's Esc sends its release too, which the encoder writes only when the program's kitty flags ask for releases (flag 2). With legacy encoding or flag 1 it's still one `1b` or `CSI 27 u`. Under flags 31 a program got `CSI 27 u` and never `CSI 27;1:3 u`, since the real release comes in normal state, where keymaps send none, so a program that tracks held keys would have thought Esc stayed down. A count of 2 sends press, release, press, release. A program that reads releases gets every key's in insert already, so this is no new kind of input to it.
863. Tests, on real PTYs with HOMEs of their own, in die-term's `terminal.rs`. `esc_on_the_alternate_screen_goes_to_normal_and_esc_esc_reaches_vim_and_less`, which was `esc_reaches_vim_and_less_on_the_alternate_screen`: `i<Esc>` on `vim -u NONE`'s screen goes to normal, whose snapshot is vim's screen without the shell's line, and Esc, `i` and `:q<CR>` then quit vim, so it had the Esc; less reads Esc q as one command; the raw reader gets `1b 78` from Esc, Esc, `i`, `x`; Cmd-Esc leaves; and there's no hint. `the_toggle_sends_insert_esc_to_the_program_and_a_count_picks_auto`, which was `the_toggle_sends_esc_to_the_program_or_to_normal_state`: normal state by default; the toggle sends insert's Esc to `cat -v` (`^[y`) and to vim, whose `:q` then quits; `1 C-c C-r` sends it to vim on its screen and to normal state on the main one; from auto, `C-c C-r` is back at normal; `2`, `3` and `1` pick, and `4` is refused. New, `a_fullscreen_program_loses_esc_to_normal_state_and_gets_one_from_the_next`: a script enters the alternate screen, pushes `CSI > 31 u`, turns on SGR mouse tracking (1000, 1006) and bracketed paste, goes raw and logs every byte with `dd bs=1`. Insert's Esc and its release reach it as nothing, normal state's snapshot is its screen without `seq 1 30` under it, a click sends nothing, the next Esc logs exactly `CSI 27 u CSI 27;1:3 u`, two more log nothing, and `i<Esc><Esc>` logs one more. Each fails with its change taken out: the old default fails all three, no release fails the last, and no normal-state Esc on the alternate screen fails the first at `:q`.

**A terminal's margins: `\z` and the centered width**

866. What you hit: "\z doesnt let me add margins in the terminal? it should work the same as any other buffer for providing the side margins". A surface drew over its window's whole area (746), and `toggle-max-width` changed text settings only: in a terminal's normal state it reached copy state's snapshot, which draws nothing of its own, and in insert it said the buffer isn't text.
867. **For veto:** a surface's area is a text window's text area with no gutter: `min(max_width, cols)` columns at the same `pad`, on every row, with blank margins in the default face and the status line still edge to edge. A surface buffer has settings of its own, from the same `text_defaults` a text buffer starts with, and only their `max_width` and `center` mean anything there. So in your config a terminal opens 80 columns wide and centered, as a file does; in an 80-column window that's the whole window, as before. A config with no maximum width keeps the whole area. This reverses 746's "no gutter, margins or centered width". Veto for terminals that open at the window's width, with `\z` still narrowing them.
868. `Ctx::settings(buf)` reads a buffer's settings, a surface buffer's own rather than its snapshot's, and `Ctx::change_settings(buf, f)` changes them. `toggle-max-width` goes through it, so it works on a surface in insert and normal state, and while the snapshot is up. A change of `max_width` or `center` sends each window showing the buffer a `Resize` with its area, a change of `center` alone too, at the same size, and the snapshot takes the new width. `Ctx::surface_area(w)` gives the area as a `Geometry` (`gutter` 0), held inside the window, and `Settings::place(cols, rows, gutter)` is the one rule it and `TextBuf::geometry` share. `text_mut(buf)?.settings_mut()` on a terminal in normal state still reaches the snapshot's own settings, which aren't the way to change its width.
869. Resize events, the frame, `cursor()`'s place and the pointer's cells and pixels are the area's. die-term's size rule (804) takes the smallest columns and rows among the areas, so TIOCSWINSZ and XTWINOPS answer the area too. A new terminal starts its engine and PTY at the area the focused window gives it, rather than at the window's width with a resize as it shows. `open_window` tells a surface its area only when that's narrower than the size its caller gave (751).
870. The snapshot has the surface buffer's `max_width` and `center` and is still `Wrap::Off`, so its text area is the surface's area: die-vim's cursor, the selection and the matches are drawn at the area's offset, and a click maps by the same cells. It never wraps, since its lines are the terminal's rows, at most the area wide. A toggle in normal state reads the whole text again after the surface hears its `Resize` (769), so the overlay lands on the reflowed cells.
871. The pointer: a press, motion or release over a margin doesn't reach the surface. In insert the press only focuses the window, as on a file's margin; in normal state it's a click in a file's margin, the row's start from the left one and its end from the right one. The wheel over a margin reaches the surface, held at the area's edge, as the wheel over a file's margin scrolls its text. A drag from a press in the area is held inside it (751).
872. The prompts and the echo area keep to a terminal's margins under a maximum width, as they do to a file's, from the same rule (`\z`'s row in using-die.md).
873. Budget: die-core from 15,295 to 15,385 (15,380 now): surfaces and PTYs from 1,335 to 1,380 (1,375: 868–871), workspace, windows and pointer from 1,365 to 1,395 (1,393: `settings`, `change_settings`, `open_window`'s Resize), layout from 960 to 965 (961: `place`, the toggle), ext, dispatch and rt from 1,635 to 1,640 (1,636: the settings in `Content`). No die-term limit moves: its commands are 353 of 360 with 869's start at the area, and die-term is 2,096 of 5,000.
874. Tests. die-core's `surface.rs`, with your config's 80 centered: a 120-column window shows an 80-column frame at pad 20, with blank default-face margins, the status line across, the cursor at the offset and a `Resize` of 80; `toggle-max-width` in insert gives 120 at pad 0 and a `Resize`, then 80 again; `change_settings` with `center` off draws at the left, and at 100 tells 100; the pointer at the area's offset, drags held at both edges, presses, moves and releases in the margins reaching nothing, and the wheel from a margin; copy state's cursor at the offset, a 100-column line not wrapping, margin clicks at the row's start and end, the selection drawn on columns 20–22, on 0–2 after the toggle, and `\z` in normal state back to 80; `open_window`'s narrower area; and two windows, 121 and 50 wide, told 80 at 20 and 50 at 0. The older tests run with line numbers and no maximum width, so a surface still has the whole window there. die-dev's `terminal.rs`, on a real PTY through your config in a 120-column frame: bash's prompt at column 20, `stty size` says `24 80`, then `24 120` after `\z` in normal state, then `24 80`. die-dev's `config.rs`: a surface in your config starts 80 wide and centered, at 20 in 120 columns, and `\z` widens and narrows it.
875. Open: a `Resize` when only `center` changes comes at the same size (868); die-term's fit ignores it, and copy state reads the whole text again for it. The wider of two windows on one terminal still shows the smaller grid at its area's top left, not centered in the area (804).

**Esc on the alternate screen: what its review found**

876. A review of 859–865 found 2 major and 3 minor things. I reproduced each with a test of its own on real PTYs before fixing it, and all five held. All are fixed (877–881), and each test fails with its fix taken out.
877. **For veto (reverses 810's last sentence):** `:q` in the last window, `:qa`, `ZQ`, and `:wq`, `:x`, `:wqa` and `:xa` once they've written, ask "A program is running in *terminal*. Quit anyway? (y or n)" as Cmd-Q does, and so do `:q!` and `:qa!`. Since 859, Esc on vim's screen leaves insert, so vim's own `:q` after one Esc was DIE's. In the last window it quit DIE and hung up every terminal without asking, and `:q!` dropped your unsaved files too, while `:wq`, `ZZ` and `:x` only said the buffer has no file name, and vim stayed in its insert mode. It's reachable: your `~/.bashrc` sets no `EDITOR` or `VISUAL` and git has no `core.editor`, so `git commit` in a terminal opens vi. `!` asks too, since it speaks for unsaved text, not for running programs. Your Evil's `:q!` is `kill-emacs`, which asks nothing, and Vim's `:q!` ends its terminal jobs, so that's the part to veto. The question comes after the refusal of unsaved text, so Cmd-Q no longer asks and then refuses. die-vim's `Vim::asking_before_quit` takes the question, and the facade's `built_in` gives it Cmd-Q's, so the `quit` command is `die_vim::quit_app` alone. using-die.md's Esc bullet now says that after one Esc, vim's `:wq` and `:q` are DIE's.
878. In normal state the wheel goes to a program that tracks the mouse, as in insert, and as your ghostel's `ghostel--scroll-intercept-map` sends it in every Evil state from `emulation-mode-map-alists`. Over Claude Code's fullscreen mode it did nothing after an Esc: the alternate screen has no scrollback for copy state to scroll, and the program heard nothing. Core's `Surface::takes_wheel()` is new and false by default. die-term's says yes while the program tracks the mouse, on either screen, since ghostel's rule doesn't look at the screen, so `fzf --height` gets it on the main screen too. Clicks and drags are still copy state's, and the program hears none (861). With a prompt open, the wheel is the prompt's, as before.
879. A key's release goes to the surface its press went to, whatever state the buffer is in by then, and even when another window has the focus. With kitty's flag 2, a letter rolled over into Esc, or the Ctrl of Ctrl-[, was pressed in insert and let go in normal state, where releases reached nothing, so the program heard it pressed and never let go. 862 fixed this only for normal state's Esc. Claude Code pushes `>1u` or `>5u`, by the reviewer's reading, and neither asks for releases, so it wasn't affected. A repeat still goes only where its press went, in passthrough (824). The contract's case of a Cmd pressed alone before Cmd-Esc is gone: its release goes where its press went too, and die-term sends no Super keys.
880. `C-c C-c`, `C-c C-z`, `C-c C-\` and `C-c C-d` in normal state send the program the bytes 03, 1a, 1c and 04 (`term-send-C-c` and the rest). Your ghostel's `ghostel-mode-map` binds them in every input mode, and vterm's map binds `C-c C-c`. They're the raw bytes, as ghostel sends them, so the line discipline makes signals of them whatever the kitty flags are. So `C-c C-c` interrupts a job or Claude Code after an Esc, without `i`. Before, each said "<C-c><C-c> is undefined". Insert's `C-c` still goes straight to the program (798). ghostel's other `C-c` keys (`C-c C-t`, `C-c C-y`, `C-c M-w` and the rest) aren't bound.
881. A switch of screens in normal state sends every window to the terminal's cursor, as `G` does. `SurfaceText::renumbered` is new, and die-term sets it when the screen it reads isn't the one it read last. Before, a window that had gone up kept its line number across the switch. So after `gg` on a program's screen, the program leaving put you on the scrollback's oldest line, 3,000 lines from anywhere you'd been. The other way round, a window up in the scrollback when a program takes the alternate screen goes to the cursor too. Other whole reads keep their lines as before: a resize, and the engine numbering its rows afresh after a flood (833).
882. Docs: using-die.md's Quit line, and in its Terminal section the Esc in insert, the program's keys, the mouse, new output and restarts. Also scope D18 and §5.4 to §5.6, contract §7 and §10, and the doc comments on core's `Event::Key` and `Surface::event`.
883. Tests, each failing with its fix taken out. In die-term's `terminal.rs`, on real PTYs with HOMEs of their own, over 863's fullscreen script, now a helper:
     - `the_wheel_in_normal_state_goes_to_a_program_that_tracks_the_mouse`: insert's wheel logs `CSI < 64;6;6 M` three times, and normal state's then logs `CSI < 65;6;6 M` three times.
     - `a_key_let_go_in_normal_state_has_its_release_sent`: `a` rolled over into Esc logs `CSI 97;;97 u CSI 97;1:3 u`, and Ctrl-[ logs Ctrl's `CSI 57442;5 u` and `CSI 57442;1:3 u`.
     - `c_c_c_c_in_normal_state_sends_the_program_c_c`: the four bytes in order, and `C-c C-c` in normal state ends `sleep 1000`.
     - `leaving_the_alternate_screen_takes_normal_state_to_the_terminals_cursor`: after `seq 1 3000`, `gg` on a script's screen, then the script leaving, the cursor is on the prompt's line, drawn as it's yanked.
     - In die's `terminals.rs`, `colon_q_asks_while_a_program_runs_in_a_terminal`: during `sleep 1000`, `:q`, `:q!`, `ZQ`, `:qa` and `:qa!` each ask, `n` keeps DIE, and `:q` then `y` quits.
     - Each of these, taken out, fails its test: the facade's question, `takes_wheel`, core's release routing, die-term taking releases outside insert, the `C-c` bindings, die-term's `renumbered`, and core's reading of it.
884. Budget: die-core from 15,385 to 15,400 (15,396 now). Its ext, dispatch and rt go from 1,640 to 1,650 (1,646: 878, 879), and surfaces and PTYs from 1,380 to 1,385 (1,381: 878, 881). die-vim from 10,391 to 10,410 (10,407: 877). die-term's commands from 360 to 370 (366: 880), and die-term is 2,119 of 5,000.

**`\z` in normal state: what the margins review found**

886. A review of 866–875 found one major thing, and it held: `\z` in normal state while scrolled back put the cursor on other output. Through your config in a 120-column frame, with the reviewer's own case (sixty 100-character lines, `gg`, `/M30x`), `\z` left the cursor on line 40, which the reflow had made `M50`'s, and `yy` yanked `M50xxx…`; `\z` again went back to `M30`. Ten 86-character lines at 80 columns put a cursor on `L3` onto `L6`. 769 kept a window's line number through a resize and said that after a width change those lines are the reflowed ones; 867 made that change a routine `\z`, where a file's window keeps its text.
887. Fixed by keeping each window in its line of output, not by sending it to the terminal's cursor as `renumbered` does (the reviewer's fallback), which would lose your place in the scrollback, as `\z` in a file never does. `SurfaceText::wrapped` is new: the lines that run on into the next one, each with the columns it takes. die-term fills it from each row's soft-wrap flag (741), at the terminal's width. Copy state keeps it a line at a time through updates and dropped lines. The whole read after a resize puts each window that isn't following (its cursor, its selection's other end and its top row) at the same place in the same line of output: as many lines of output before it, and as many columns before it in its own. A follower goes back to the terminal's cursor as before, and `renumbered` still sends every window there. So `\z` either way, a split and a frame resize keep a frozen window on its text, a selection's two ends too, and `yy` yanks what it was on. This replaces 769's last sentence for windows that don't follow.
888. A wrapped row's width is the terminal's columns. A wide character that didn't fit at a row's end leaves a blank cell there and starts the next row, so later in that line of output a place can land one character off after a reflow. Lines with no wide character wrapped that way land exactly.
889. Docs: contract §10's resize bullet says what `wrapped` is and what a resize keeps; scope §5.5 and using-die.md's Terminal section (size and margins, lines are rows) say the cursor stays on its text. D18 and contract §7 say nothing of resizes, and needed nothing.
890. Tests, each failing with the fix taken out:
     - die-core's `surface.rs`, `a_reflow_keeps_each_window_in_its_line_of_output`: a surface that wraps ten 103-character lines at its width, in two 120-column windows under your 80 centered. `\z` takes a cursor on `L3`'s second row at column 10 to `L3`'s one row at column 90, its top from `L2`'s first row to `L2`, and the other window's cursor at `L1`'s column 5 to `L1`'s row. `toggle-max-width` in visual state puts a selection from `L1`'s start to `L3`'s column 90 back at row 2 and at row 7 column 10, with its top. Without core's mapping it stays on row 7.
     - die-term's `terminal.rs`, `toggle_max_width_in_normal_state_keeps_the_cursor_on_its_text`, on a real PTY in a 120-column window with an 80-column maximum: ten 86-character lines; after `/L3-`, the widening leaves the cursor on `L3`'s one row, drawn where it's yanked, and `yy` takes all of `L3`; after `$`, the narrowing puts it on `L3`'s second row at column 5, under its first. Without core's mapping, or without die-term's `wrapped`, the widening shows `L6`.
     - die-dev's `terminal.rs`: the reviewer's case through your config, where `\z` and `yy` after `/M30x` yank `M30` and 97 x's. Without the fix they yank `M50`.
891. Budget: die-core from 15,400 to 15,440 (15,433 now), its surfaces and PTYs from 1,385 to 1,425 (1,418: 887). die-term is 2,122 of 5,000, its copy state 71 of 80.
892. Open: 888's wide character. A window that left the terminal for another buffer has its parked place carried through a whole read as an edit, as before, not put back on its text; I didn't try one. 875's two items stand. Not tried in your DIE.app, since nothing may go on your screen.

**The harness's wait, at the merge gate**

893. The gate on 4815b75 hung in `a_flood_doesnt_hold_up_a_key`: the harness's `Ctx::run_until` drained until idle, and an endless `yes` is never idle, since each 4 ms parse leaves a zero-delay timer due, so it could take a million turns. It now takes a frame's turns at a time (`run_ready`), as the host does, and asks `done` between them. DIE itself was never affected: the host doesn't drain, and the test's frames stayed under 2 ms. Two tests raced once the wait came back a frame sooner, and now wait for what they check: `g_goes_to_the_terminals_cursor_and_p_takes_a_count` pastes only after `stty -echo` has run (it saw `clear`'s pid as busy, and the terminal echoed the paste as well), and `it_leads_a_session_on_its_own_controlling_terminal` waits for the shell to take the terminal back from `ps`. Each passed 6 runs of 6; the flood test passes in 39 s.

**die-orchid-client**

894. What you asked: "is die in a position now where we would be able to write up some extensibility packages - not part of die itself as the core product but similar to my emacs-orchid-client. Would we be able to create a die-orchid-client that emulates the same behaviour? I think that should be our next development target". This is the base package: the crate, its wiring, and what the browser and chat packages build on, which start next in files of their own (`browser.rs`, `chat.rs`). The calls 895–905 were settled for the whole port before it started.
895. **For veto:** the crate is `die-orchid-client` at `extensions/die-orchid-client` in DIE's workspace, not `crates/`, which stays the product. `Extension::name()` is `orchid`, so its commands and faces are `orchid-*`. It depends on die-core alone (die-vim and die-test-cap for its tests), never on `die`, so it's platform-neutral: lint's NEUTRAL list has it. A new lint rule (`check_d14`, with a test like D15's) holds that an extension depends on die-core alone among the workspace's crates, and that nothing under `crates/` depends on one. It moves to its own repository at M4, which is M4's exit test now. Scope D14, M4's row, §11's lists and the contract's header note say so, in your words.
896. **For veto:** it targets orchid 0.2.2's interface exactly, and fixes the Emacs client's 0.1-era mismatches (A1–A12) and its bugs (B1–B9) rather than reproduce them. "Faithful" is the same buffers, names, keys, Evil states, layout, widths, faces and windows: never a split, the selected window changes, quit kills. This package's share: the registry from `metadata.json` (A1), `agent_name` (A2), orchid's `message` before its `error` (A5), `send --id ID -- MSG` (A6), `call_id` (A7), `edits[]` and `paths[]` (A8), stubs for `failure` and `termination` (A9), results that aren't file maps (A10), only valid agents offered (A12), and opening by label (B1).
897. **For veto:** read-only exactly where Emacs has it, the separators and the restored line; the extension's own edits always go through (`Origin::Extension`). The contract's §9 example said "orchid's transcript", and now says the separators and the restored line.
898. **For veto:** RET in normal state doesn't send, as Evil's precedence has it in Emacs. RET at or after the input marker in insert sends the trimmed input, and an empty input sends nothing (B3).
899. **For veto:** the header says `Agent:`, as 0.2 has agents, not policies. `/scope-escape` is dropped (A10 of the client map: it wrote a field orchid drops, outside its lock). help-echo's hover hints have no equivalent. `/config` opens the find-file prompt at the config root, as DIE has no dired.
900. **For veto:** local time through `jiff`, pinned at `=0.2.41` in `[workspace.dependencies]` with only `std`, `tz-system` and `tzdb-zoneinfo`: the system's zoneinfo, no bundled database. The zone is read at its first use. Tests pass a fixed one, UTC+10 (`test_zone`), Sydney's in July as the Emacs tests had it, since edition 2024 makes `set_var` unsafe. It brings `jiff-core` on macOS and Linux; the lockfile also has `jiff-static`, `portable-atomic` and Windows crates for other targets, none built here.
901. **For veto:** the marks' confirmation wants `yes` typed out, as `yes-or-no-p` does: a bare RET, or `y`, asks again ("Please answer yes or no."), and a cancel answers nothing. `yes_or_no` in lib.rs, for the browser's `x`.
902. **For veto:** one poll timer for all of orchid (`poll.rs`): 0.5 s while any subscriber says it's fast (a chat whose session runs or whose send is out), 2 s while any subscriber is left, to see changes made elsewhere, as from Emacs, and none at all with none. Subscriptions are handles a buffer's mode keeps, so a kill drops them, and the extension's kill hook stops the timer then; a handle dropped otherwise stops it at the next tick. The indicator's 1 s tick runs only while a turn runs (the chat package's). The Emacs client ran a 0.5 s timer per chat, a 1 s one per indicator and a 300 s idle one from startup.
903. **For veto:** `*Orchid Log*` keeps its last 2,000 lines (`Options::log_lines`), where Emacs's grew without end. M-p and M-n stay unbound, as they do nothing in Emacs.
904. **For veto:** after `:rebuild`, orchid buffers come back as working orchid buffers in the same windows: rebuilt from `events.jsonl`, unsent input kept, following again, never as dead plain text. This package has the plumbing: `Orchid::track(buf, Kind, state)` keeps a buffer with what its module needs, `track_with` adds a closure that works out what's kept when the session looks (a chat's unsent input, from its marker), and `restore_session` hands it back by kind (`browser::restore`, `chat::restore`), with the extension checked out. The browser and chat packages fill those in. `Mode::save` is still never called (the readiness study's gap 5), so this goes round it.
905. **For veto:** your config registers `Orchid::default()`, its options typed fields of `Options` (C7), and binds `<leader>o` to `orchid` in normal state, as `init.el` does. orchid is found on `die_core::process::path()`'s PATH, asked inside the job, so DIE.app from the Dock finds `~/.local/bin/orchid`. The root is orchid's own rule, `$ORCHID_CONFIG` then `$HOME/.config/orchid`, worked out at its first use, never at registration. Each child's environment loses `ORCHID_SESSION_ID`, `ORCHID_CONFIG` and `ORCHID_HOOK_DEPTH`, and every command gets `--config ROOT` first, so a message's own `--config` stays a message.
906. **For veto:** the budget has an `extension = true` kind (`xtask/src/budget.rs`): counted, never capped, in no product total, shown as `ext`. It must live under `extensions/`, and anything there must be one. die-orchid-client is 2,145 lines; the product total doesn't move.
907. Options and their defaults are the Emacs client's: `orchid`, orchid's root, 250 events from the last 5 MB, and a 300 s timeout per command (its dead `orchid-core-default-timeout`, and the contract's example); a hung command holds one thread that long. `Orchid::for_tests(root, FakeRunner)` is the test constructor; `fixtures.rs` writes sessions by hand in 0.2.2's shapes under a `TempRoot`.
908. The registry is every session's `metadata.json`, scanned in a job (`refresh_sessions`), not `orchid list`, which loads every events file and returns only ids and labels. A file that doesn't read is skipped, as one caught mid-rename. A status change, a new session or a gone one is news for each `StatusWatch`, delivered together at the top of the loop with the extension put back, before the refresh's own callback. `orchid-list-sessions` lists the scan oldest first, as `orchid list` did.
909. `orchid-list-sessions` shows its lines in `*orchid-list-sessions*`, as Emacs's echo area grew to show them and DIE's command row shows one line. It and `orchid-log-show` show as `display-buffer` does (`display_buffer`): a window showing it already, else the window used last that isn't this one and shows text, else a split below, the focus staying. Everything else the client shows goes in the selected window.
910. **For veto:** stubs where Emacs had none or a wrong one: `[fail] [message]` in `orchid-collapsible-failure-stub` (on `warning`), `[term] [reason]`, a tool orchid hasn't got as `[tool] [name]` (Emacs's became `[event]` with no preview), `bash` without a command as `[bash] []`, and `fs_read` of one path showing the path, of more `[N files]` (Emacs showed `[1 files]` for every read). Legacy `usage` and `reasoning` lines show nothing, as orchid 0.2 ignores them; any other unknown type is `[Unhandled event: t]` and its line, as before.
911. **For veto:** a result object is a file map, as `fs_read`'s, unless it's `{"error"}` (`[error] why`), `{"status","error"}` (`[terminated] why`) or has `edits_applied` (`path: N edits applied`), each said in one line, eagerly. A lone file read that's literally named `error` would read as an error.
912. An edit's detail is `diff -u` per edit, run on `/usr/bin/diff` through two files of its own when the section opens, as Emacs ran `shell-command`. The file header is dropped only before the first hunk: Emacs dropped any line starting `---`, and so a removed line starting `--`.
913. Face names drop Emacs's `-face`. Each has a base parent that draws it as mono-dark drew the Emacs face, but the header's workspace (`font-lock-string-face`, mid grey), which mono-dark now styles; its test allows faces core or die-orchid-client declares. Emacs's six faces nothing applied aren't declared.
914. `cli::Runner` runs a call; `ProcessRunner` is orchid as a process, stdout apart from stderr, killed at its timeout, with an optional PATH so a test never asks your login shell; `FakeRunner` records argv and answers by command, with an orchid error naming any it wasn't told. `Runner::find` is `executable-find`, for `orchid-check-cli`.
915. die-dev's leader tests built their harness from `built_in()` and your config's core alone, which C8 now refuses, since `\o` names `orchid`. They take your config's extensions too, as `die::start` does.
916. Tests: 34 unit tests (the Emacs parser, browser-format, indicator and restored-line expectations that hold for 0.2, the store's shapes, scan, tail and restore, argv and replies); `extension.rs` on a harness (registration reads and runs nothing and sets no timer, check-cli's four answers, the registry's news, open by label, the agent prompt, the session list, the log's cap and placement, what a restart keeps, yes-or-no); `poll.rs` on the fake clock (no buffer, no timer; fast and slow; a kill stops it); `process.rs` in processes of their own, on a stand-in sh script (the PATH, `--config` first, the scrub, stdout and stderr, exit codes, the directory, the timeout, orchid's root rule); die-dev's `orchid_comes_in_through_the_config_and_leader_o_opens_it`; lint's D14 test and the budget's. `tests/orchid.rs` is ignored: with `ORCHID_BIN` it runs your orchid 0.2.2 on a root and HOME of its own with its `echo` connection, and it passed here once (create, agent, send with `-hello there`, the turn's events, restore, set, list, stop, delete).
917. Open: the browser and the chat (the next two packages). Not tried in your DIE.app, since nothing may go on your screen. Core gaps the port goes round, for M1: `rename_buffer`, styled insertion, a public buffer-local keymap, `Mode::save`, file watching and a `process` API; none needed a core change here.

**die-orchid-client: the session browser**

918. The browser package: `*orchid-sessions*` (`browser.rs`) and seek's port (`seek.rs`), as the Emacs client's §2.2 and §2.4 have them, on the base's registry, poll, restart tracking and `yes_or_no`. It fixes A1 for the browser (UPDATED, the order and `•RUN` from `metadata.json`), B6 (`p`) and B7 (seek's row count). `orchid-open-session` by label (B1) and `orchid-list-sessions` in a buffer were the base's, and are unchanged.
919. seek's port takes the browser's state, the width and the height, and gives back styled lines, where seek read the selected window (and orchid swapped in a fake `window-width`). Its chrome is counted as drawn, 6 lines, where seek counted 4 and so showed two rows fewer than fit (B7). Padding counts columns, where seek counted characters; that changes only trailing spaces of a wide-character line, which the 78-column cut takes off anyway. Empty, it says `No results`, seek's word, for no sessions too.
920. **For veto:** the faces are seek's and the Emacs client's: the title and the column titles `bold`, separators `shadow`, STATUS `orchid-session-browser-active` or `-idle`, UPDATED `shadow`, a marked row wholly `error` (`DEL`) or `warning` (`KILL`), and the selected row padded and wholly `highlight`, as `propertize` put `highlight` over the row's own faces. seek's search line while searching was `(:background "#3a3a3a")` over the whole line, hiding the query's `isearch`; it's `orchid-seek-search`, declared on `highlight`, which mono-dark styles `#3a3a3a`.
921. The faces come from the mode's `Mode::decorate`, from the spans of the last draw and only on the revision it drew, not from stored decorations: every change redraws the whole buffer, so nothing needs mapping through edits.
922. Its width is 78, or the text area of the window showing it if that's less, the focused one first: your 80-column centred defaults apply, as visual-fill-column applied to Emacs's `window-width`. Lines are cut at 78, then by the window, as `truncate-lines` cut them: in a 40-column window the column titles and rows are cut there. The height is the window's, at least one row. It's read-only, `wrap` is off, no cursor is drawn (Emacs's sat at the end), and undo is off. The cursor sits on the selected row, so a short window scrolls to it.
923. **For veto:** it stays current by itself. Any status news redraws it, and a redraw changes the text only when a row shown changed, which is Emacs's rule of redrawing for a visible session. While a window shows it, the shared poll's 2 s tick reads the sessions again, one scan at a time in a job, so UPDATED, the order, labels and ACTIVE keep up with changes made elsewhere; Emacs's browser redrew only when an open chat's indicator flipped a status. Buried, it doesn't scan, and `orchid` scans each time, as Emacs's synchronous `list` did.
924. **For veto:** when the list changes under it (a refresh, a scan, news), the selection stays on its session; Emacs kept the index, so a re-sort put it on another session just before `RET`. The keys that move it (`j k J K p`, `D` and `S`, typing a query, `d`, `C-g`) move the index as Emacs did, clamped as seek clamps.
925. **For veto:** `•RUN` is `metadata.json`'s `running`, wherever the turn was started, and `S` marks only such a session (A1: Emacs's `:running` came from an open chat's indicator alone). ACTIVE is a chat buffer tracked as `Kind::Chat` on the session; DIE has no log buffers.
926. Keys are in normal state, on the mode `orchid-session-browser-mode`; DIE has no motion state. Searching enables a second mode over it, `orchid-session-browser-search`: `<char>` (any printable key, where Emacs bound ASCII 32–126), `<BS>`, `<CR>`, `<Esc>` and `<C-g>`. What it doesn't bind falls through to die-vim (`C-w`, `:`), where Emacs's emacs state gave Emacs's global keys. Commands keep the Emacs client's names and docs (`orchid-session-browser-select` …), but refresh's says it reads the sessions. Counts are ignored, as the Emacs commands ignored them. Insert and visual state keep die-vim's keys, where Emacs's major-mode map also worked in insert state.
927. **For veto:** `x` runs the marks in one job, `delete` or `stop` each in the order marked, detached so its summary still comes if you quit the browser meanwhile; a second `x` while it runs says so. Failures go in `*Orchid Log*` with orchid's message, which Emacs dropped. The summary is Emacs's (`Deleted 1 session, Killed 2 processes, 1 failed`), said after the sessions are read again and the browser redrawn.
928. `RET` shows the chat open on the session if there is one, as Emacs checked `:chat-buffer`, else `chat::open`; the browser is killed after, and not if opening fails. `n` takes the browser's directory, which is the buffer's it was made from, as `get-buffer-create` kept it; the browser stays, as Emacs's did. `orchid` draws at once from what the registry knows, or waits for the first scan when it knows nothing, so no `No results` flashes.
929. A restart keeps the browser's query, whether it was searching, the selection, the scroll and the marks, tracked again at each draw. `restore` turns its modes back on and reads the sessions, which redraws it in its window; until then the text the session kept shows, without faces. A job's `Sub` stays active after its `done` has run, so the browser keeps its own flag for a scan that's out, given up on after 30 s; noted for M1.
930. Tests: seek (6: the chrome count, clamping, the layout, the search line and `No results`, header lines, scrolling and cutting with faces); the browser's own (8: labels, statuses, the fuzzy match, the ERT 46-column row cut with `..`, marks' faces, the order, nothing past 78 columns with a wide query, what a restart keeps); `tests/browser.rs` on a harness with die-vim (11: the layout at 78 with its faces, `No results`, `j k p J K`, `r g q`, marks with a bare RET, `no` and `yes` and a failure kept, searching, live redraws and the poll stopping, narrow and short windows, `RET` with and without an open chat, `n`, a restart). Two (`RET` without a chat, `n`) take either the chat package's behaviour or its stub's message, so they hold before and after it lands.
931. Open: the chats (the next package), which the browser's `RET` and `n` call as the base defines them. Not tried in your DIE.app, since nothing may go on your screen. A kill shows the buffer used last that no window shows, which can differ from `quit-window`'s return to that window's own previous buffer when another buffer was made in between; it didn't seem worth a core change.

**die-orchid-client: the chat**

932. The chat package: `*orchid-chat-<agent>-<wsdir>-<id5>*` (`chat.rs`), its collapsible sections (`sections.rs`) and its processing indicator (`indicator.rs`), as the Emacs client's §2.2 and §2.5–§2.9 have them, on the base's store, parse, poll, CLI and restart tracking. It fixes A3, A4 and A11 (the indicator), B2, B3, B4, B5, B8 and B9; `/new` takes `agent_name` (A2). The browser's `RET` and `n` reach it now.
933. **For veto:** your input starts at the blank line under the last separator, a line earlier than Emacs's; the bytes are Emacs's, and a send trims that line away. die-vim's motions never put the cursor on the empty line after the last newline (scope §10), which is where Emacs's empty input line was, so `G`, `j` or a click landed on the blank line above it, where typing went into the transcript and RET made a newline.
934. **For veto:** a user message in `events.jsonl` this chat didn't send, as from Emacs, starts a turn here as one of yours does: its stub and a separator above your input, and the indicator if the session runs. Emacs drew nothing for it and put that turn's events inside the last one. Messages sent from here are matched by text until they appear, so they aren't drawn twice.
935. **For veto:** a turn already running when a chat opens counts from your last message's time, and one from elsewhere from its message's, as orchid keeps no start for a turn; Emacs counted from the open (A11). `[Chunks: N]` and the status hints are gone, as orchid writes neither. The line is drawn each second only while a window shows the chat, and again at the finish (B4).
936. **For veto:** at a finish, a window reading the transcript goes to the end, as Emacs sent every window there, but one in your input stays where you're typing.
937. The chat's own edits are `Origin::Extension` and silent, no undo step: events, stubs, separators, the indicator, `[SYSTEM]`. One isn't: a send's removal of your input is recorded, so `u` nets it out with your typing. A silent one left your typing's step pointing at text that was gone, and undo's next step was a conflict that dropped the history.
938. **A die-vim bug, fixed there:** an insert session kept its places (the last insertion, where it left the cursor, replace state's takings, a block's line) as byte offsets, which an extension's silent edit above them left stale. Esc after an event streamed in sliced the text inside a `─`, panicked, and stayed in insert state, and `.` held the wrong text. The session now follows the change log's `Extension` and silent changes before it reads them (`Session::follow`; its own are `User` changes it moves them for itself), and the slice checks its bounds. die-vim's `an_extensions_edit_above_what_youre_typing_keeps_the_insert_session_on_it` (`hostile.rs`) panics without it. `.` is what you typed on both sides of the event, where Evil's after-change tracking would have restarted at the event. die-vim's limit is 10,450, from 10,410 (guard 7; `budget.toml` says why).
939. A section is its stub and a detail that starts with the stub's newline, under a `Deco::Hide` whose reveal runs `orchid-chat-reveal`, so a search that lands inside expands it. A `Deco::Tag` over both names it, and its faces are `Deco::Face` decorations, as core has no styled insertion (the readiness study's gap f4). `TAB` toggles the innermost at the cursor, or the one the line ends at a line's end, where insert state's cursor sits after a stub. A lazy detail is made once, at its first expand. `orchid-collapsible-report-stats` counts them for every chat, in `*orchid-collapsible-stats*`, as its report is five lines.
940. **For veto:** `TAB` inside an expanded detail collapses it and takes the cursor to its stub. Emacs left point in the hidden text, and in a lazy detail once made, `TAB` didn't collapse it at all, as the made text had no section property.
941. Read-only is Emacs's: the restored line with its newline, and the separator after it or after a running session's indicator. `[SYSTEM]`, a send's separator and those between restored turns aren't. `[SYSTEM]` goes just above your input, framed as Emacs framed it at the end, so what you'd typed stays your input under it (B9).
942. **For veto:** `orchid-chat-load-more-history` puts each batch of older events, as many as a restore takes (250), above the last, where B5 put them below, and just under the first separator, where the restored events start; Emacs put them above that separator. A separator goes between batches before your message, as a restore lays them out.
943. **For veto:** `/` alone at an input line's start opens the slash menu without typing the `/` (Emacs typed it, then took it back); elsewhere it's die-vim's `vim-insert-char`. `/config` runs `find-file` with the chat's directory set to orchid's root for the moment, and puts it back (call 899). A cancel does nothing, as Emacs ignored `C-g`.
944. A new session's chat is `*orchid-chat-<agent>-<wsdir>-new*` at once, and takes typing while orchid makes the session. Then the session's chat takes its place in each window that showed it, with what was typed and the cursor's place in it, in insert state if you were typing: DIE has no `rename_buffer` (gap e4), so it's a new buffer and the pending one is killed. `RET` there says orchid is still making the session and keeps the input. `set ID --label <agent>-<wsdir>` follows, fire and forget, as Emacs's did. A failed create says `[SYSTEM] Failed to create session:` in the pending chat, which a restart doesn't bring back.
945. After a restart a chat is rebuilt from `events.jsonl` in place of the plain text the session kept, with its unsent input and the cursor's place in it, and follows again, with its mode, keys and sections. A pending one makes its session again: had DIE restarted while orchid was making it, there'd be two. One whose session has gone says so and keeps the input. Insert state isn't kept, as for any buffer.
946. Measured, test profile, on a 2,000-event, 1.9 MB `events.jsonl`: opening restores 250 in 14 ms. That chat is 194 KB of text with 819 decorations; a key takes 0.4 ms, and session.json is 208 KB, encoded in 1 ms. With all 2,000 loaded it's 1.5 MB with 6,506 decorations, 0.7 ms a key, and a 1.6 MB session.json (7 ms). A streamed event costs 1.8 ms. So core rewriting a chat's whole text into session.json at each event (the verifier's concern) is about 200 KB a write for a restored chat: fine for now. Keeping a buffer's place in the session without its text, which the chat rebuilds from its files anyway, is for M1.
947. The poll: a chat is fast while its session runs, a send is out or its indicator shows, slow otherwise, and off once its session is archived or it's killed. Each tick reads `metadata.json` before `events.jsonl`, since orchid has written a turn's events before it says the turn is over, and a finish reads the events once more. Each read updates the registry, so the browser hears. Trouble reading either file goes in `*Orchid Log*` once until it changes (B8's spam).
948. Tests: units (sections: collapsed at first, the toggle, nothing outside, a lazy detail once, nesting, numbers, marks, the stats' text; the indicator's line with and without tokens); `tests/chat.rs` on a harness with die-vim (15: the restore's text, rows, faces and read-only parts exactly; an empty session; the header; names and `<2>`; `TAB` eager, lazy once, nested, in insert state, in the input and elsewhere; search and `j k` over hidden text; read-only refusals; the slash menu and each command; the keys by `describe-key`, closing and the browser; older history in order; the stats); `tests/follow.rs` (17: typing while events stream in, `.` and undo; the send's argv and stub; the estimate's fallback; `[SYSTEM]`; the indicator on each of orchid's four ends, and the poll's pace; a quick turn; an unreadable status; a running session at open; the restore and follow's seam and dedup; an archived session; a turn from elsewhere; a new session typed into while pending; a failed create; three restarts, of a chat, a pending one and a gone one; no timer once it's killed). The base's open-by-label and new-session tests check the real chat now. `tests/real_chat.rs` is ignored: with `ORCHID_BIN` it runs your orchid 0.2.2 as `tests/orchid.rs` does (a HOME and root of its own, `ORCHID_*` removed, the `echo` connection), and it passed here once: a new session made and labelled through the chat, a send of `-hello there`, its echo followed in and finished, and the archive said once. die-vim's test is 938's.
949. Open: not tried in your DIE.app, since nothing may go on your screen. I ran `orchid --version` once in my own shell to look at the binary before that test; `--version` reads and makes no root, but it ran with your HOME. Hover hints have no equivalent (call 899). Core gaps the chat goes round, for M1: styled insertion, `rename_buffer`, keeping a buffer's place without its text (946), and `Mode::save`. A pending chat a restart brings back makes its session again (945).

**die-orchid-client: holding up in your use**

950. The package: each orchid buffer back working after a restart, twice in a row; the whole client through die-dev's config on the harness; a long chat's cost measured; an end-to-end run on a stand-in orchid through the real runner, and one on yours; and what the earlier packages left open that was in reach.
951. **A core bug, fixed there:** every queued call counted as you doing something, so any repeating timer kept core's tick at its 0.5 s pace for good. With an idle chat open DIE woke 120 times a minute, against 12 without one: twice S1's `idle-60` limit of 1 a second. Now only input wakes the tick (a key, a click, the focus, a resize); a timer's or a job's call doesn't, and `found_nothing`, which exempted the tick's own call, goes. A change a timer or a job makes after 30 s of nothing from you is written at the next look, within 5 s, rather than 1 s after (session.rs's header says so). die-core's `an_extensions_repeating_timer_doesnt_keep_the_looks_awake` (`tests/session.rs`) counts 120 without the fix. 'ext, dispatch, rt' is 5 lines smaller, 1,641. An idle chat now costs 36 wakes a minute: orchid's 30, and core's resting 12, some at once.
952. **For veto:** a restart brings a chat's sections back as they were, rather than all collapsed. A section made from an event has a key, the event's id and its place among the event's sections (`ID#0`; a result's second file `ID#0.1`), held in its `Deco::Tag`; the expanded ones' keys are kept, and a restart expands them again, a section before those inside it. Your message's stub as a send draws it has no key; after a restart it's the restored stub, which has.
953. **For veto:** a restart puts each window on a chat back on the same text, cursor and top. What's kept is each window's offset in the text the session saved, with its anchor: the keyed section it's in, or the last one before it, or the input. The restart finds each window's restored offset in that list (within 8 bytes, for where normal state rests it) and puts it as far from its anchor in the chat built anew, so events that came while DIE was down, or the 250 shifting, don't move it. One that can't be told goes to the end, as before. A window's place in a chat it no longer shows, and `g;`'s, aren't mapped.
954. A pending chat keeps when its create started, and a restart first looks for a session made since (less 2 s), of its agent, in its directory, with no events yet: one orchid made while DIE restarted, whose answer went with the old DIE. The chat takes it over rather than make a second (call 945's risk). A create still running in the old DIE's child when the new DIE looks can still make two.
955. Restarts are tested as `:rebuild` makes them (`tests/restart.rs`): a harness on a real session directory hands over and drops, and a new one starts on the same file, twice in a row. A running chat in a split: sections expanded, a window reading the transcript and one in the input with unsent text, an event that came while DIE was down, the indicator ticking again, then the finish and a send. The browser still searching, a chat no window shows following, a pending chat made once over two restarts, and a chat whose session went while DIE was down saying so once. And a session orchid made during the restart, taken over.
956. die-dev's config end to end (`apps/die-dev/tests/orchid.rs`): `die::built_in()` and your config's core, its one extension, `orchid`, swapped for the same made for a test (a temporary root, orchid faked), since yours reads your `~/.config/orchid`. `\o`, `RET`, typing and sending, events in while you type, `TAB`, `C-w s` and `\]` hiding the chat in both windows while it follows to the finish, `S-Tab`, `q`, `C-c C-q`. In a 120-column window the browser is 78 columns at column 20 and the chat's separators 80 at 20, at 0 after `\z` and at 20 after a second. An idle chat costs 30 wakes a minute, and once it's closed there's no timer and none.
957. The runner's timeout stops the child's process group: the child leads one (`process_group(0)`), and a timeout runs `/bin/kill -KILL -- -PGID`, as std can't signal a group. A hung orchid's own children went on before, holding its pipes. orchid's turn worker leads a group of its own (orchid's `cli_send.rs`), so no timeout stops a turn.
958. `tests/fake-orchid`, a POSIX sh stand-in for orchid 0.2.2 in its shapes (create, set, send with a background worker that echoes, delete, agent, `--version`, and a stop that hangs with a child of its own), run by its absolute path through the real runner from a chat (`tests/end_to_end.rs`), in a process of its own with a temporary HOME and the three `ORCHID_` variables set. It checks `orchid-new-session`'s prompt offering only the valid agent; the session made and labelled, with no parent, and none of the three variables reaching the child; the PATH the child got; a send's echo followed to `[Finished: ]`, with stderr's chatter not taken for the answer; a refused send's message, from stderr with exit 1, in `[SYSTEM]`; `/kill` hanging until the 2 s timeout, with its child gone too; and the archive said once. `tests/process.rs` stays, for the lookup on the PATH and the root rule.
959. `tests/real_chat.rs` sends `hello` now, and restarts at the end through a session directory of its own. Run here once against `~/.local/bin/orchid` 0.2.2, as its rules have it (a HOME, root and session directory of its own, `ORCHID_*` removed, the `echo` connection): made and labelled through the chat, `hello` and its echo followed in to `[Finished: ]`, a restart that rebuilt the chat from orchid's files with the unsent input, and the archive said once, in 0.48 s. No worker or temporary directory was left.
960. Measured (`tests/perf.rs`, ignored; test profile, a loaded machine, two runs). A 2,002-event, 8.8 MB `events.jsonl`, with a 40 KB bash output every tenth turn and a 20 KB file read in each: opening restores 250 in 9–28 ms, to a chat of 239 KB with 792 decorations and a 247 KB session.json, made in 0.4–0.7 ms. A key takes 0.3–0.8 ms, 0.3–1.0 ms while events stream in; a streamed event 0.5–1.6 ms. While a turn streams with its indicator in view, session.json is written every 6 s, 40 times in 240 s and 13 MB in all, 250–370 KB each: the indicator's draw each second never lets the session be quiet for 1 s, so it's written at the 5 s limit. With all that loads loaded (the last 5 MB) and two windows: 1 MB with 3,689 decorations, a 1 MB session.json in 3–4 ms, 0.7 ms a key. The browser's scan of 500 sessions takes 22 ms on a job's thread, every 2 s while a window shows it. None of it wanted a fix.
961. **For veto:** a tool's output stays an eager detail, though a lazy one would take big outputs out of the text and the session file: your `init.el` sets `search-invisible t`, so your search finds text in a collapsed output, and a lazy detail can't be searched until it's made. Faces stay stored decorations, as a key costs under 1 ms with 3,689 of them.
962. Smaller: the browser tests' fallbacks to the stubs' messages are gone, and they check the real chat (`RET` opens it in this window) and create (`n`'s argv and directory). using-die.md's orchid section says what a restart brings back, what the client looks at and when, the timeout, and what the session file keeps. `cargo xtask cross-check` passes for both Linux targets, the client's first.
963. Open: not tried in your DIE.app, since nothing may go on your screen; it's on m0/integration only. The session file keeps a chat's whole text, written every 5 s while a turn runs (960): keeping a buffer's place without its text is M1's, with styled insertion, `rename_buffer` and `Mode::save` (call 949). Core's, left: a job's `Sub` staying active after its `done` (call 929), and the browser's `q` showing the buffer used last rather than `quit-window`'s. die-vim's limit raise (call 938) still wants your OK. A create running in the old DIE's child as the new one starts can still make two sessions (954). History loaded with `orchid-chat-load-more-history` comes back as the last 250.

**die-orchid-client: the review's fixes**

964. The package: the independent review of the four orchid packages, 16 findings, each checked here first against the Emacs client's source, your `init.el` or Emacs 30.2 in batch (a temporary HOME), or reproduced on the harness. 14 held and are fixed, each with a test that fails with its fix taken out (checked fix by fix). Two are calls already logged for veto, and stand (978). Core and die-vim are unchanged.
965. A restored chat's rule between turns has a blank line before it and two after it, as Emacs drew it: its restore made the separator an event (`\n`, the rule, `\n\n`, `orchid-log-restore.el`) and its display put `\n` after every event (`orchid-chat-display.el`), where DIE left that last one out. The same for the rule over each batch of `orchid-chat-load-more-history`. `a_restore_lays_the_chat_out_as_emacs_did` pinned the wrong spacing; it pins Emacs's now, and `older_history_loads_above_in_order` the batches'.
966. **For veto:** widths are counted as DIE draws them (`parse::width`), so a stub never passes column 80 and wraps: a grapheme cluster as wide as Unicode says, one at least, a control character two (`^M`) or four (`\200`), and a tab eight, as Emacs's `string-width` counts it (DIE's tab stops never take more). A stub with tabs is Emacs's byte for byte now, where a tab counted none and the time wrapped onto a row of its own. `⚠️` and `✔️`, common in replies, count two, as DIE draws them; Emacs's `string-width` says one (checked in batch), so their stubs have a space less padding than Emacs's. Cuts fall between clusters. The crate takes the workspace's `unicode-segmentation` (=1.13.3, die-core's already): Cargo.lock gains only that edge, no crate.
967. `orchid-log-show` is Emacs's: it asks `Session ID: ` (or takes it after the name) and shows ` *orchid-log:<id>*` as `display-buffer` does, the session's `events.jsonl` as the chat following it has read it. The first time, the last 5 MB (`restore_max_bytes`) up to where the chat's follow stands, in whole lines, as Emacs's monitor read the file when it started; then each line as the chat reads it, all of the file again if it shrank. It goes with the chat, as Emacs's cleanup stopped the monitor. With no chat following the session, Emacs's `Not monitoring session <id>`. `*Orchid Log*` has no command, as in Emacs; you go to it as to any buffer. This replaces the half of call 909 that showed `*Orchid Log*`.
968. **For veto:** the browser's rows wrap at a word rather than being cut (`Wrap::Word`). The mode's `truncate-lines t` never held in your Emacs: your `init.el`'s `global-visual-line-mode` turns `visual-line-mode` on after the major mode, so `*orchid-sessions*` had `truncate-lines` nil and `word-wrap` t (the reviewer checked it in batch). At 78 columns and wider nothing changes; in a 50-column window a row reads `…IDLE•RUN   14` / `hours ago`, as Emacs drew it. Where a line passes the edge inside a run of spaces, core's word-wrap rule (contract §9) breaks before the word ahead of them: at 40 columns, `LABEL` / `STATUS     UPDATED`. Call 922's "cut … as truncate-lines cut them" goes.
969. `yes_or_no` takes the answer in any case and doesn't trim it, as Emacs 30.2's `yes-or-no-p` downcases what's typed and compares it whole (checked in batch: `YES` and `Yes` are yes, ` yes` asks again).
970. A chat's directory is the one it was opened from (the browser's for its `RET`, the buffer's for `orchid-open-session`), as Emacs's `get-buffer-create` gave it the current buffer's; it was the session's working directory. So `find-file` and `orchid-new-session` from a chat start where Emacs's did, and `/new` goes by the session's working directory, as Emacs's did. A new session's chat keeps its pending chat's directory, as Emacs's renamed buffer kept its own.
971. Emacs's `orchid-session-browser-show` (the browser, as `orchid`) and `orchid-collapsible-toggle-at-point` (the section at the cursor, nothing where there's none) are there. Every interactive defun of the client's now has its name in DIE but `M-p` and `M-n`'s (call 903); the registration test lists all 32.
972. `orchid-open-session` says `Opened session: <what you typed>` once it's open, and `Failed to open session: Session '<x>' not found` as a message, as Emacs's did, rather than an error worded its own way.
973. **For veto:** text going in above your input keeps each window's whole selection, both ends, its shape and its goal column, rather than putting a cursor where its head was, and so does the indicator's redraw for a selection with an end on its line. At a finish, a window reading the transcript in visual state has its selection stretched to the end, as Emacs's `(goto-char (point-max))` stretched the region; in normal state it's a cursor there, as before. Before, a turn started elsewhere (a send from Emacs, a child's notify-parent hook) collapsed a visual selection in your input, and `d` took one character.
974. A chat whose session is gone sends nothing, and says so: orchid 0.2.2 refused it, and its reconcile took the session's lock, which made `sessions/<id>/.flock` again. A session is gone when its `metadata.json` is, which orchid replaces by a rename and so never lacks while it has the session, rather than when its directory is: one left holding only `.flock` (a send or `set` that got there first, or orchid's worker after a delete) was never gone, kept the poll fast and logged two lines every 0.5 s. Trouble reading each file is logged once for that file until it changes or the file reads, where one slot for both let the two take turns.
975. A kept input comes back byte for byte: what you typed on the blank line under the separator (after `G`, `j` or a click) came back after a restart with a blank line before it and the cursor a character to the left. Only a new chat's empty input becomes the blank line.
976. `TAB` in your input in insert state is die-vim's quoted insert of the tab key (`vim-quoted-insert`, with `<Tab>` as its key), so the insert session takes the tab in with what's typed around it: after `Gia<Tab>b<Esc>`, `.` holds `a\tb`, as Evil's insertion did, where it held `b`. In normal state, or without die-vim, it's the plain insert it was. The crate still depends on die-core alone; it names die-vim's command as it names `vim-insert-newline-bare`. In replace state the tab types over a character, where Emacs's inserted.
977. **For veto:** `Agent: ` waits for a prompt you opened while `orchid agent` ran, looking again every 200 ms until it closes, and then asks. Emacs called orchid synchronously before `completing-read` (`orchid-browser-populate.el`), so it never took a prompt from you, where core's `prompt` cancels the open one. The look runs only while both are out.
978. Left as logged, for your veto, which the reviewer found stated accurately: call 933 (after `G`, `j` or a click what you type sits right under the last separator, where Emacs had a blank line between) and call 940 (`TAB` inside an expanded lazy detail collapses it, where Emacs only said what `TAB` is for; inside an eager one Emacs collapsed it too).
979. Tests, each failing with its fix taken out: units for widths (the tabbed stub as Emacs's, clusters, controls, cuts between clusters); `tests/chat.rs` (the restore's spacing, the load-more batches', stubs with tabs, emoji and `\r` on one row, the chat's directory, toggle-at-point); `tests/follow.rs` (a selection kept as a turn goes in, the indicator ticks and the turn finishes; a gone chat sending nothing and a `.flock`-only directory gone, said once; trouble logged once a file; `orchid-log-show`'s buffer, its updates and its going with the chat; `.` holding a tab); `tests/restart.rs` (an input typed on the blank line, back over two restarts); `tests/browser.rs` (a row wrapped at a word at 50 and 40 columns); `tests/extension.rs` (every command of the Emacs client's, yes-or-no's case and spaces, open-session's messages, `Agent: ` waiting for your prompt).
980. Open: not tried in your DIE.app, since nothing may go on your screen; it's on m0/integration only. `orchid-log-show`'s buffer is read from the file at its first show, up to where the chat's follow stands, rather than kept from the chat's open as Emacs's was; orchid only appends, so it's the same text. Still open from earlier: calls 929, 938 (die-vim's limit, still wanting your OK), 945 and 954, and keeping a buffer's place in the session without its text (946).

**M3a: placements and pixel buffers**

981. The package: the whiteboard's core slice (the build brief's Part B, die-core's half), pulled ahead of D20, M2's Linux branch and M1 by your acceptance of freehand drawing as a definite want (2026-11-10), general primitives only (scope guard 5): die-term's kitty graphics are the second user (`crates/die-term/src/lib.rs` holds the engine's image limit at 0 until DIE draws them), plots and viewers follow (scope §9). die-render's half (the placement quad, the staging ring, the pending flag, the drag fix) lands apart, on this API.
982. `Placement { buffer, col, row, cols, rows, z }` in `Scene.placements`: a pixel buffer's pixels on a rectangle of cells from its top-left corner, 1:1 in physical pixels, clipped at the rectangle's edge; z < 0 draws between the cells' backgrounds and their glyphs, z ≥ 0 over the glyphs and under the cursors; the list is in drawing order, lower z first, stable within a z. The source rectangle, the sub-cell offset and images wait for M3. `Scene` still derives `PartialEq`, so die-render's unchanged-scene skip sees a placement come and go; a present that changes no cell is the render builder's pending flag.
983. **For veto:** `Frame::place(buffer, z)` places over the whole area and isn't kept between renders: a surface places what it shows at every render, where its cells persist. One push a frame, against a removal API; the contract said "surfaces place them in `render`". `Frame::placed()` reads it back, for a surface's own tests.
984. **For veto:** a placement stops above the prompt's list, as the hollow cursors do (`covered`), rather than reaching under it: at z ≥ 0 it would cover the list, and at z < 0 an opaque buffer would hide the list's backgrounds. Its bottom rows go, which the 1:1 rule makes a crop, not a squeeze.
985. `pixels.rs`, a module of its own (188 lines): `Ctx::pixel_buffer(width, height) -> Result<(PixelBufferId, Producer)>`; `Producer` (`Send`, owns the premultiplied RGBA8 buffer at `width × 4` a row, `pixels_mut`, `size`, `stride`, `resize`, `present(damage)`, destroys on drop); `PixelRect` (`whole`, `clipped_to`, `union`); the `PixelSink` trait (`create`, `present(id, pixels, width, damage)`, `resize`, `destroy`; `Send + Sync`, implemented for `Arc<S>` too); `Ctx::set_pixel_sink`, once, as `set_waker` is; `Ctx::pixels_supported`; `MAX_SIDE` 16,384. Err with no sink, or a side of 0 or over the cap. Ids never repeat in a workspace, so a dangling placement names nothing.
986. **For veto:** `PixelSink::present` gets the whole buffer and the damage, as S1's ring took it (`spikes/s1-renderer/src/pixbuf.rs`), rather than the damaged rows cut out: the same bytes, no copy, and a sink may copy more on its first frame. The damage reaches the sink clipped to the buffer and never empty; an empty one isn't a present and doesn't wake.
987. The lock rule is in the shapes: `present` runs on the producer's thread and may block it; core takes no lock at all (each buffer's liveness is an `AtomicBool` the producer clears on drop and compose reads), and the trait's docs put the `try_lock` hand-off on the host's sink. `Producer` is asserted `Send + Sync` at compile time and holds the `Waker` and an `Arc<dyn PixelSink>`, nothing of `Ctx` (D7). A present and a drop wake the loop through the `Waker`, once until the loop's next turn, as a job's end does.
988. **For veto:** `resize` clears the buffer to transparent and tells the sink, and presents nothing of its own: the producer presents when it has drawn. A buffer shows nothing until its first present after `create` or `resize` (the sink's contract), so a placement never shows what the GPU's memory held.
989. The harness connects a `FakePixelSink` to every `Harness`: `Harness::pixels(id) -> Option<(w, h, Vec<u8>)>` as last presented, `fake_pixels().take_calls()` the `SinkCall`s. `pixels_supported()` is true in the harness and false on a bare `Ctx`. The fake takes one `Mutex` on both sides, which a host's sink mustn't; its docs say so.
990. die-render: no change. `Scene` is built through `compose` everywhere, so the new field reached no constructor. The host's one call is documented for the render builder: after `ctx.set_waker(...)` in `die_render::run` (window.rs), `ctx.set_pixel_sink(sink).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?`, with its ring as the sink.
991. Budget (`cargo xtask budget`): die-core 15,430 → 15,747 (limit 15,755); faces and scene 970 → 1,001 (1,005); surfaces and PTYs 1,420 → 1,442 (1,445); harness 732 → 806 (810); pixels new at 188 (195); ext/dispatch/rt 1,641 → 1,643, inside its 1,650, since `Ctx`'s pixel methods live in `pixels.rs`. `cargo xtask api-diff` lists 32 new items and `Scene`'s new field.
992. Tests, `crates/die-core/tests/pixels.rs` (12): supported with a sink and not without, a second sink refused; a present updates the fake's pixels, whole and by rectangle, with the calls; damage clipped, and an empty one no present; a resize reallocates, tells the sink and clears; bad sizes; a present from another thread wakes the loop once a turn; a surface's placement at the area's offset with its z, and gone when not placed; drawing order; a dropped producer's destroy and its placement gone; stopped above the prompt's list; the snapshot's section; `PixelRect`'s union and clip.
993. Open: nothing draws yet; die-render's quad, ring, pending flag and drag fix are the render builder's package. `Frame::place` places over the whole area only; a narrower placement, images, the source rectangle, `Hide` with a placement and the display link wait for M3. Contract §11 is rewritten to this and to what the render builder adds, so it reads a little ahead of the tree until that lands.

**die-whiteboard**

994. The package: the whiteboard you accepted on 2026-11-10 ("a bit of freehand, a bit of placing shapes/arrows with appropriate keybinds and ability to convert/save/export to png, svg"), as `extensions/die-whiteboard` with die-orchid-client's wiring: die-core alone (die-vim and die-test-cap for tests), `[lints] workspace = true`, a budget row with `extension = true` (3,864 lines, uncapped), the lint's NEUTRAL list, the workspace's members and default-members, and registered in die-dev with `<leader>w` → `whiteboard` in normal state. The brief's Part A without `pixels.rs`, which waits for the core slice (Part B, M3a) being built beside it. Three crates.io dependencies, pinned in `[workspace.dependencies]`, pure Rust with no build scripts: tiny-skia `=0.12.0` (`std`, `simd`; every call behind `canvas.rs`), png `=0.18.1`, fontdue `=0.9.5` (`std`; behind `fonts.rs`, the PNG exporter's labels only). Cargo.lock goes from 232 packages to 251.
995. The renderer trait and the view, so the pixel renderer slots in without the model, tools or keymap changing: `render::Renderer { name, resize(cols, rows, cell), board_changed(Damage), render(frame, &Scene, info), zooms }`, where `Scene` carries the board, the view, the six inks as cell colours, the selection, the gesture's live element and the marquee; `Damage` is `All` or a `Region` in board units. `view::View { pan: (f32, f32) in physical pixels, zoom }`: board units are logical pixels (the SVG's units), physical = board × `cell.scale` × zoom + pan, and the pointer goes through the inverse; nothing else reads cell coordinates. `cells.rs` runs today; `pixels.rs` is a documented stub (`AVAILABLE = false`, `new() -> None`) and `BoardSurface::new` picks it in one line. The surface keeps a `rev` for anything on screen and a `board_rev` for the committed picture, so a pointer sample redraws the live element over the cached grid rather than the board.
996. **For veto:** `h` and `l` do double duty, since the brief gives each two meanings (`h` the highlighter and `l` the line tool, and `hjkl` nudging the selection): with a selection they nudge, without one `h` toggles the highlighter and `l` picks the line tool; `j` and `k` with nothing selected say `Nothing is selected`. `M-x whiteboard-tool-line` and `whiteboard-highlighter` are there by name.
997. The pen as the brief has it, streamline 0.5 on each sample and Douglas-Peucker at 0.5 px at release, with one addition: the release point is appended before simplifying, so a stroke ends where the pointer lifted. With few samples the low-pass leaves the last smoothed point well short: a two-sample stroke of 100 px ended at 50.
